Consumer: all three production review paths from day one; measurement: the §26.5 contestation KPI (000086) joined contestation-×-injection on the verdict mode stamp | §31.6, §31.2, §31.7 |
| 108 | mode B: index & retrieval — **routed out** | **No longer built in this repository.** Step 107's baseline readout is the input to a three-way decision, recorded and never defaulted: kill, defer, or build. Building it *here* is not one of the three — that was settled when the extension boundary was drawn (§34), and the same work is specified as Steps E5-E7 of the separate repository's own plan. What remains this repository's Step is the DECISION and its record: under a kill, this row's entire deliverable is that record, citing the readout, and Phase 9 closes on it (§31.9). The row keeps its number so every citation of it elsewhere stays valid **DÉCIDÉ (2026-09-17) : construire**, dans le dépôt séparé, où le travail est déjà spécifié en Steps E5 à E7. Le readout du Step 107 reste l'entrée de référence et sa fenêtre continue de s'accumuler ; la décision est prise sans l'attendre, ce qui est un choix assumé et non un oubli. Écartées : **tuer** — la mieux outillée des trois, dont le livrable entier aurait été ce même enregistrement, et sous laquelle 109, 110 et 156 mouraient ensemble ; **différer** — la seule qui aurait tranché sur des chiffres, écartée parce que le plan ne chiffre nulle part combien de reviews la fenêtre demande, ce qui en faisait une attente sans condition de sortie. CE QUE LA DÉCISION RALLUME, et c'est le coût réel : le **Step 156** cesse d'être conditionnel — un fournisseur d'embeddings hébergé recevant de la prose dérivée du client est un canal d'egress neuf, et l'adaptateur compatible fil qui permet à un auto-hébergeur de servir le même corpus devient du travail requis. Les **Steps 109 et 110** repartent aussi : tous deux ont besoin des tables de corpus du 108 | §31.10 |
| 109 | kb_search — **routed out** | **No longer built in this repository.** The pull surface for builder sessions belongs with the corpus it reads, which is not here. Specified as part of Step E7 of the separate repository's plan. Whatever builds it inherits, unweakened, this repository's own rules: scope derived server-side AND verified against Step 106's entitlement predicate — derivation alone is laundering (§31.4) — and authorization against the turn's stamped mode, never the live setting (§31.2) | §31.10 |
| 110 | OKF read-only export — **routed out** | **No longer built in this repository.** An export surface for a corpus that does not live here. Specified as part of Step E7 of the separate repository's plan. It remains, wherever it is built, an export surface and never an authority, never the retrieval substrate, and never written into any customer repository (§30's collision) | §31.10 |

**Phase 9 milestone**: mode A's prior-decisions block live on all three review seams with the
contestation-×-injection KPI reporting per verdict mode stamp, and Step 107's baseline readout
read and its three-way decision recorded (§31.9) — kill, defer, or build in the separate
repository. Those are this repository's whole obligation: a retrieval substrate is not built
here under any of the three answers, so no clause of this milestone waits on one. Contested and
shadow-epoch content is demonstrably excluded from live reads in the gate's own SQL, which is
this repository's forever — the flip a later substrate performs changes ordering, never
eligibility (§31.6). Step 107 alone is already production-useful: mode A gains cross-PR memory
whatever the decision turns out to be.

## Phase 10 — Metrics export (2 Steps, additive)

The path from an emitted metric to a backend that can evaluate an alert on it. `platform.SetupOTel`
wires `stdouttrace`/`stdoutmetric` and nothing else — there is no OTLP module in `go.mod` at all —
so every instrument this platform registers is written to its own process's stdout and aggregated
by nobody. That is the whole of why §10-P6's exit criterion ("platform serving production traffic
under monitoring") is unmet: the code half is in place, the monitoring half is not, and
`docs/PRODUCTION_CHECKLIST.md` item 5 already records it as a launch blocker rather than a silent
gap. Technical plan §33.

On placement: by the letter of the Phase 5/6 rule above this is rollout-enabling platform glue and
belongs in Phase 6. It is not folded in, for the reason Phases 8 and 9 give for themselves —
Phase 6's own Steps were long since scoped, shipped and audited, folding Steps into a closed phase
would rewrite history, and inserting them mid-sequence would shift Steps 79-110 (the Phase 4
standing rule) and every cross-reference that names them. It therefore lands appended as its own
phase, with execution order stated here instead.

Execution order: schedulable immediately; it waits on nothing and nothing waits on it. **The two
Steps do not gate the same thing, and the distinction is the scheduling decision.** Step 111 alone
closes P6's exit criterion — it makes real, at once, every instrument registered control-plane-side
(outbox lag, orphan reaps, rollout refusals, image builds, cloud-identity mints, and Step 77's five
spawn/liveness/watchdog/false-failure instruments). Step 112 reaches only the four instruments
emitted inside the sandbox, and gates no phase: what it buys is that **SLO 1 and
`BootDurationP95High` stop resting on a metric nobody can read**. Step 111 is small and urgent;
Step 112 is neither, and may be scheduled independently. Step 112 is ordered after Step 111 so that
its own exit criterion is checkable end to end.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 111 | control-plane OTLP export | Config-gated OTLP exporter in `platform.SetupOTel` behind a new validated `platform.Config` endpoint field (the flag-by-config precedent the object-store endpoint already sets); **unset → stdout exactly as today**, so dev, CI and every existing deployment are byte-identical and this Step is a no-op until an operator opts in. Makes every control-plane-side instrument real in one change. sandbox-agent shares `SetupOTel` but is never given the endpoint — and could not reach a collector anyway, since §27.6's server-appended allowlist floor admits only the control-plane host plus the session's git hosts (`allowlistFloorHosts`), which is a property to preserve, not to widen. Exit: an integration test against a fake OTLP receiver observes the control plane's metrics, and the unset-endpoint path is proven unchanged | §5.3, §33 |
| 112 | sandbox boot-timing relay | The four sandbox-emitted histograms (`sandbox_agent_boot_duration_seconds`, `..._hook_rerun_...`, `..._git_fetch_...`, `..._git_checkout_...`) stop being recorded inside the sandbox and are recorded control-plane-side instead, from a new best-effort `boot_timing` sandbox-ws event carrying the **already-measured** seconds plus its low-cardinality tags — the fact crosses the wire, never raw observations or pre-aggregated buckets, so the value keeps being measured by the same `time.Since` bracket on the sandbox's own clock and §33.3's "identical semantics, same names, same buckets" property holds. Recording is gated on `appendRawEvent`'s own `inserted` flag, the Step 77 `turn_false_failure_total` precedent, because §6.1's reconnect resend would otherwise double-count. The repo name is dropped from metric attributes as unbounded cardinality (it still rides the event into the `events` log for per-session debugging). Deletes the two sandbox-side telemetry files; keeps sandbox-agent's `SetupOTel` bootstrap. Exit: a forced WS reconnect replay leaves each histogram holding its data point **exactly once** | §27, §33, §6.1 |

**Phase 10 milestone**: an alert defined on a control-plane instrument fires from a real
backend rather than from a process's own stdout — which is what closes §10-P6's exit criterion,
unmet until then and recorded as a launch blocker in `docs/PRODUCTION_CHECKLIST.md` item 5.
Step 112 is deliberately not part of this gate: it reaches only the four sandbox-emitted
histograms and gates no phase, and its own criterion is that SLO 1 and `BootDurationP95High`
stop resting on a metric nobody can read.

## Phase 11 — Named gaps (scheduled in full)

A holding place for work that is real, is not speculative, and is **not blocking anything currently
scheduled**. The rule that keeps it honest: a gap goes here only if the Step that found it shipped
correctly without it, having said on screen or in its own docs what is missing. A gap that blocks a
scheduled Step is not filed here — it becomes a Step of its own, immediately before its consumer,
the way Steps 88 and 90 did — which is also why those two sit in Phase 7 despite being backend
work, and why that phase's own title says so.

It was a holding list with no exit criterion, drained one item at a time when something started
needing one. **That changed by decision, not by drift**: the owner chose to work it through in
full, so this phase now has an execution order and a milestone like any other. The filing rule
above still governs what may ENTER it; what changed is that entering no longer means waiting
indefinitely.

Execution order, by nature rather than by number — several of these are not "write the code" at
all, and pretending otherwise is how a list like this quietly stalls:

- **A, mechanical** (116, 115, 114, 118, 131, 117): clean corrections with no design risk. First,
  because they are cheap and because 116 and 114 are debts a desktop client would otherwise pay
  at the worst moment.
- **B, wire and UI completions** (121, 123, 127, 122, 113): screens that today say "not available"
  honestly, and the fields behind them.
- **C, design first** (119, 120, 124, 125, 126): each needs a decision before any code. Two are
  taken and recorded below.
- **D, investigations** (129, 130): no known fix. 129 in particular may legitimately end in
  "still no hypothesis" — its own row forbids stabilizing a timing test on a single data point,
  and that prohibition survives this scheduling decision.
- **E, the sweep** (128): 183 citations, each verified against the title of the row it names.
  Never by arithmetic — that was tried once on the technical plan and got five wrong.
- **F, rows appended on 2026-09-28** (198-205): independent of one another, with two
  exceptions: 203 introduced the failure class Phase 20's Step 191 reuses, and leaves the kinds a
  second delivery would add to out of its rule, the formal review's among them until Step 190; 204 starts once §30.4 serves
  review sessions read-only in every mode. Separately, 198 adds a session timer kind
  (`owed_review_request`); Step 222 has shipped, so group H's ordering note asks nothing of it.
- **G, row appended on 2026-09-29** (216): a defect in shipped code, filed together with the fix
  that closes it; it depends on no other row.
- **H, rows appended on 2026-09-29** (218-226): each confirmed in the code on `main` after the
  adversarial reviews of the recent MCP, sandbox-boot, actor-lock and stop work, or of this group's
  own filing (226), named it. Independent of one another. One ordering note reached outside the
  group, and **222 has shipped, which settles it**: a Step that adds a session timer kind had either
  to land after 222 or to ship a migration whose down deletes that kind's rows, as 000151's does for
  `stop`, so that a rollback past it would not strand the kind. The dispatcher now bounds any kind
  a binary does not know by the time since its row was last armed (technical plan §2): kept at the
  claim cadence for an hour, longer than a rolling deploy is assumed to run, backed off past that,
  deleted a day after its last arm. A Step that adds a kind needs no such down migration any more,
  unless the kind is armed about a day or more before it is due (§2 says why, and how far ahead a
  kind may be re-armed); one may still ship it, if
  its sessions should read settled as soon as the rollback runs rather than once the rows are
  deleted. Three unshipped rows add a kind:
  223 here, 198 (group F) and Phase 15's Step 150, and each row says so. Nothing waited on 222, and
  this phase still gates nothing. 225 is an investigation in the manner of 129: no test in it is
  changed on a single data point.
- **I, row appended on 2026-10-01** (227): three intermittent tests Step 225's own runs saw while
  it fixed six others, filed from its review rather than changed there on one observation each,
  and a fourth that failed on `main` after it merged.
  An investigation like 225, under the same rule; it depends on no other row.
- **J, row appended on 2026-10-02** (228, shipped): the frame limit Step 226 left one-sided. The
  control plane read the sandbox socket at the WebSocket library's 32 KiB default, which 226's
  review found and its PR named a follow-up, and an agent built before 226 lost a larger prompt
  with nothing naming why. It built on 226's per-gen capability record and
  `MaxPromptFrameBytes`, both shipped, and depended on no other row. **228 has shipped**, in three
  PRs: the control plane states and reads 1 MiB, every agent write fits what its connection's
  control plane reads, and an older agent's larger prompt is refused at dispatch and named. Group
  K records the one assertion it shares with 229, which ships second and so updates it.
- **K, row appended on 2026-10-03** (229): a defect in shipped code, older than the rows around it.
  Step 93 found it for `step_finish` and shipped around it, and the review of 228's own filing named
  the tool events. One ordering note: 228's scenario-23 `tool_result` cases assert that a
  `tool_result` adds no row, which holds only until 229 ships, so whichever of the two ships second
  updates the other's assertion. It depends on no other row.

**Two decisions taken, recorded here rather than left to be rediscovered at the Step:**

*Step 120, where the analytics line falls.* A deployment's own numbers stay in this repository —
sessions by outcome, success rate, false failures, cost by model, boot p95, top failure reasons.
A deployment that cannot state its own success rate is incomplete, and no base capability moves
out. What belongs to a composed module is the cross-cutting view: comparing repositories,
aggregating by team, fleet trends. The operative test, so the question is not re-litigated at
every new chart: **can the value be computed from this deployment's own rows?** If yes it is
this repository's; if it only means something once aggregated ACROSS deployments or
organizations, it is not. The same rule is stated in technical plan §34.

*Step 119, how ingress surfaces become optional.* Not by inferring intent from absent secrets —
that would make a typo in a variable name indistinguishable from a deliberate disabling, which is
the fail direction this repository refuses everywhere else. An explicit declaration instead
(`NARVI_INGRESS_ENABLED`): a surface not declared is not mounted, and a surface declared without
its secrets is a loud boot failure. In every outcome, `configured` on the §12.5 read model must
become honest or be removed — a field that cannot be false informs nobody.

**Phase 11 milestone**: every row — including the thirty-eight appended after the phase was scheduled
(152-156, filed once a sweep of this session's own audits found real absences nothing recorded;
160-161, filed when the adversarial reviews of Steps 130 and 126 each turned up a real gap
belonging to neither Step; 171, 172-179, 189 and 198-205, each filed by the Step, review or analysis that
found it; 216, filed together with its fix; 218-226, filed from the reviews of the Steps that
shipped without them and of this group's own filing; 227, filed from the review of Step 225,
whose runs saw its failures; 228, filed from the review of Step 226, whose PR named it; and 229, filed
from the review of 228's own filing, Step 93 having recorded its `step_finish` half) —
either shipped, or closed by a recorded decision that says why
it will not be — the second being a legitimate outcome for 129 and 119's residue, never a silent
omission. `configured` no longer reports a value it cannot fail to report. And the sweep in 128 is
followed by widening the checker to the roots it audited, so the citations it corrected cannot
rot again.

The home view renders all three states and the third was verified by reading the branch rather than by exercising it, because reproducing it needs a live GitHub returning one good and one failing discovery query. Wanted: a way to exercise it end to end, so the branch is covered by something other than inspection | §16.2 |

One character | — |

Found while inserting Steps 88/90; deliberately NOT fixed by arithmetic, because at least one reference could not be mapped to a row by content with confidence, and a half-corrected sequencing note is worse than a uniformly stale one. Wants a careful pass mapping each number to its row by title | — |
| 118 | flaky port-based readiness tests | `internal/sandboxagent/services`' `freePort` binds `127.0.0.1:0`, reads the port and CLOSES the listener, after which `TestRun_PrimaryTimeoutIsFatal`/`..._SecondaryTimeoutIsWarning` assume nothing binds it for 300ms. On a loaded runner the kernel can hand that ephemeral port to something else, the readiness probe then succeeds against a stranger, and `Run` returns nil — observed once on CI (2026-08-21) on a docs-only PR, 8/8 green locally. Likely fix: probe a fixed port below the ephemeral range instead of a just-released one. Filed rather than fixed on sight: one occurrence, and changing a timing test on a single data point is how flakes get papered over instead of understood | — |

The sweep must verify each citation against the title of the row it names, never shift it arithmetically: that was tried on the technical plan during Phase 7 and got five wrong, three of them already broken by an earlier renumber in a way no shift repairs. Widening the checker to those roots is the second half and cannot land before the sweep, or CI goes red on 183 pre-existing citations | §11 |
| 129 | opencode adapter: `TestRealTurn_Aborted` under the full suite | Fails intermittently under the full parallel `-race` run and passes in isolation every time; the package was untouched by the Phase 8 work that surfaced it (observed three times, 2026-08). Filed rather than patched: there is no hypothesis yet that explains the failure, and stabilizing a timing test without one is how flakes get papered over instead of understood (Step 118's own rule) | — |
| 130 | githarden: repo-selected command classes, closed by a recorded decision | **Closed by decision, not by code.** The row's own premise was wrong: it assumed a checked-in `.gitattributes` alone cannot arm a filter because the command half lives in config the sandbox controls — but §30.5 grants the in-sandbox runtime ownership of `.git`, so writing that config half is an ordinary unprivileged act for it, and sandbox-agent's own later invocations run the planted command as itself. Both resolutions this row originally offered were tried and refuted by two adversarial rounds measured against real git. An attributes override (`* -filter` in `$GIT_DIR/info/attributes`) was implemented and **withdrawn as net-negative**: it lives in the same runtime-owned directory the attack starts from (0 of 60 racing trials blocked), it reaches only one of the classes, and writing it required a root-privileged `os.WriteFile` into an attacker-owned directory — itself redirectable, via a symlink at the parent, into truncating an arbitrary host file. Three classes were reachable; two remain closable by nothing here: `filter.<name>.clean`/`smudge`, and `merge.<name>.driver` (fires during `syncOne`'s own `stash pop --index`, and no `filter` override touches it) — each still demonstrated live by `githarden_test.go`'s `TestOpenClass_ContentFilterExecutes`/`TestOpenClass_MergeDriverExecutes`, which run real git and observe the planted command execute, so the gap goes red when it finally closes rather than rotting into a comment. The structural remedy for those two is still Step 171. **CLOSED (Step 171 shipped):** the split-git-dir shape landed — sandbox-agent's own git now always runs `-C <worktree> --git-dir=<agent-owned> --work-tree=<worktree>`, an agent-owned, root-only-writable directory the runtime never gets a write to, so there is no config left for either driver's own command half to be planted into. Per this row's own closing signal, `TestOpenClass_ContentFilterExecutes`/`TestOpenClass_MergeDriverExecutes` went RED and were deleted, not repaired; `githarden_test.go`/`githarden_split_test.go` now carry `TestClosedClass_ContentFilterDoesNotExecute`, `TestClosedClass_MergeDriverDoesNotExecute`, `TestClosedClass_StashPopIndexDoesNotExecuteEither` (the exact `stash pop --index` call shape named above) and `TestClosedClass_RuntimeHookDoesNotExecute`, each planting the identical attack on the runtime's own worktree `.git/config` and asserting the driver does NOT run. Hand-verified by mutation: pointing `Args` back at the pre-171 `-C`-only shape makes `TestClosedClass_ContentFilterDoesNotExecute` fail with the marker reappearing. The third, `remote.<name>.uploadpack`/`receivepack` (**no `.gitattributes` half at all**, deterministic on a plain fetch), is now **CLOSED** — not by a key of its own, but as a consequence of the transport-class hardening (`protocol.file.allow=never`/`protocol.ssh.allow=never` deny the only two transports that key is ever consulted for; sandbox-agent speaks only https). See `TestTransportClass_FileUploadPackNoLongerExecutes` (`githarden_test.go`), which now asserts the planted command does NOT run. That same transport class also closes `ftp`/`ftps` and, via `GIT_ALLOW_PROTOCOL` set in the child process's own environment rather than any `-c` flag, an arbitrary attacker-named `<name>::` remote helper — a namespace no `-c` enumeration could ever cover in advance (`TestTransportClass_ArbitraryRemoteHelperBlockedByAllowProtocol`, `TestTransportClass_FTPBlockedByAllowProtocol`) | §30.5 |
| 131 | real-spawn hook tests time out under full-suite load | `TestCloudIdentityTokenReachesRealSpawnedHook` and `TestOIDCClusterBindingTokenReachesRealSpawnedHook` (`cmd/sandbox-agent`) run a real `setup.sh` under a 5-second ceiling; under a full local `make test` with concurrent compile load both timed out (2026-09-03) and passed isolated immediately after. Same family as Steps 118/173, same honesty rule: filed with the observation, and any raised ceiling should follow from a worst-case spawn-latency argument, not from one loaded run | — |
| 152 | no un-entitlement, ever | A repository becomes eligible for session creation by having a `github_pr_sessions` row and never stops being so: nothing an operator can do — removing the GitHub App installation, revoking it, deleting the repository — takes that eligibility away. Found while auditing Step 106, where it was correctly ruled out as a defect of that Step: the predicate it added is sound, and this is the absence behind it. An authorization gate that can only ever open is worth a row of its own. The shape needs deciding before code: whether eligibility is recomputed from a live installation read, expired by age, or revoked by an explicit operator action — the third is the only one that stays true with no network **DECIDED (2026-09-15): explicit operator revocation.** Of the three shapes this row named, it is the only one that stays true with no network -- a live installation re-read fails open or blocks session creation whenever GitHub is unreachable, and an age expiry leaves a revoked repository eligible until its deadline. The cost is accepted deliberately: a new operator action and its own RBAC row, which is a visible surface rather than a silent one, and that is the point. An authorization gate that can only ever open is the defect; a gate an operator can close is the fix. **SHIPPED.** An administrator revokes a repository with a required reason (1 to 500 characters) and restores it: a `repo_entitlement_revocations` row keyed by the exact `repo_full_name` (its own migration), deleted on restore, history in `audit_log` (`repo_entitlement.revoked`, `repo_entitlement.restored`, each in its change's transaction). Eligibility and revocation are read in one statement (`ReadRepoEntitlement`, `GitHubPRSessionStore.RepoEntitlement`; `RepoKnown` is gone), and `authz.AuthorizeRepo` refuses `Revoked` before `Known` (`RepoRevokedError` unwraps to `ErrRepoForbidden` and `ErrRepoRevoked`). Every creation path refuses with its own words: REST and MCP 403 `repository entitlement revoked by an administrator: <repo>`, Slack and Linear an acknowledgement naming who reopens it, automations a failed run (a strike: three refused invocations auto-pause an automation, and a restore does not resume it). GitHub-originated sessions are bound too, keyed by the pull request's base repository (`ResolveGitHubRepoEntitlement`, which also reads the clone URL's): a new mention, a follow-up, a label re-trigger and a fork's mention are refused silently with the delivery claim kept, and `coalesce.go` and the sentinel auto-fix now map the refusal to their permanent-denial paths (the auto-fix before its branch is created). Running turns finish, unless their sandbox restarts or dies while the repository is revoked, which ends them with the revocation reason; pending turns are refused at spawn (`refuseIfRepoRevoked`) and at dispatch (`revocationRefusalForDispatch`) in every rollout mode, through the session's clone URLs and its pull-request claims, with a banner and a `repo_entitlement_revoked` review check, marked `"delivered": false` since nothing was sent to that gen; a revocation read that fails at dispatch is an undelivered prompt that backs off, never a refusal. A §3.3 receipt re-send to the same gen reads the revocation before the transaction claims the reconnect (`resendRevokedRepo`): a revocation leaves the reconnect unclaimed, sends and counts nothing and never fails the turn, so the first heartbeat after a restore re-sends the prompt (one revocation read per heartbeat, for at most `PromptResendWindow`), and a read that fails rolls the evaluation back for the next heartbeat; automatic re-review reads it before any GitHub read, turn insert or budget spend, so a revoked repository's pushes spend nothing, post no budget notice and do not read as scheduled in the session's status. Refusals count on `session_repo_entitlement_denied_total{reason, stage}`, whose stage (`create`, `spawn`, `dispatch`, `resend`, `auto_retrigger`) tells creation denials from the rest. The operator action is three admin-only routes under `/api/repos/{owner}/{repo}/entitlement` behind the new `manage_repo_entitlement` (§13.3's new row), 404 for an unknown repository, 409 for a second revoke or an idle restore, with a card on the Repository settings screen; the repository-scoped admin routes stay reachable for a revoked repository. `internal/ops`' `ScanRepoEntitlementReads` fails the build on four name-based shapes of a reader skipping revocation (rules a-d, with allowlists of readers and of non-eligibility queries, each with its reason). A revocation names the repository by its name: a rename or transfer on the code host needs the new name revoked too (§31.9 limit 4). Rolling back re-opens every revoked repository: the previous binary never reads the table. | §31.4 |
| 153 | the bot token is an egress credential in the ingress bundle | `NARVI_GITHUB_BOT_TOKEN` posts comments and commit statuses — pure egress — yet it lives in the same required-set as the webhook secret and bot handle, which are ingress. Step 119 made ingress optional and this bundling immediately produced a notifier registered with an empty token; the fix gated the notifier, and recorded that the bundling itself is the cause. Any future egress needing the bot token inherits the same silent tie to ingress being enabled. Untangling it means deciding which credentials belong to which axis, the same boundary question Step 119 already answered one way for GitHub OAuth login and the SCM adapter **DECIDED (2026-09-15): split the axes.** `NARVI_GITHUB_BOT_TOKEN` moves to its own egress required-set, independent of whether any ingress surface is enabled. Gating each consumer instead -- what Step 119's own fix did case by case -- leaves the trap armed for whichever future egress consumer forgets to gate, and that is precisely how this one was found. Making the token unconditionally required was rejected: it would force a GitHub credential on a deployment that wants no ingress, contradicting what Step 119 established. Cost accepted: one more environment variable to document, and a configuration migration for existing deployments. **SHIPPED.** The switch is `NARVI_OUTBOUND_ENABLED` -- "outbound", not "egress", which already names §30's per-repository live/shadow mode, §27.6's sandbox egress and §42.4's NetworkPolicy; this switch is not §30's. It reads like `NARVI_INGRESS_ENABLED` over the same `Provider` vocabulary and accepts only `github` (`integrations.OutboundProviders`); `slack`, `linear` and typos are refused (`InvalidOutboundEnabledError`). Rules, all in `platform.Load`: unset resolves to `github` while GitHub ingress is on, so default deployments boot unchanged; unset while a valid `NARVI_INGRESS_ENABLED` excludes github is refused alone (`UndeclaredOutboundError`; an ingress list with an invalid entry is refused for that entry alone); `""` declares none; GitHub ingress requires GitHub outbound (`GitHubIngressRequiresOutboundError`), never the reverse; the bot token is required exactly when GitHub outbound is on (`MissingRequiredEnvError` gains an optional `RequiredBy`) and ignored, never stored, when it is off; RWX previews require GitHub outbound (`RWXPreviewsRequireGitHubOutboundError`, renamed from the ingress-keyed error); the bot handle is stored only while GitHub ingress is on. `Config.GitHubBotToken` is deleted: the token lives only in `*platform.GitHubOutboundConfig` (`Config.GitHubOutbound`, nil when off). Every required consumer -- the five `githubapi` notifiers, the review check, sentinel auto-fix and description autofix notifiers, the auto-merge and release-manifest workers, the GitHub webhook handler -- refuses nil in its constructor; every optional reader -- review readout, session result and its MCP twin, verdict tool, re-review button, the actor's automatic re-review and sentinel fix PR -- takes its existing degraded path on nil without making the call. `controlplane/githuboutbound.go` is the one place the notifiers and workers are wired, publication and bot-only writes separately, replacing the per-notifier ingress gates Step 119 added, and a Build test proves the composition root hands every optional reader the same config. `internal/ops`'s `TestGitHubOutboundGuard` scans Go source outside `internal/platform` for exactly three things: (a) the token's variable in a non-test string literal, (b) `GitHubOutboundConfig` named other than behind a pointer, or its constructors in non-test code, (c) `.BotToken` in non-test `controlplane/*.go`; consumers elsewhere unwrap the token inside themselves by design, and `TestLoadStoresTheBotTokenOnlyInGitHubOutbound` proves no other `Config` field holds it. `reviewpost.RerunGuidance("")` now points at the web review's Re-run review button alone. Configuration migration (§12.5 has the full list): only a deployment whose `NARVI_INGRESS_ENABLED` excludes github is affected, and it must declare one of two values, neither of which is exactly its old behaviour -- it used to post nothing to GitHub, but ran the auto-merge and release-manifest workers and, with a token set, read pull requests with it. `NARVI_OUTBOUND_ENABLED=` still posts nothing, but stops both workers (leftover release-manifest rows are no longer processed) and ends a set token's live reads: on review sessions created while GitHub ingress was on, the readout's live facts read unavailable, result/MCP freshness reads unconfirmed, verdict findings stay unanchored, and re-reviews run without pre-fetched context. Since Step 202, the decision inbox also reads no base branch's required checks without the bot: it shows no pull request as ready to merge and says so (`requiredChecksNotRead`), without reporting a failure, while still offering Merge on a row that meets everything else (`mergeableIfRequiredChecksPass`); that click reads them and merges with the person's own token, or names the unmet check. `NARVI_OUTBOUND_ENABLED=github` with a real token keeps all of that and also posts, as the bot, the verdicts, labels, check runs, comments, statuses and fix branches this deployment never posted before. | §12.5 |
| 154 | automerge polls on indefinitely through an auth failure | The automerge worker has no dead-letter and no backoff for an authentication failure: with a bad or empty bot token it keeps polling GitHub, forever, at full rate. Surfaced by Step 119's audit and correctly refuted as a defect of that Step — it is `main`'s behaviour verbatim, predating it. Every other outbound path in this repository either dead-letters or backs off; this one does neither, and an operator sees only rate-limit noise | §17 |
| 155 | a repository rename splits its own corpus | `repo_full_name` is the scope key for per-repository knowledge, and that corpus outlives any PR session — so a rename or transfer silently leaves the old prose unreachable under a name nothing will ever query again, with no error and no migration. Named as an accepted residual limit in the spec rather than hidden, and filed here so it is not rediscovered as a bug. Consistency with `repo_settings` and `github_pr_sessions` argues for inheriting whatever rename fix those tables get, which is the cheapest correct answer and does not exist yet | §31.9 |
| 156 | the embeddings provider is an un-closed egress channel | If mode B is ever built, a hosted embeddings provider receives customer-derived prose — a new egress channel, surfaced in the spec and left open. The named path to closing it for a self-hoster is a wire-compatible adapter, so the same corpus can be served by something the operator runs. Deferred, not dropped. Filed with a condition attached rather than as unconditional work — et **cette condition est résolue : la décision du 2026-09-17 sur le Step 108 est CONSTRUIRE**, donc ce lot n'est plus sans objet et n'est plus différé. Un fournisseur d'embeddings hébergé reçoit de la prose dérivée du client dès que mode B tourne ; le canal doit être fermé, pas seulement signalé. Le chemin nommé reste le même : un adaptateur compatible fil, pour que le même corpus puisse être servi par ce que l'opérateur fait tourner lui-même | §31.5, §31.9 |
| 160 | git transport helpers are the same unclosed class as content filters | `githarden`'s `hardeningFlags` sets no `protocol.allow`, no `protocol.ext.allow` and no `core.gitProxy` restriction. `remote.origin.url` lives in the same runtime-owned `.git/config` as a filter driver (§30.5 grants the agent runtime that ownership), so a rewrite to `ext::<command>`, or a planted `core.gitProxy`, runs as sandbox-agent on the next fetch or default-branch resolution. Surfaced while auditing Step 130 and deliberately left unfixed there — it is a different class, and Step 130's own conclusion (a file inside a directory the adversary owns cannot be a trustworthy guard) may or may not apply here, since these keys DO have `-c` overrides that a filter driver's unbounded namespace does not. That difference is what makes this worth its own row rather than a footnote on a closed one | §30.5 |
| 161 | `plan_documents` is written by one place and read by none | The approval snapshot is created inside the approving transaction and consulted by nothing in production — not `content`, and since Step 126 not `structured_steps` either. The read path instead recomputes from the event log over a bounded window (2000 events), which is strictly weaker: past that window `ExtractContent` returns its honest `(plan content unavailable)` placeholder while the snapshot two tables over holds the text. Found auditing Step 126, which extended the table rather than created the shape. Needs a decision before code — whether the snapshot becomes the read path's first choice, or the table is dropped as the dead weight it currently is — because either answer changes what the API returns for every approved plan, and §5.1's single-source-of-truth reasoning is what has to settle it **DECIDED (2026-09-15): the snapshot becomes the read path's first choice**, with the bounded event-log recompute as the fallback. An approved plan then stops being able to render `(plan content unavailable)` while the text sits two tables away. Dropping the table was rejected because it would leave that 2000-event window as the only source, and lose the immutable record of what was actually approved. The decision has one consequence the implementation must answer first: it changes what the API returns for every approved plan, so what a plan approved BEFORE the table existed renders as has to be settled deliberately, not discovered. | §12.2, §5.1 |
| 171 | sandbox-agent must not run git against a runtime-owned `.git` | The root cause Step 130 recorded and could not fix. §30.5 gives the in-sandbox runtime ownership of the workspace, `.git` included, so it selects any command git will later execute on sandbox-agent's behalf — content filters and merge drivers are the two remaining demonstrated instances (Step 130's `TestOpenClass_ContentFilterExecutes`/`TestOpenClass_MergeDriverExecutes`), and the list is open because the mechanism, not the key, is the defect. (A third instance, the uploadpack/receivepack transport pair, was demonstrated the same way here originally; Step 130's own row now records it CLOSED by `-c`-flag transport hardening, not by this Step's structural remedy — it is no longer part of what this Step still has to close.) No flag, attributes override, or file written into `.git` by a process that does not own `.git` can close it; Step 130 measured one such attempt at 0 of 60 racing trials blocked. **The separate-git-dir shape is measured, not assumed** — `git --git-dir=<agent-owned> --work-tree=<runtime-owned>` was verified against real git to read config from the agent-owned dir only: an armed `filter.evil.smudge` in the runtime's `.git/config` did not run, and the working tree was still populated correctly. Two constraints came out of that measurement and are the design's real content. **Git refuses a symlinked `HEAD`** (its own safeguard), so HEAD must be synced rather than shared, which is what decides how branch switches propagate. And everything that carries no command — `objects` (via `objects/info/alternates`), `refs`, `index`, `logs` — CAN be shared, which is what keeps the agent-owned dir at roughly a tenth the size rather than a full copy; only `config` and `hooks` must be agent-owned, because only they name commands. What remains to decide is which of the three shapes to build: this separate git-dir, a distinct uid for git, or narrowing what §30.5 grants. Each trades differently against the reason §30.5 exists, which is why this is filed rather than assumed. **Closing signal**: when this ships, Step 130's `TestOpenClass_*` must go RED — that is the signal to delete them, not to repair them **DECIDED (2026-09-15): the separate git-dir**, the one shape measured rather than guessed. A distinct uid closes the class by permissions but takes away the runtime's ability to commit at all, which would need a mediated path -- new surface, designed from nothing. Narrowing what §30.5 grants reopens the question §30.5 settled and touches everything downstream of that boundary. The measured shape leaves the runtime committing normally and needs no new interface. First implementation question, already known from the measurement: HEAD cannot be shared, so how a runtime branch switch propagates is what the design has to answer before anything else. **SHIPPED.** `internal/sandboxagent/gitdir` builds and seeds the agent-owned git-dir (`Layout`, `Seed`, `Run`, `SyncHeadIn`/`SyncHeadOut`, `MirrorSparseCheckout`); `githarden.Repo{WorkTree, GitDir}`/`Args` emit the measured `-C <worktree> --git-dir=<agent-owned> --work-tree=<worktree>` shape and drop `safe.directory` entirely (moot once `--git-dir`/`--work-tree` are explicit, verified directly). HEAD answered the way the measurement anticipated: kept as a real, agent-owned file, synced in by `SyncHeadIn` before every spawn and out by `SyncHeadOut` (as the runtime's own identity, `symbolic-ref HEAD`) whenever a spawn actually moved it — proven by `TestHeadSyncedOut_AfterAgentCheckoutMinusB_RuntimeCommitsOntoRightBranch`, which checks out a fresh branch agent-side and then commits runtime-side, landing on the branch the sync propagated. `objects`/`refs`/`packed-refs`/`index`/`logs`/`info` are shared by directory/file symlink, not `objects/info/alternates` as this row's own text speculated — alternates was measured and rejected (agent-written objects never became visible to the runtime's own git through it; see `docs/DECISIONS.md`); `TestSharedObjects_VisibleBothWays` pins the symlink shape both directions and fails under alternates by mutation. **Closing signal fired**: `TestOpenClass_ContentFilterExecutes`/`TestOpenClass_MergeDriverExecutes` (named in Step 130's own row) went RED once this shipped and were deleted, replaced by the `TestClosedClass_*` proofs Step 130's own entry now records. Residual, accepted risks carried forward, not closed by this Step: a worktree-root redirection (the runtime replacing `<worktree>` itself, pre-existing and out of this Step's scope), and git's own parser still running as root inside the sandbox (unchanged; this Step removes the runtime-owned-config attack surface, not root's own exposure to a malicious repository's tracked content). A third, found by this Step's own review and filed as Step 189: root's git still writes THROUGH the shared data entries, so a symlink the runtime plants under its own `.git` redirects that write -- pre-existing, unchanged by this Step, and not closable by a top-level check. Correction found in review, not in the original measurement: `Run` — the one choke point every agent git invocation goes through — now `Lstat`s `repo.WorkTree` and `repo.WorkTree/.git` before every single spawn, not only once at `Seed` time, and refuses if either has been swapped for a symlink since; `TestRun_RefusesASymlinkSwappedWorktree` pins it, and removing the guard by mutation reopens exactly the failure that test catches. | §30.5 |
| 172 | automations never dispatch on a real webhook | `MatchesGitHubTrigger`/`MatchesLinearTrigger` exist and are tested, and `internal/domain/automation/doc.go` says so itself: live dispatch, wiring either matcher into the already-merged ingress handlers, was never built. So every automation trigger is evaluable and none fires. Step 52 shipped correctly without it and recorded the absence, which is why this is filed here rather than folded back into it. Wire the ingress to the existing engine FIRST; only then normalise `check_run` and `status` with name and conclusion plus deduplication. For a commit status, a branch that merely CONTAINS the commit is not the branch whose tip it is -- treating them alike fires on the wrong branch | §8.4 |
| 173 | a review verdict does not know what it reviewed | `review_verdicts` persists `HeadSHA` and nothing else about the context, and `ComputeEligible` compares that against the live head alone. `Base` in the GitHub decoder reads `ref` and never `sha`. So a PR evaluated on another PR's branch, then retargeted or with that parent moved, keeps an unchanged head and its old verdict still reads as current. Verified: no base-branch gate exists in `autoapproval` or `revalidate.go`, and the one that would provide it is Step 142, unshipped -- nothing downstream stops it. Persist repo, PR, headSHA, baseRef, baseSHA, the ordered ancestor chain and a policy version; add an attempt id distinct from that context, so two attempts on identical code share a context but only one may publish; and make `not_assessed` a first-class outcome with no invented risk value. The chain is per PR, never a whole-stack revision: changing PR5 invalidates PR5 and its descendants, not PR1-4. Amend §21.1 and §39.3 together, since §39.3 reuses the head-only freshness this replaces | §21.1, §39.3 |
| 174 | the review has no GitHub-native result surface | Narvi posts formal reviews and labels; no `narvi/review` check run is published anywhere (check-runs are read, never written). A check would express queued, running, stale and terminal, and let a repository make the review required. Publish through the existing outbox, keeping the check identity and re-checking attempt and context before emitting; select by SHA **and GitHub App**, so another app's same-named check is never adopted; open a NEW check when a review restarts after a terminal result; and surface permission, rate-limit and transient failures distinctly. Requires an installation with `checks:write`, to be confirmed for Narvi. Human acceptance binds to the exact verdict, attempt and context, with author, justification and auditable revocation; a new verdict or context makes it inapplicable, and it never rewrites the risk as low. **Policy:** `not_assessed` is never published as `neutral`: GitHub counts `neutral` as satisfying a required check, so an unassessed PR would become mergeable by rule. That is the same shape as a failure rendering as a confident normal state, which this repository has now fixed three times elsewhere. `not_assessed` must never satisfy Narvi's auto-merge. Migration must also handle old blocking `REQUEST_CHANGES` reviews: a passing check does not retract them. **Amended (§21.1b):** an emission carries the attempt and context it was produced for and is REFUSED when either has been superseded -- head equality alone cannot identify a result, so an older emission with a matching head must still lose to a newer attempt or a newer base, and a publisher keying on head overwrites a current result with a stale one invisibly. A creation race resolves to ONE identity by §5.1's atomic claim: two active identities for one PR is worse than none, since each is plausible and which one a reader sees depends on timing. The `not_assessed` question is not settled by refusing Narvi's own auto-merge -- what a check REPORTS for a review that did not complete is a separate decision, as is whether a repository may make the check required, and what the check must still say truthfully when enforcement is off. Acceptance binds to one verdict, attempt and context, is made inapplicable by a new attempt or a moved base, and never rewrites the assessment: CI green at the current head, blast radius known and sensitive paths stay mandatory, because an acceptance that reclassifies a verdict as low-risk destroys both §21.1's analytics and §21.2's contradiction-rate calibration. **Starts after Step 173 ships** -- the publisher identifies a result by attempt and context, which did not exist before it. **SCOPE NARROWED (2026-09-18, owner decision): this Step is the PUBLISHER ONLY.** Human acceptance moves to Step 188, because the two are separable and acceptance depends on the publisher existing rather than the reverse; the experience of Step 173 -- twelve review rounds over one wide surface -- is the reason for splitting rather than a preference. **THREE POLICY DECISIONS TAKEN (2026-09-18), each recorded rather than discovered.** (1) A review that did not complete publishes **`action_required`**. GitHub has no conclusion value meaning "not assessed", and this is the only one whose GitHub semantics match the truth: it does NOT satisfy a required check, and it tells a human an action is expected. Rejected: `failure`, which blocks correctly but lies by excess -- an operator would go looking for findings that do not exist, and it collapses two states the rest of this design separates; and leaving the check `in_progress`, which is honest and blocking but makes an unevaluated review indistinguishable from a crashed worker, which is the exact 'a failure rendering as a confident normal state' shape. (2) A repository **may** make the check required, and a check is published in **`queued`** as soon as a pull request enters scope -- before any review starts. Without that, a PR on which no review was ever triggered carries no check at all, and a required check that is never published is the classic hole: the PR becomes mergeable by absence. (3) When enforcement is OFF the check must still say exactly what it says when enforcement is on. A check that gates nothing is still a statement to every human reading the pull request, and a publisher that softens its own output when nothing depends on it is reporting to the rule rather than to the reader. **Shipped, and inert until Step 193:** the publisher writes with the static bot token, but only a GitHub App can create a check run and an App's installation token lapses after an hour, so the check has no durable credential (§44.1) until Step 193 gives it the `publish` App's minted tokens | §8.2, §21.1, §21.1b |
| 175 | the pinned agent runtime, its stream, its catalogue and its image drift apart | `opencode-ai@1.17.15` is pinned in `platform/config.go` and twice in CI; the embedded catalogue carries neither Astra nor Fable 5.1; and `sse.go` deliberately ignores `message.part.delta` as redundant with the cumulative `message.part.updated`. Delta handling changes precisely at 1.18.30, which makes that assumption a migration risk rather than a present defect -- the current pin is not shown to be broken. Treat the bump as one lot: verify against the real binary that parts assemble per message, that deltas do not duplicate the full updates, that parent and child sessions stay separate, and that the transcript is exact after a reconnection; refresh the catalogue from the runtime actually chosen, with capabilities, efforts and costs consistent. Do not import private model endpoints. **Preservation criteria, because this lot is where they would be lost:** a runtime and image bump is exactly the change that can silently drop a protection nobody re-reads. Three already exist and must still hold afterwards, each asserted in this lot rather than assumed. A **restored** sandbox must come up with the same capabilities a freshly created one has, and tools disabled since the snapshot was minted must be gone from it -- a snapshot carries what it was minted with, whatever the current configuration says (§35.5b). A media key that was forged, or that belongs to another session, must still be refused: server-side key construction and bounded reads exist today and a bump must not route around them. And a result arriving from an older provider generation must still be rejected -- `gen` fencing (Step 7) is what makes a superseded sandbox's late answer harmless, and it is invisible until the day it is gone. None of these is a new test campaign; each is one assertion added to this lot's own acceptance. A fourth exists since 2026-09-27: the `token` cadence. The store keeps every distinct frame of a text part (§6.1, "Stored `token` frames"), and 1.17.15 sends two per part, which `tokencadence_realbinary_test.go` pins against the real binary with no credential; a runtime whose delta handling changes that cadence must coalesce frames in the adapter before the bump, not after, or rows and cumulative bytes grow with every delta. **Amended:** the catalogue drift has grown -- `internal/app/modelcatalog/snapshot.json` also lacks Opus 5.5 and GPT 6 Sol/Luna -- and two more assertions join the acceptance: a reply belonging to an earlier turn never completes the current one, and a compaction summary is never a turn's answer | §7.1, §6.1 |
| 176 | the image decision is unreadable after the fact | Warm boot logs skip, delta and full, but the image choice falls back to the default silently for several ordinary states, so the cause of a cold boot is lost once the page is reloaded. Persist a selection or fallback reason and expose which dependency manifests changed, reusing the existing collection and event log rather than adding a surface | §19 |
| 177 | a provider failure names its union member and nothing that locates it | The adapter classifies an engine error well enough to decide whether to retry -- `openCodeTaggedError` carries the union name and `openCodeErrorData` is modelled, deliberately, only for the fields that decision consumes. Once the retries are exhausted a human gets `opencode: APIError` and a failure reason: not the model that ran, not the runtime version, not the provider's request identifier, which is the one token that makes a support conversation with that provider possible. The sandbox side already does better -- §4.1's `ProviderError` carries a fixed-vocabulary `Op` and the provider's own code. Retain the available fields by **allowlist**, never by keeping the error object whole: `APIError`'s payload also carries `responseHeaders` and `responseBody`, where credentials and the prompt itself live, and a blocklist over those two is a redaction rule the next schema addition defeats. Size-cap on top. Surface one record in both the session journal and the correlation-id-scoped operator path, never a detail present in one and absent from the other. The retry decision does not move: it stays on the typed discriminator, and the moment a diagnostic field becomes an input to it this has become classification by error text. Exit: a provider failure is traceable from the diagnostic to its cause **and** the same diagnostic is asserted to contain neither credentials nor request content -- a test that checks only the first half is a check that passes while verifying nothing about the half that carries the risk | §7.3 |
| 178 | the guide drift check only runs in the direction that does not fail | `TestNoGuideDrift` fails a guide documenting a command the code does not implement. It cannot fail a guide that **omits** one the code does implement -- and omission is the realistic failure, because shipping a capability and updating its guide are two acts, usually by two people, at different times, and only one of them is enforced. Step 78 shipped correctly and said so: the check ties documented commands to real routes, which is exactly half the property the directory claims. The missing half is harder than its sibling and the difficulty is the design: **nothing in the router knows which routes are meant for a person.** A webhook receiver, a health endpoint and a BFF route the web app calls are all real and belong in no user guide, so the check needs an explicit register of routes deliberately absent from the guides, each with a reason. That register is a claim someone makes, never a default -- and it is also the way this check fails: a register that becomes a dumping ground turns the whole thing into a check that passes while verifying nothing, which is the defect it was built against. Require a non-empty reason per entry, refuse a catch-all or wildcard entry, and review the register as code. Exit: adding a user-facing route with no guide entry and no register entry makes the test fail, and the mutation is recorded in the PR the way Step 78 recorded its own three | §10-P6 |
| 179 | a half-read CI surface reports green, and auto-merge acts on it | `fetchCIConclusionLive` makes two GETs -- the legacy combined-status endpoint and check-runs -- and each is `if body, err := doGet(...); err == nil { ... }`, so a failed call contributes nothing at all. The terminal switch then returns success whenever `sawSuccess` was set and neither failure nor incomplete was. A status GET that succeeds with `state: success` beside a check-runs GET that returns 500 therefore yields **CI green**, not degraded and not unknown, and only a failure of BOTH falls back to unknown. Reproduced against the real adapter with an `httptest` server during Step 173's sixth review round: `found=true err=<nil> CI="success" reviewDegraded=false filesDegraded=false`. Its two siblings in the same composite each carry a real degraded field (`ReviewDecisionDegraded`, `ChangedFilesListDegraded`) and `ComputeEligible` refuses on each; the CI read has no such signal, so nothing downstream can tell a green it verified from a green it half-read, and the unattended merge worker acts on it with no second CI read. **Not introduced by Step 173** -- that Step bounded the composite and closed the deadline flavour, which is why this is filed rather than folded in: the ordinary-failure flavour (a transient 5xx, a secondary-rate-limit 403, an undecodable body) leaves the context error nil and every guard silent. Narrower than it looks: a repository whose checks are Actions-only reports `total_count: 0`, contributes no `sawSuccess`, and falls through to unknown safely -- the exposed set is repositories carrying third-party commit statuses alongside Actions. Give the CI read the same fail-closed shape its two siblings already have. Exit: a status GET succeeding beside a failing check-runs GET must not produce an eligible PR, and the mutation is recorded -- an assertion that only checks the both-failed case is a check that passes while verifying nothing about the half this Step exists for | §21.2, §8.2 |
| 189 | root's git follows symlinks the runtime plants under its own `.git` | Step 171 moved every command-carrying piece of git state (`config`, `hooks`) into a git-dir sandbox-agent owns, and deliberately left the data SHARED with the runtime-owned `<wt>/.git` by symlink -- `objects`, `refs`, `packed-refs`, `logs`, `info`, `index` -- because data names no command. It still names a PATH. The runtime owns `<wt>/.git` and can replace `index`, `packed-refs`, any file under `logs/`, or any directory at any depth (`objects`, `objects/<xx>/`, `refs/heads/`, `info`) with a symlink to an arbitrary absolute path. The index and packed-refs lockfiles resolve the symlink before their rename, and objects, reflogs and ref directories are written through ordinary path resolution, so the next fetch, checkout, stash, sparse-checkout or push that sandbox-agent runs as root creates that path, appends a reflog line to it if it already exists, or rewrites it if it already parses as an index or packed-refs file. (A symlink at a LEAF loose ref is not followed: git replaces the link itself, and a dangling one only makes the update fail.) Reproduced with real git during Step 171's review and again when this row was filed, same-uid in a scratch tree (in production the writer is sandbox-agent, i.e. root): `index` pointed at a non-existent outside path had that file created, `objects` pointed outside the workspace received the new loose objects there, and a planted `logs/HEAD` appended a reflog line to an existing outside file. **Pre-existing, not introduced**: before Step 171 root ran git directly against the very same entries, so the exposure is unchanged, which is why it is filed rather than folded into that Step. Measured impact so far: arbitrary file creation as root with git-formatted content, and an append to an arbitrary EXISTING file of a reflog line whose branch names the runtime controls (Seed sets `core.logAllRefUpdates`, so that path is live). Code execution is not demonstrated, but the reflog append makes it plausible wherever a root-read file is parsed as a whitespace-separated list, which puts this row ahead of the other Phase 11 rows in priority. **What is NOT a fix**: checking that the six top-level entries are real directories and files. A symlink can sit at any depth, and the runtime is live while sandbox-agent's git runs, so any walk is a TOCTOU -- a guard that covers the top level reads like a closure and is not one, the same enumeration trap this plan has refused for transport helpers and filter drivers. Shapes to MEASURE, not assume, in the manner Step 171's own shape was measured: (a) run the local, credential-free operations (checkout, stash, status, rev-parse, sparse-checkout, clean) under the runtime's own uid, still against the agent-owned config, so any redirected write lands only where the runtime could already write -- root is then needed only for the network operations, which carry the credential; (b) fetch into agent-private objects and refs and publish into the runtime tree through a write the runtime cannot redirect; (c) a mount the runtime cannot reparent. Each trades differently against why sandbox-agent runs git as root at all (the credential helper must not be reachable by the runtime's uid). **Related, closed separately, not by this row**: root's re-own of the workspace for the runtime (`boot.ChownWorkspaceForRuntime`) had the same exposure -- a planted symlink redirected root's chown and listing outside the workspace -- and is closed by an fd-relative walk whose guarantee §30.5 records. That walk re-owns; it does not make git's own path-based writes safe, so it closes nothing here. Whatever this row restructures must keep the re-own walk fd-relative: its swap tests (`workspaceowner_redirect_test.go`) go red if any of its listings, looks, descents or re-owns goes by a path instead of through the directory fd it holds. Exit: a runtime-planted symlink anywhere under `<wt>/.git` cannot make any sandbox-agent git operation write outside what the runtime can already write, proven with real git, and the Step 171 review's reproduction kept as a test that goes red if it ever regresses | §30.5 |
| 198 | automatic re-reviews pile up behind a running review | Step 65's debounce handler (`handleReviewRetriggerDebounceTimer`, `internal/app/sessionactor/reviewretrigger.go`) decides from the opt-in, a plan awaiting approval, the latest verdict's head and the budget, never from the session's in-flight turns: each firing inserts a new pending review turn whether or not a turn of the same review session is already running. | 216 | a live session actor pins a query connection | Every live session actor held a connection from its replica's query pool for its whole life, only to keep the session-level advisory lock §2 gives it: `hydrateAndAcquire` (`internal/app/sessionactor/hydrate.go`) took it from the pool and released it only when the actor shut down, at least `ActorIdleTTL` (30 minutes) after its last command. Hydration then asked the same pool for more, unbounded and on its caller's context. So once all but one connection were pinned, the next spawn took the last as its lock connection and waited forever for a second inside `BumpActorEpoch`, and every other user of the pool (HTTP handlers, the timer pump's claim, the outbox and automation claims, every live actor's `transact`) waited behind it with no deadline. Readiness failed and liveness, a TCP check, did not, so the replica stayed up, unready and wedged; when the stuck caller was the timer pump every timer on the replica stopped, and the stuck actors kept their locks, so their sessions stalled on every replica. Reproduced at pool sizes 1 and 4, and it hung CI once. Filed together with the fix that closes it. **Actors hold no query connection.** A replica holds all its actors' locks on one dedicated connection outside the pool (`lockHolder`, `internal/app/sessionactor/lockholder.go`), dialled as the pool dials its own: from the pool's configuration, host by host under each host's `connect_timeout`, in the background and never under a hydration's bound, which only waits for it. When the URL sets no `connect_timeout`, each connect attempt is bounded by `ActorLockConnectAttemptTimeout` (5 s, below `ActorLockProbeInterval`) rather than the pool's 2 minutes: every hydration on the replica waits for that one dial, and one started during a partition would otherwise keep the replica from hosting anything for up to about a minute after the heal. Each statement on it runs under `ActorLockStatementTimeout` and never under a caller's cancellation, because a cancelled statement breaks the connection and with it every lock the connection holds. A statement that finds the connection gone (closed, a network error, a FATAL or PANIC, or an overrun) presumes it lost: it is closed, every actor locked under it is stopped, a generation makes an older connection's unlock a no-op, and a new connection is dialled at once; a server-side ERROR on a live connection fails only that hydration, except on an unlock, where any error closes the connection, since closing is the one release that is certain. A hydration that finds the connection it was handed already dead retries once, on a new one, within its bound, and a probe every `ActorLockProbeInterval` finds a connection that died silently and keeps one dialled. Closing a lost connection releases its locks only once the server hears of it, and after a partition that outlasts TCP's retransmission it never does: the backend would keep every lock for the server's own keepalive time, over two hours by default, and none of those sessions could be hosted anywhere meanwhile. So every lock connection asks the server for short keepalives on its own end (`ActorLockServerKeepalive*`, overriding the URL's; they reap such a backend within `ActorLockServerReapTime`, 25 s, which `Validate` keeps above one probe and below `TimerClaimDuration`), and the first connection dialled after a loss terminates the lost one's backend if it still runs the lost connection's session, waits up to `ActorLockOrphanTerminateWait` (1 s, which `Validate` keeps below the hydration bound) for that backend to end, since a backend releases its locks only as it exits, and is installed only once that terminate has returned; a server older than Postgres 14 cannot wait, so there the backend was only signalled (a fallback Step 224 removed once boot refused any server below 16). The match is on pid, start time and database, and on the lost connection's own application name, `narvi-actor-locks-` and a nonce drawn afresh on every dial, never on the new connection's own backend: behind a session-mode pooler a backend outlives the client session that locked on it, and is reset and handed to whichever client the pooler links next, under that client's own name. Advisory locks are re-entrant per backend, so concurrent spawns of one session share one hydration, and the holder refuses a second lock on a session it already holds. A hydration is bounded by `ActorHydrateTimeout`, past which only a lock statement already running and the failure's own unlock may run, and fails with a retryable `ErrActorUnavailable`: the sandbox socket answers 503 with `Retry-After`, the timer pump ends its batch and lets the claims expire, and a dispatch trigger after a committed write only logs, as a refusal from another replica already did. The lock, its key and the epoch fence are unchanged, so old and new pods can share a rolling deploy. A replica's connections are its pool plus one, whatever it hosts (§5.1): boot logs that budget against `max_connections` and warns, never refuses, and a transaction-mode pooler is unsupported. Not taken: ownership by a lease row and heartbeat, deferred in `docs/DECISIONS.md` with the condition that reopens it. Exit: six actors on a pool of two hold none of its connections and an unrelated query answers at once; a hydration on a saturated pool fails within its bound although its caller would wait longer, and leaves the lock free; sixteen concurrent spawns of one session get one actor and stack no lock; losing the lock connection stops every actor it held and the next spawn hydrates on a new one; an older connection's unlock leaves the new lock held; at the shipped timeouts, the first spawn after the lock connection died silently succeeds, and so does a timer-pump tick's every delivery; behind a multi-host URL whose first host hangs, the lock connection still comes up and hydration then succeeds; a server-side error fails one hydration and stops no actor; a hydration racing a loss is refused at insert; after a partition that outlasts TCP's retransmission, the lost connection's backend still holds its sessions at the heal, and both sessions rehydrate on the same replica within the reap time plus one probe because the next connection terminated it; the lock backend runs with the lock connection's server keepalives whatever the URL sets, and the pool keeps the URL's; that terminate spares a backend whose start time, application name or database differs, and, behind a session-mode pooler, spares the redial and another replica's live lock connection when either is handed the lost connection's backend, while it ends one still running the lost session; the new connection is installed only once that terminate has returned, and only once the backend it signalled has ended, so the first hydration after the install finds that backend's sessions free; a backend that outlasts the wait is logged and the connection installed anyway; a server that cannot wait still had the backend signalled, until Step 224 removed that fallback with its test, boot refusing every server that cannot wait (below 14; the floor is 16); a loss is retried once on a connection open before the call and not at all on one dialled for it; with no `connect_timeout` in the URL a hung first host is passed after `ActorLockConnectAttemptTimeout`, and a dial started while the network drops packets ends at that bound, after which the first hydration, or the next probe, dials afresh, while a URL's own `connect_timeout` is kept; a loss is redialled at once, and a probe that finds no connection dials one; an earlier generation's unlock never waits its turn, and the current one's always does | §2, §5.1 |
| 218 | Slack's Request-changes modal drops the person's feedback on a `409` | `handleViewSubmission` (`internal/adapters/inbound/slack/interactive.go`) creates the revision through `CreateTurnCore` with `RejectIfOpen`, reads no plan status, and on any error from that call only logs "create request-changes turn failed" and answers a bare `200`. While any turn of the session is pending, dispatched or processing -- an approved implementation running, typically -- the call is refused `409`, Slack closes the modal as accepted, and the feedback typed into it is gone: nothing is queued, no busy reply is posted, and the person is told nothing. Step 183 shipped correctly without changing it -- its plan-decision PR (contracts 1.12.0) brought plan decisions and prompts over MCP and left the Slack modal as it was -- and §43.21(b) states it as it is (the modal "only logs it and acknowledges the submission, so the modal closes and the feedback is lost with no reply"). The two other chat paths that meet an open turn, a Slack thread reply and a Linear reply (`DropIfOpen`), already answer with a busy reply, so this is the one chat path that fails silently. Answer the `409` the way the same handler already answers an authorization denial, with a `response_action: errors` response: the modal stays open with the text still in it and says a turn is running and the change can be submitted once it ends. Any other failure of the create shows the generic error text the handler already uses when the authorization check itself fails, never a closed modal. Nothing is queued: §43.21(a)'s revision rule is unchanged, and no chat ingress gains a queueing policy. Exit: a Request-changes submission while the approved implementation is processing leaves the modal open with the feedback and the busy message, and creates no turn; the same submission once the session is idle creates the revision as today; a server error on the create shows the generic error rather than closing the modal. **SHIPPED.** `CreateTurnCore`'s `RejectIfOpen` refusal now carries a sentinel, `httpapi.ErrTurnAlreadyOpen`, beside its unchanged `409` and text, so REST and MCP answer exactly as before. `handleViewSubmission` answers any create failure through one builder, `writeViewSubmissionError`, which every other refusal of the modal (blank feedback, an authorization denial, a failed authorization check) now uses too: a `200` with `response_action: errors` and one message keyed on the modal's input block (`slackapi.RequestChangesBlockID`), so the modal stays open with the feedback in it. `requestChangesRefusalText` picks the busy text for the sentinel alone and the generic text for anything else, including a `409` without it. A created revision is still answered with an empty `200`, which closes the modal. Nothing is queued and no admission policy changed. The tests run on the real handler and Postgres: the modal is opened through the real `block_actions` path and its input block read back from the `views.open` request, so the error is looked up under the block the modal renders; with the approved implementation pending, dispatched or processing, the busy text, no turn inserted or audited, then the revision created once it has completed; a create whose transaction cannot begin shows the generic error; and the real refusal, reworded or stripped of its sentinel, pins that the type decides, not the text or the status | §43.21, §8.1 |
| 219 | an open enum is open only in the MCP output schemas | `contracts/manifest.json` lists 18 open enums, and `contracts/COMPATIBILITY.md` grades a value added to one MINOR (rule 11 of its table) -- a promise that a consumer written against today's contract keeps working when the value arrives. Only the MCP tool output schemas keep it: `bundleOutputSchema` (`internal/adapters/inbound/mcp/schemas.go`) moves each open enum's `enum` into `examples` (§43.10). Everywhere else the list is closed. The published `contracts/rest/v1/dtos.schema.json` carries all 18 as closed `enum` nodes, and only 4 of their descriptions say a consumer must tolerate a value it does not recognise, so a client validating a response against the published schema rejects a later value. And `go-jsonschema` makes each a type whose generated `UnmarshalJSON` checks `enumValues_*` (`contracts/gen/go/restdtos/restdtos.go`) and fails the whole decode on a value it does not list: a `Session` whose `status` or `spawnSource` a newer server added makes the entire response undecodable -- in the generated Go decoders `COMPATIBILITY.md`'s consumer obligations name as a platform-to-client consumer, obligations that cover unknown properties and union variants and say nothing of enum values. Nothing in this repository decodes a response through them (the control plane only encodes them, and the MCP bridge passes the REST body through unparsed), so no shipped path breaks, which is why Step 183 shipped correctly without it: its first PR (contracts 1.9.2, which opened `Session.spawnSource`) also opened every open enum in the MCP output schemas, where a client is asked to validate what it receives, and none of its PRs changed the published schema or the generated types' decoding of an open enum. An external client built from this contract breaks under exactly the version skew the MINOR grade exists for -- a rolling deploy, a rollback (a value added to a Postgres enum cannot be removed), a client older than its server. Make open mean open in every published artifact, from the one list: the Go types generated for an open enum decode a value they do not list and keep it distinguishable from the known ones (`tools/contractspatch`'s post-processing, or generation from an opened schema), every open enum's description states the tolerance, and `COMPATIBILITY.md`'s obligations name enum values. Whether the published schema itself trades `enum` for `examples`, as the MCP bundle does, or keeps it and leaves the promise to the manifest, is decided first: dropping it is itself a change `tools/contractscompat` has to grade. Closed enums and client-to-platform enums are untouched. Exit: a generated Go client decodes a `Session` whose `status` and `spawnSource` this build does not know, and still refuses an unknown value of a closed enum; a new `openEnums` entry reaches the generated types and its description with no hand edit, pinned by a test that fails on an open enum the generator left closed | §6.3, §43.10 |
