| 221 | the session view reads a sandbox's boot, and a live escalation, by rules the server no longer uses (shipped) | Two readings in the web session view derive from the event log or a list what the server now decides otherwise. **Boot.** `buildTimelineModel` (`web/src/session/timelineModel.ts`) sets `sawReady` on the first `ready` event and clears the boot phase, after which `isStillBooting` (`sessionStatus.ts`) stops reading an empty session as booting; `buildSandboxRailModel` (`sandboxRail.ts`) likewise lets a `ready` event set the rail's status to ready over the snapshot's, and close the open boot phase. But `ready` is the first event of a connection (the sandbox-ws contract), and §3.2 keeps the sandbox `booting` after it until the same gen has shown boot evidence and a heartbeat reports a null phase -- the fix that stopped the control plane marking a sandbox ready mid-boot, which left this reading in the web as it was. The agent dials before it clones, and a repository with no service and no Docker reports no boot phase as an event, so for the whole clone, sync and hooks an empty session reads "No events yet." and the rail reads ready, while the server holds the sandbox `booting` and dispatches nothing to it. **The featured workflow run.** `featuredRun` (`workflowRunFormat.ts`) features the newest run that is `running` or `needs_review`, and a test pins a `needs_review` run winning over a newer completed one. But `needs_review` is never left, the next turn starts a fresh run beside the parked one, and §43.20 makes an escalation live only while its run is the session's newest, has run an attempt of its own, is not a built-in's, and no turn has been created since it escalated -- the rule Step 182 gave the status surface. So once a session has escalated, `WorkflowRunsView` features that parked run over every later one and keeps polling it, on a session the status surface reads as finished. Both server rules shipped correctly; the web was left reading the older ones. The session view takes both from the server: the boot from the sandbox status the server derives, never from `ready` -- the snapshot carries that status but is read on (re)subscribe only, so its change has to reach an open page -- and the featured run by the rule the status surface applies, a parked escalation that is no longer live staying listed in the rail but never featured. Exit: an empty session whose sandbox is still booting after its `ready` reads booting, with its phase when one is reported, until the server marks it ready, and the rail agrees with the server; a session with a parked `needs_review` run followed by a completed one features the completed run and does not poll the parked one; a live escalation of a custom workflow is still featured with its decision gate **Shipped:** the session actor writes a sandbox's status or generation only through `sandboxWrites` (`internal/app/sessionactor/sandboxstatus.go`), and every committed transaction that leaves either changed appends one `sandbox_status` event (`{"sandbox": {"gen", "status"}}`) in that transaction, broadcast after the commit -- none for the liveness bump, a passage through a status and back, or a rollback (`TestSandboxStatusEvent_OnlyACommittedChangeIsReported`, `TestSandboxStatusEvent_BootReportsTheServersStatus`); `TestSandboxStatusWritesGoThroughTheRecorder` and `TestSandboxStatusQueriesAreTheRecordedOnes` keep every such write, in Go and in SQL, on it. Every `fetch_history` reply carries the sandbox row (`FetchHistoryResponse.sandbox`), and `SessionActivity.escalation` reports the open escalation whatever `awaiting` names first -- contracts 1.16.0, MINOR. The web takes the boot from that row (`SessionStream.sandboxState` follows every reply): `isStillBooting` reads the server's status (a session with no sandbox yet still reads booting, as before), `buildTimelineModel` drops its phase only on a `sandbox_status` that ends the boot, and the rail's status is the snapshot's alone -- the agent's `ready` is shown as "agent connected", and neither it, a `boot_progress` nor a fatal agent `error` moves the status; a phase still open when the server says the boot is over shows finished, its duration unknown. `featuredRun` features the newest live run (`running`, or the run `escalation` names), else the newest run not parked at `needs_review`, and only a live run is re-read on a timer (`isLiveRun`); the run view polls `GET /api/sessions/{id}/status` beside the run list, and a parked escalation's banner says nothing waits on it any more. A sandbox-socket frame typed as one of the control plane's own event types (`serverEventTypes`: `sandbox_status`, `image_decision`, `shadow_egress_suppressed`), one without a type, and one with a top-level `events`, `nextCursor` or `sandbox` key are dropped like a stale gen's, unstored, unbroadcast and unacked (`refusedSandboxFrame`, `TestSandboxSocket_RefusesFramesASandboxMayNotSend`), and the page takes a frame for the `fetch_history` reply only when it has no `type`; `TestServerWrittenEventTypesAreReserved` keeps that set whole, SQL inserts into `events` included; the two write guards type-check the production packages and read `UPDATE ONLY`, a qualified or quoted table and a column-list `SET`. A page reads the boot from the agent's `ready` from a reply without the sandbox row until its next subscribe (rollout compatibility, `SessionStream.serverReportsSandbox`, true from every subscribe reply). | 222 | a timer kind this binary does not know is redelivered forever (shipped) | `handleTimerFired`'s default branch (`internal/app/sessionactor/timerfired.go`) logs "ignoring TimerFired with unknown name" and returns without deleting the row. The pump claims a due timer by moving its `fires_at` forward by `TimerClaimDuration` (30 s) before delivering it, so an unknown kind is delivered again every 30 seconds for as long as its row exists, each delivery waking the session's actor, and §43.20's status counts it as scheduled work (`TimerCanCreateWork` counts a kind it does not know), so the session never reads settled. Keeping the row is right while a newer replica armed it during a rolling deploy: deleting it there would drop the newer binary's work. Keeping it forever is wrong once nothing will ever handle it: after a rollback past the Step that introduced the kind, or once a kind is retired. Step 217 met this with its own `stop` timer and worked around it in its down migration, which deletes every armed `stop` timer for exactly this reason (`migrations/000151_session_stop_requested_at.down.sql`) -- correct for that kind, and the reason the default is filed rather than fixed there: every later kind inherits it. Bound it once, in the dispatcher, not per migration, and by the time since the row was last armed (`session_timers.armed_at`, added for it by migration 000153: every arm and re-arm sets it and the pump's claim does not; `created_at`, first named here, keeps the first insert, so a kind a newer binary keeps re-arming in place would have read as old as its session), so that a newer replica's timer during a rolling deploy costs no more latency than it does today. While the row is younger than a grace window longer than any rolling deploy (a bound in `internal/platform/timeouts.go`), an unknown kind is handled exactly as today: kept, and back within one `TimerClaimDuration`. Past the grace it is re-armed at a backoff well above `TimerClaimDuration` instead of every claim window, logged at warning with its name and counted; past a second, longer bound it is deleted with a warning naming it. Until then status keeps counting it as work, the safe direction. Exit: an unknown kind younger than the grace is delivered again within one `TimerClaimDuration` of its claim, so an implementation that applies the backoff inside the grace fails the test; one older than the grace is delivered at the backoff, not every claim window, and is gone after the second bound with one warning naming it; a kind a newer replica armed during a deploy is still there, at the claim cadence, when that replica's pump claims it; the known kinds are unaffected. **Shipped:** the decision is one pure function, `sessionactor.DecideUnknownTimer`, beside `ClassifyTimer`, fed the row's `armed_at` and the database's `now()` from one statement (`TimerStore.Age`), so no replica's clock is involved; `handleTimerFired` sends a name only when no handler of its own takes it, and applies the decision in one transaction: kept, it writes nothing; backed off, it moves `fires_at` alone; deleted, it deletes -- both writes only while the row still carries the `armed_at` read, so a re-arm between the read and the write wins. Migration 000153 adds `armed_at` (`NOT NULL DEFAULT now()`, backfilled from `created_at`); `UpsertSessionTimer` sets it on every re-arm, and the claim never moves it. The previous binary works with the column present, but cannot boot on 000153 until it is migrated down or forced back to 152, as the migration's own "Rolling back" says. Three bounds in `internal/platform/timeouts.go`: `UnknownTimerGrace` (1 h), above `RollingDeployCeiling` (30 min, an operational assumption the production checklist asks the operator to keep: a Deployment's progress deadline only bounds the gap between two progress events, so nothing in Kubernetes bounds a rollout; `internal/ops` checks that the shipped two-replica Deployment is replaced within it unless Kubernetes flags the rollout); `UnknownTimerBackoff` (10 min), above `TimerClaimDuration`; `UnknownTimerDeleteAfter` (24 h), above the grace. Past the grace each backoff and the deletion log one WARN naming the kind and count `session_timer_unknown_kind_total` by action; a kind inside the grace logs as before and is not counted. One case keeps the down migration: a kind armed about a day or more before it is due is deleted at its first delivery by a binary that does not know it, and one re-armed with a lead `L` survives only about `UnknownTimerDeleteAfter` minus `L` after a rollback, so a new kind keeps its re-arm lead well under a day | §2, §43.20 |
| 223 | a dispatch trigger that fails is not redelivered (shipped) | Every path that creates a turn commits it and then, outside the transaction, asks the session's actor to plan a dispatch: `TriggerDispatch` (`internal/adapters/inbound/httpapi/create.go`) and its inline twin at the end of `createTurnLocked` (`turn.go`), which the web, Slack, Linear, the code-host mention and MCP all reach; plan and workflow-step approval, child sessions, the automation fan-out and the sentinel auto-fix call it too. A third copy serves the release composition review: `dispatchCompositionReview` (`internal/app/releasereview/compositiondispatch.go`) inserts its turn with a bare `CompositionTurns.Create`, in autocommit, with no transaction and no session lock, and then calls `releaseCompositionDispatcher.EnsureDispatched` (`controlplane/serve.go`), whose failure it only warn-logs; it runs on the release manifest worker, which with more than one replica is often not on the replica hosting the review session. A failure on any of the three only logs: `GetOrSpawn` refused because this replica could not host the actor within `ActorHydrateTimeout` (`actor_unavailable`) or because another replica owns it (`actor_elsewhere`), or a `Send` to an actor that has stopped. The caller answers success, rightly -- the turn is committed, and a retried request would create a second one. The pending turn is then dispatched by the next command that reaches its session, a sandbox frame or a timer; a session with neither -- no live sandbox (a new session's, or one that idled out) and no armed timer -- keeps its turn pending until something else touches it, which may be never. `TriggerDispatch`'s own doc comment says so ("durable redelivery of a failed trigger is not built yet"), which is why Step 216, whose bounded hydration makes `actor_unavailable` a routine answer under a saturated pool or a lost lock connection, shipped correctly without it. `actor_elsewhere` needs no fault at all: with more than one replica, it is the answer on every replica but the one hosting the session. Make the trigger durable the way §2 makes a stop request durable: the transaction that creates the turn also upserts a session timer due at once, under the lock REST already takes to insert a turn, so the timer pump -- on whichever replica claims it, and again after each claim window -- delivers the dispatch evaluation even when the post-commit trigger fails. The composition review has no such transaction to join, so it gains one: its turn insert and the timer upsert commit together under the session's actor-epoch lock, as `createTurnLocked`'s do, rather than as two autocommit writes a crash can separate. All six insert sites call it -- `createTurnLocked`, `CreateSessionOnTx`, `DecidePlanOnTx`, the workflow engine's `dispatchNextAttempt`, the re-review debounce's `insertAutoRetriggerTurn` and the composition review -- so every path the row lists reaches it, and `TestEveryTurnInsertArmsTheDispatchTimer` fails for a `CreateTurnParams` value built anywhere in non-test code except as a literal handed to it or to `CreateLockedTurn`, and for a Go string literal inserting into `turns`; `TestNoOtherSQLInsertsTurns` fails for any sqlc query but `CreateTurn`, or any migration, inserting into `turns` (SQL assembled at run time from pieces stays unseen); the plain `Create` stays for test fixtures. The composition review gains its transaction through `postgres.LockedTurnCreator.CreateLockedTurn`: the session's actor-epoch row lock, the turn and the timer, one commit; `releaseCompositionDispatcher` stays as the fast path. The handler is `handleEnsureDispatched`, and every dispatch evaluation, whatever asked for it, deletes the timer first in `planDispatch`'s own transaction, so after a trigger that succeeded the row is gone within that evaluation and the status settles (`TestDispatchTimer_AWriterQueuedBehindAnEvaluationKeepsItsTimer` pins that the delete runs first, inside the evaluation's own transaction under the actor-epoch lock); an evaluation that fails rolls the delete back and the actor backs the timer off in a transaction of its own, to the database's now plus the timer's age since its first arm, held between `DispatchRetryBackoff` (1 min, above `TimerClaimDuration`) and `DispatchRetryBackoffMax` (1 h, above `ActorIdleTTL`), `armed_at` untouched, and only while the row carries the `armed_at` the evaluation read when it deleted it (`DeleteSessionDispatchTimer ... RETURNING armed_at`), so a turn re-arming the timer in between wins (`TestDispatchTimer_ABackoffNeverPostponesAReArm`); and a spawn refused on policy -- a repo the cohort rollout does not admit, an environment the provider cannot honor -- no longer fails the evaluation: `endTurnsOnSpawnRefusal` ends every open turn of the session forward in its transaction (`TriggerAbandon` from pending or dispatched, `TriggerTimeout` from processing, `OnTurnRefused`, one synthetic `execution_complete` each with the reason text the turn-dispatch-time refusal writes, each turn's `enqueueOutboxNotification` -- the Slack or Linear failure notice, a review attempt's check closed as not assessed naming `rollout_not_enrolled` or `substrate_unsupported` -- and one session warning naming the refusal and the remedy), so it is logged, told and counted once and the actor idles out; the turn-dispatch-time path that ends a turn already processing (`failDispatchedTurn`, a rollout refusal on a live sandbox or a prompt the commander could not deliver) now tells the turn's channel the same way (`rollout_not_enrolled`, or `prompt_not_delivered` on a review attempt's check), the refusal records the same warning, and it reaches the workflow engine through `OnTurnRefused`, so a step's blocked self edge no longer queues the refused step again for the dispatch timer to bring back; and a prompt the commander could not deliver backs the dispatch timer off in the transaction that ends its turn (`backOffAfterUndeliveredPrompt`, the chain's first arm carried from the timer the evaluation deleted), so a step's blocked self edge re-queues it at the doubling backoff -- at most about 4 attempts before a sandbox with no live connection reads failed -- not at every pump tick (`TestUndeliveredPrompt_AWorkflowRetryIsBackedOffNotLooped`); `planDispatch` was already idempotent -- a turn in flight on its sandbox's current gen is never re-sent, a sandbox still spawning is left to `EvaluateSpawnDecision`'s skip -- so a late firing sends nothing. `ClassifyTimer` puts `dispatch` with `turn_deadline` and `stop`, a turn in flight. No migration: a rollback leaves a `dispatch` row to Step 222's bound, and the kind is armed and re-armed due at once, a lead of zero, far under `UnknownTimerDeleteAfter`. A pump that cannot deliver the timer (`actor_elsewhere`, `actor_unavailable`) leaves it claimed, never dropped, and the replica that can host the actor delivers it once the claim lapses; each lapsed claim is a fresh race between the replicas' pumps, so a non-hosting replica that wins it again costs one more claim window -- the pump's standing trade-off, unchanged; the tests show the single lapse a hosting replica wins, not a bound. The workflow engine's `Deps.Turns` must be bound to its caller's transaction, as every production caller's already was. Exit tests: `test/resilience/dispatch_trigger_test.go` (createTurnLocked and session creation, each trigger failing, two registries, the dead replica, dispatched once, the status settling), `internal/app/sessionactor/dispatchtimer_integration_test.go`, `internal/app/sessionactor/spawnrefusal_integration_test.go` (the refusal ended once, the backoff), `internal/app/releasereview/compositiondispatch_integration_test.go`, `internal/adapters/outbound/postgres/turn_dispatch_integration_test.go` | §2, §3.3, §43.20 |
| 224 | the Postgres versions a deployment may run are stated nowhere (shipped) | No document names them: not the production checklist, not the deploy templates, not §1's stack choices, which name `pgx/v5` and `sqlc` and no server. The code already depends on versions. `WITH ... AS MATERIALIZED` (`internal/adapters/outbound/postgres/queries/events.sql`) does not parse before Postgres 12; Step 216's orphan terminate needs `pg_terminate_backend(pid, timeout)`, from 14, and only signals the backend below it (`internal/app/sessionactor/lockholder.go`); boot's connection budget reads `reserved_connections`, from 16, as zero where it does not exist (`controlplane/dbbudget.go`), and the checklist's item 10 says "on Postgres 16 or later". Every Postgres-backed test runs on one version: each testcontainers site pins `postgres:17-alpine`, as does `docker-compose.dev.yml`, and the query plans the migrations record were measured on 17. So the fallbacks for 14 and 16 are exercised only through their own seams, a server older than 12 fails at the first query that needs it rather than at boot, and an operator choosing a managed Postgres has nothing to choose against. Step 216 shipped correctly without it: its fallback is the first place the code admits an older server, and the question it leaves open is which older servers are supported at all. State the floor in the production checklist and in §1; check it at boot (`server_version_num`, one query), refusing a server below it with both versions named; and run the Postgres-backed suites on the floor as well as on 17, or state that only 17 is tested. Exit: the floor is stated where an operator reads it and matches what boot refuses; a control plane pointed at an older server refuses at boot naming both versions; CI runs the Postgres-backed suites on the floor, or the checklist says it does not. **Shipped:** the floor is Postgres 16, one constant, `platform.MinPostgresServerVersionNum` (160000), taken as a default rather than measured: boot's connection budget reads `reserved_connections`, and 14 reaches the end of its support in November 2026; no document named a floor before it, item 10 of the checklist reading only "and, on Postgres 16 or later, `SHOW reserved_connections;`", a condition that allowed an older server. 17 stays the tested version. Every subcommand reads `current_setting('server_version_num')` first on its pool, bounded by `PostgresVersionCheckTimeout` (15 s), and refuses an older server with both versions named ("the Postgres server runs version 15.13, and this control plane needs 16.0 or later"): `serve` before the connection budget and the migrations, `seed` before the migrations, and `routes` before `Build`, which reads the database (`CountSuppressedRepos`) and, in a binary composed with extension modules, applies each module's own migrations (`applyModuleMigrations`) -- so `routes` now needs a reachable database of a supported version, as the image check already gives it. A source test finds every migration entry point (the functions that call golang-migrate: `applyMigrations`, `applyModuleMigrations`; no file outside `controlplane` imports it) and fails on any function that reaches one, directly or through another, without the check earlier in its own body; `Build` is its one exception, whose contract is that its caller checked first, and `serve`, `seed` and `routes` must each check. Both fallbacks went, since boot refuses those servers before either runs: the lock connection's terminate that only signalled a lost connection's backend below 14, and the budget's read of `reserved_connections` as 0 where it does not exist. The checklist states the floor as item 13, §1 and §5.1 state it, and the deploy secret names it beside the database URL, each only as "Postgres N or later"; `internal/ops` fails when one of them names another number there, when one words a Postgres version another way it recognises (`N or newer`, `at least N`, `version N`, a bare `Postgres N`, and the like; quotations and "tested on Postgres N" aside), or when the floor job's image names another major. CI's `test-integration-postgres-floor` job runs, on `postgres:16-alpine` through `NARVI_TEST_POSTGRES_IMAGE`, the postgres adapter's whole suite, the lock connection's tests and a boot test that confirms the server is the major its image names, passes the check, applies every migration and reads the budget; every other suite runs on 17 only, and the checklist says so. Lowering the floor means restoring the fallbacks and running those suites on the lower version first | §1, §5.1 |
| 225 | six tests fail intermittently on `main`, by cause (shipped) | Each has failed intermittently on `main`. One cause is reproduced; the others rest on what the test's own code and its recorded failure show, and each cause below is one that recorded symptom allows. The rule of Steps 118 and 129 holds -- no timing test is stabilised on a single data point. By test: `TestRun_ServicesRunConcurrently` (`internal/sandboxagent/services`) is a wall-clock upper bound over real spawned processes: three `python3 -c` services, run through `/bin/sh -c` with no script written, listen after 0.4, 0.9 and 1.4 s, and `Run` must return in under their 2.7 s sum, so interpreter start-up and the readiness poll on a loaded runner eat the margin. Its recorded failure is that shape ("took 2.206698958s, want well under the sequential sum 2s", before the margin was widened, its own comment says). `TestIndexLineBoundary_WriteNewlineOnlyLinesScalesLinearly` (`internal/sandboxagent/boot`) is a ratio of in-process wall-clock timings: it fails only when both of its 2x steps exceed 3x, which absorbs one unlucky sample, but a load that rises during the measurement slows each larger size more than the one before it and moves both steps together; the failures its comment records are of its earlier single-bound and single-ratio forms, both timing. `TestRunHooks_SuccessContinuesToLaterHooksAndRepos` (the same package) is not a timeout: it already passes its own 5-second ceiling (production's is `HookTimeout`, 10 minutes), and its two recorded CI failures (runs 36492023572, first attempt, and 32232334323) took 0.01 s and 0.00 s, each an absent `marker-a-setup`. The cause is Go's fork/exec race over a freshly written script, which `docker_test.go`'s `waitExecutable` comment already records for this package: a concurrent test's fork inherits the write descriptor of a script just written, and exec'ing that script fails with `ETXTBSY`. Seven of `hooks_test.go`'s nine parallel tests (all but the two that write no script) and `runboot_test.go`'s four write their scripts with `writeScript` and exec them with no guard, and the fork that inherits the descriptor can come from any parallel test in the package's test binary -- the docker tests spawn processes and the dependency-ladder tests exec git -- so the window can only be closed at the writer. Fresh mode logs a failed spawn and carries on, so here the race surfaces as a missing marker; its fatal-mode siblings have failed with the error itself (`fork/exec .../repo-a/setup.sh: text file busy`). Reproduced on Linux in this row's review, with the spawn error printed, and gone in 800 runs once `writeScript` wrote under `syscall.ForkLock`. `TestCloudIdentityTokenReachesRealSpawnedHook` and `TestOIDCClusterBindingTokenReachesRealSpawnedHook` (`cmd/sandbox-agent`) are Step 131's pair, still failing after that Step left their 5-second ceiling as it was. Their recorded failures were timeouts at that ceiling (5.01 s, the one timed): the budget starts after the spawn returns and is spent waiting on a reaper goroutine the scheduler can starve under `-race`, a scheduler dependence no bigger number bounds. The race above would not look like that: they run in build mode, where a failed spawn is an immediate error. `TestPushOneRepo_MaliciousInputsRejectedBeforeSpawn` (`cmd/sandbox-agent/push_test.go`) is a wall-clock bound too, and a tighter one: it times one real `git --version`, once, as its baseline and fails any rejection that takes half of it or more, so one fast baseline sample, or one GC or scheduler pause under `-race` inside a rejection, fails it. Its recorded failure on `main` (run 36640372589, first attempt) is that shape: the `malicious_remote_(argument_injection)` subtest took 3.59 ms against a 1.815 ms baseline, and the marker files that prove no git ran stayed absent. Timing stands in for a spawn count only because `supervisor.Supervisor` exposes none to assert against, as the test's own comment says. Three shapes, then -- a wall-clock bound (two tests), a fork/exec race over a script written a moment before, a result that rides on goroutine scheduling -- and each wants its own remedy: readiness proved by order rather than by elapsed time; for the push test, an assertion on the spawn itself (a spawn count the supervisor exposes to tests, or a rejection error typed so the test can tell it from a git failure) in place of the timing, or, while timing stays, the rejection timed as the minimum of several runs (a pause only ever slows a sample) against a baseline that is not a single sample; a scaling proof that does not ride on wall-clock (an operation count, or a benchmark outside the `-race` run); a guard in `writeScript` -- writing under `syscall.ForkLock`'s read side, or every script before `t.Parallel()` -- and not `waitExecutable` as it stands, which probes by executing the script and would run the hook body before `RunHooks` does; and for the spawned-hook pair the scheduling isolation or retry-on-timeout Step 131 named. Record each failure's output when it occurs. Exit: each of the six is changed on a stated cause or kept with a recorded reason; the hooks test's guard is shown by a Linux run that fails without it and passes with it; none fails in 20 consecutive full `-race` runs of its package under the CI runner's load | — |
| 226 | a prompt lost between its dispatch and the sandbox holds its turn until the deadline (shipped) | `tryPlanDispatch` (`internal/app/sessionactor/dispatch.go`) commits the turn Pending → Dispatched → Processing, stamps `dispatched_sandbox_gen` and arms `turn_deadline`; only then, outside the transaction, does `executeDispatch` write the `prompt` command, through `SandboxRegistry.SendCommand` (`internal/adapters/inbound/wshub/commander.go`), one `conn.Write` with no receipt. The command carries no `ackId`: the ack protocol (§6.1) covers the agent's six critical events, never a command. So a control plane that dies between that commit and the write, or a frame lost with its socket after a write that returned, leaves a turn Processing on a sandbox that never received it. The sandbox reconnects on the same gen and nothing on that gen notices: its `ready` moves no Ready sandbox, heartbeats carry no busy or idle state, `planReenqueueOrRespawn` re-sends only when the gen has changed (`turn.NeedsReenqueue`), and `inactivity` defers while a turn is Processing. Nothing automatic ends it but `turn_deadline`, 60 minutes on (`TurnDeadline`), as a timeout; a person's stop also ends it (Step 217: the agent has nothing to abort, so the `stop` timer cancels the turn once `StopGrace` runs out and retires the gen). Meanwhile the session reads running and its next turns wait. Reproduced in this group's own review, with the real actor on Postgres: replica A dispatches and its send is lost, replica B hydrates, the sandbox sends a same-gen `ready` and three heartbeats, dispatch is evaluated again -- no prompt is re-sent, the turn stays Processing, and `turn_deadline` is an hour out. Step 21 shipped correctly without it and said so where the gap sits: `tryPlanDispatch`'s own comment, written by that Step, says its scope "has no separate 'the sandbox acknowledged receipt' signal to gate the second transition on" and that "'we successfully sent it' is treated as sufficient for both" -- the Dispatched → Processing transition a receipt would gate. Its kill-pod test meets §9.3 scenario 1's "fails-with-reason" only through `turn_deadline`, and Step 28's re-enqueue re-sends only across a changed gen and says a same-gen turn in flight must never be re-sent, which is right for a prompt the sandbox holds. What is missing is receipt: the control plane cannot tell a prompt the sandbox holds from one it never got. Two facts constrain any fix. The capability has to be advertised by the agent, never inferred from its build: every agent reports `agentVersion` "dev" (nothing stamps `NARVI_AGENT_VERSION`, as the `ready` schema's own description says), and agents older than the change keep running from snapshots and repo images, the reason the boot-evidence rule (§3.2, `internal/app/sessionactor/bootevidence.go`) tells agent generations apart "without trusting the agent's build". And the control plane is itself mixed during a rolling deploy: today's version stores and acknowledges an event type it does not know (`peekAckID` reads no type), so a receipt can be swallowed by a replica that cannot use it, and a turn one replica dispatched can be judged by another. The design is the Step's; it must meet these requirements. (a) A prompt is re-sent only for a turn whose own dispatch asked for a receipt, recorded in that dispatch's commit. (b) The agent answers every prompt that asks for a receipt with a receipt, a duplicate included, and runs a `messageId` at most once. (c) The capability counts only for the gen whose `ready` advertised it, stored against that gen as `sandboxes.boot_evidence_gen` is, so no respawn, restore or resume inherits it. (d) A turn a person's stop flagged is never re-sent, the check `planReenqueueOrRespawn` already makes on `StopRequestedAt`. (e) A receipt stored by a replica that does not know it must not lead a newer replica to fail or re-send the turn. Whatever the contract gains is additive, MINOR under `contracts/COMPATIBILITY.md`. Exit, as named tests: an agent without the capability is never re-sent a prompt and never runs one twice, its lost prompt still ending at `turn_deadline`; a capable agent's lost prompt -- the dispatching replica killed after its commit, or the frame dropped -- is delivered once more after the same-gen reconnect and the turn completes; with mixed replicas, in both orders -- dispatched on an older replica with the sandbox then reconnecting to a newer one, and dispatched on a newer replica with an older one then storing the receipt -- the turn completes and is never failed; a capable gen followed by a restore from a snapshot taken before the change re-sends nothing and runs nothing twice; a stop-flagged turn whose prompt is unacknowledged is never re-sent **Shipped:** the receipt is data, never a transition: `tryPlanDispatch` still commits Dispatched → Processing with the dispatch, and nothing is added to the turn's transition table. The contract gains three MINOR additions (contracts 1.16.0): `ready.capabilities.promptReceipt`, `prompt.receiptRequested` (no `default`) and the event `prompt_received` (`promptMessageId`, `duplicate`, and the deterministic `messageId` `prompt_received:{promptMessageId}`; not critical, no `ackId`). Migration 000155 adds `turns.receipt_requested_message_id`, `receipt_requested_at` and `receipt_checked_ready_seq`, and `sandboxes.prompt_receipt_gen` and `ready_seq` (`NOT NULL DEFAULT 0`), all catalog-only, with a guarded down; the previous binary works with them present and cannot boot on 000155 until it is migrated down or forced back to 154. `handleSandboxEvent` records every ready of the live gen (`RecordSandboxReady`: `ready_seq + 1`, and `prompt_receipt_gen` set to the gen when the ready advertised the capability and cleared when it did not -- the latest ready decides), stored against the gen as `boot_evidence_gen` is (c). `tryPlanDispatch` and `tryPlanReenqueue` record, in the dispatch's own commit, which `messageId` asked, when on the database's clock, and at which ready (`SetTurnPromptReceiptRequest`), only for a capable gen, and the prompt asks only then, from the same value; any other dispatch records NULL, so a dispatch by a binary without receipts never matches the request it leaves behind (a). `planReenqueueOrRespawn`'s same-gen branch calls `tryPlanReceiptResend` (`internal/app/sessionactor/promptreceipt.go`), which reads nothing more unless `turn.PromptReconnectToAnswer` holds -- Processing, its own dispatch asked, unflagged by a stop (d), the gen capable and owing no stop its retirement, and a ready counted since the last answered one -- then reads the stored receipt by its key and the request's age in one statement (`GetTurnPromptReceiptState`), claims the ready with a compare-and-set (`MarkTurnPromptReconnectAnswered`), and acts on `turn.DecidePromptResend`: a stored receipt -- whichever binary stored it, through the same generic path (e) -- sends nothing; past `PromptResendWindow` nothing is sent, one WARN is logged and `turn_prompt_resend_total{outcome="window_expired"}` counts it; otherwise `executeReceiptResend` sends the original prompt, same `messageId`, re-arming nothing and moving none of `dispatched_at`, `dispatched_event_id`, `dispatched_sandbox_gen`. A re-send that fails or that the rollout re-check refuses fails nothing and is counted (`send_failed`, `refused`). `PromptResendWindow` (10 min, `internal/platform/timeouts.go`) is kept below `TurnDeadline` and above `ActorLockServerReapTime + SandboxWSReconnectMaxBackoff + ActorHydrateTimeout`, each by `MinTimeoutMargin`. The agent (b): `wsbridge.Bridge.EnablePromptReceipts` opens a journal under `NARVI_AGENT_STATE_DIR` (default `/tmp/narvi-agent-state`, outside the workspace, a directory of its own uid writable by no one else), `prompts-<sessionId>-<gen>.log`, one JSON string per line, fsynced per append; with it open every ready advertises the capability, every prompt is journaled before anything else, every prompt that asked is answered with a `prompt_received` (a duplicate with `duplicate` set), and a `messageId` runs at most once, across a restart of the agent process within the gen; a requested prompt whose append fails is neither run nor receipted, and a journal that cannot be opened leaves the bridge as it was. Exit, as named tests: `TestResilience_Scenario22_LostPromptFrame_PreChangeAgent_NeverResent_EndsAtTurnDeadline`, `TestResilience_KillPodAfterDispatchCommit_IncapableAgent_NotResent_EndsAtTurnDeadline` and `TestPromptReceipt_IncapableGen_DispatchRequestsNothing` (an agent without the capability); `TestResilience_KillPodAfterDispatchCommit_CapableAgent_PromptResentOnSameGenReconnect`, `TestResilience_Scenario22_LostPromptFrame_CapableAgent_DeliveredOnceAndCompletes` and `TestResilience_Scenario22_LostReceiptFrame_CapableAgent_RunsOnce` (a capable agent's lost prompt, replica killed or frame dropped, and its lost receipt); `TestPromptReceipt_MixedReplicas_DispatchedByPreviousBinary_CompletesNeverFailed` and `TestPromptReceipt_MixedReplicas_ReceiptStoredByPreviousBinary_CompletesNeverFailed` (both deploy orders); `TestPromptReceipt_RestoreFromPreChangeSnapshot_NoResendNoDoubleRun`; `TestPromptReceipt_StopFlaggedUnacknowledged_NeverResent`. Its review found two more things the re-send leans on, both fixed here. A prompt frame over 32 KiB was lost on `main` already: the agent kept the WebSocket library's default read limit, and such a frame closed its connection; with receipts every reconnect re-sent it, to be lost again, for the whole window. The agent now reads up to `platform.MaxPromptFrameBytes` (32 MiB: a review's diff is capped at 4 MiB, at most six times that once JSON-escaped, plus room for the rest of the prompt), and the session actor never writes a longer prompt frame -- a first dispatch over it fails its turn as a refusal naming both sizes, its synthetic `execution_complete` marked `"delivered": false` since nothing was written, and a re-send over it is refused and counted (`frame_too_large`). An agent in an older snapshot or repo image keeps the 32 KiB default: it still loses a larger prompt, does not advertise the capability, and its turn ends at `turn_deadline`, as this row's exit accepts. And a loss that recurs is capped: `PromptResendMaxPerTurn` (3, beside the window, `Validate` keeps it positive) bounds the re-sends of one dispatch, counted in `turns.receipt_resend_count` by the same compare-and-set that claims each reconnect; past it one WARN and `cap_reached`. The journal keeps complete lines only -- a torn tail is cut off at open, a failed append cut back off -- and a prompt that asked and could not be journaled now breaks the journal: the agent stops advertising the capability, reports the prompt in a non-fatal `error` event, and ends its connection, so the next `ready` records the gen as incapable. Further exit tests: `TestResilience_Scenario22_PromptFrameOver32KiB_CapableAgent_DeliveredOnceAndCompletes`, `TestResilience_Scenario22_PromptLostOnEveryDelivery_ResendCapStopsTheLoop`, `TestPromptReceipt_ResendCapStopsADeterministicLoss`, `TestPromptReceipt_PromptFrameOverTheLimit_RefusedNeverSent`. §9.3 gains scenario 22, gating this Step. | §3.3, §6.1, §9.3 |
| 227 | intermittent tests seen while fixing six others (shipped) | Step 225's own runs saw three more tests fail intermittently, none of them among its six. Each failure is recorded here, and none was changed on it: the rule of Steps 118 and 129 holds. By test: `TestRun_PrimaryCrashIsFatal` (`internal/sandboxagent/services`) and `TestRunBoot_FatalFailureInRepoAStopsBeforeRepoB` (`internal/sandboxagent/boot`) give a primary service that runs `exit 1` a readiness port from `freePort`, which binds a port, reads it back and closes it. That is Step 118's race, on the crash path its fix never covered: `neverBoundPort` serves only the two timeout tests, though `TestRunBoot_ChownsBeforeStartingServices` already passes port 1 to the same `crashingManifestYAML`. Neither test can see `Run` or `RunBoot` return nil any other way: `waitReady` reports `PhaseReady` only after a readiness dial succeeds, and every other outcome of a primary is fatal, so each failure needs something listening on the port `freePort` released. Recorded failures: `run_test.go:344: Run() error = nil, want a fatal error (primary service crashed before ready)` (CI run 36802613945, the temporary soak job, 1 of 20 runs) and `runboot_test.go:248: RunBoot(, nil) error = nil, want a fatal error (repo-a's primary service crashed)` (1 of 300, in a `--network none` Linux container with no python3, where the only loopback listeners were the tests' own short-lived `freePort` sockets, so that occurrence's listener is not identified). Step 225's review planted a listener on the released port and reproduced the `RunBoot` failure 3 of 3. `TestRun_PrimaryCleanExitIsAlsoFatal` and `TestRun_MixedOutcomes_OneCrashesOneSucceeds` give their exiting primary a `freePort` port too. `TestUnconditionalReaping` (`internal/sandboxagent/supervisor`) is a wall-clock bound over a real spawned process: it sleeps 200 ms, expecting `/bin/sh -c "exit 3"` to have run and been reaped by then, and then requires `Wait` to return in under 50 ms inside a 100 ms context. Nothing bounds the shell's start-up, or when the reaper goroutine runs under `-race` on a loaded machine. Recorded failures: `supervisor_test.go:465: Wait() error = context deadline exceeded, want the already-reaped result` (1 of 20 in Step 225's loaded local soak); the review reproduced both of its shapes under CPU load, 2 of 200 (line 465, and line 471's `Wait() took ..., want near-instant`), and again 1 of 100. A fourth failed on `main` after Step 225 merged and is added here: `TestEvaluateCronTriggersOnce_NeverFiresTwiceInTheSameMinute` (`internal/app/automation`, an integration test) creates an every-minute cron automation and calls `EvaluateCronTriggersOnce` twice, back to back, asserting one invocation. That function reads `time.Now()` itself, with no clock to inject, so two ticks that straddle a minute boundary fall in two minutes, where the schedule rightly fires twice. Recorded failure: `triggerandextras_integration_test.go:154: invocations for automation = 2, want exactly 1 across two ticks in the same minute` (CI run 36810256624 on `main` at 39458ba, test-integration group 4, first attempt; the test took 0.20 s, between log lines stamped 03:35:59 and 03:36:00 UTC). Each wants its own remedy: for the crash-path pair, a readiness port nothing can bind, as Step 118 gave the timeout tests, or a listener the test itself holds; for the reaping test, a poll of `Process.Exited()`, which never reaps, until it reports true under a deadline that only detects a hang; for the cron test, its two ticks placed in one minute by construction, through a seam that evaluates a tick at an instant the test chooses. Record each failure's output when it occurs. Exit: each of the four is changed on that cause or kept with a recorded reason; none fails in 20 consecutive full `-race` runs of its package under the CI runner's load | — |
| 228 | an agent event over 32 KiB wedges its sandbox's socket, and an older agent still loses a larger prompt in silence (shipped) | The sandbox socket is bounded in one direction only. Step 226 made the agent read up to `platform.MaxPromptFrameBytes` (`internal/platform/wirelimits.go`; `runConnection`, `internal/sandboxagent/wsbridge/run.go`) and the session actor measure every prompt against it; the control plane's own read was left at coder/websocket's default, 32768 bytes per message: `NewSandboxHandler` (`internal/adapters/inbound/wshub/sandbox.go`) accepts with `AcceptOptions{InsecureSkipVerify: true}` and never calls `SetReadLimit`, and nothing bounds what an agent writes. Agent events over 32 KiB are ordinary. A `token` frame carries its part's whole text so far, and the pinned runtime sends two per text part, empty when it opens and whole when it closes (§6.1), so a text part over 32 KiB closes on a frame over it; a `tool_call` carries the tool's whole input, a file the agent writes among them, and a `tool_result` the tool's whole output (`translateToken`, `translateToolCall`, `translateToolResult`, `internal/adapters/outbound/opencode/translate.go`); and `push_error`, a critical event, carries `git push`'s whole stderr (`pushOneRepo`, `cmd/sandbox-agent/main.go`), the one critical event whose text nothing bounds. Reading such a frame fails with `ErrMessageTooBig`, and the library closes the connection with `StatusMessageTooBig`; `readLoop` (`wshub/dispatch.go`) returns on any read error without logging it. The agent redials at once, with no backoff, since an established connection was lost (`Run`), sends `ready` -- and, once its boot has completed, a heartbeat carrying the boot's start phase -- then replays its whole buffer in order, the frame included: a critical entry leaves the buffer only when it is acked, and a best-effort one only by eviction, oldest first and only once the buffer holds 1000 entries, and an idle runtime adds none (`wsbridge/buffer.go`, §6.1). So the frame closes every reconnect, and nothing queued behind it arrives. Every reconnect costs the control plane one actor transaction for each frame it reads before the frame -- the `ready`, the heartbeat, and every replayed entry ahead of it, up to the buffer's 1000 best-effort ones -- each a sandbox read, an insert or a dedupe, and the `last_seen_at` update `handleSandboxEvent` makes whether or not the event was new; and it stores the `ready`, and on a booted sandbox the heartbeat, as new rows, so the event log grows for as long as the loop runs. Reproduced for this filing on the real wire, a real `wsbridge.Bridge` against the real wshub handler and Postgres, with nothing buffered ahead of the frame, a small `execution_complete` sent behind it, and each frame under a `messageId` of its own (the wedge happens at the read, before storage, whatever the id): a 40 KiB `push_error` and a 40 KiB `tool_result` each took `sandboxes.ready_seq` from 1 to over 940 in 3 seconds and over 1,940 in 6, about 320 reconnects a second, with neither the frame nor the `execution_complete` stored and the agent logging `StatusMessageTooBig` ("read limited at 32769 bytes") on every reconnect; a 30 KiB `tool_result` was stored once, on one connection, with the `execution_complete` behind it. A sandbox a few turns in has its buffer near full, and this row's review measured that case on the same rig: with 500 entries buffered ahead of the frame, about one reconnect a second, and with 999, one every two seconds, while Postgres committed between about 700 and 1,200 write transactions a second in every case, the empty buffer's included. What a person sees depends on the frame. A `token`, `tool_call` or `tool_result` frame holds its turn running until `turn_deadline`, 60 minutes on, then a timeout; a text part over 32 KiB reads blank, since its one stored frame is the empty one it opened with (after this Step it reads cut, ending in the marker below). A `push_error` comes after its turn has completed, since the push is sent once that turn's `execution_complete` has committed (`sendPushBestEffort`, `internal/app/sessionactor/pushpr.go`): no pull request opens, no push failure is reported, the session reads unsettled until `MCPStatusDeliveryWindow` (10 minutes) lapses, and the next turn runs until `turn_deadline`. Either way the sandbox rail adds "agent connected" for every `ready`, with no warning and no error on the session. Every `ready` bumps `last_seen_at`, which both `liveness_check` and `inactivity` read (`timerfired.go`), so the sandbox never turns Suspect and is never reclaimed as idle: the loop runs until a person stops the session or the sandbox's own lifetime ends it, and every later turn's events queue behind the same frame. The control plane logs one `wshub: sandbox ws connected` per reconnect; the cause is only in the agent's own log, inside the sandbox. The prompt direction keeps the matching residue: an agent built before 226 -- in an older snapshot or repo image, or a sandbox alive at its deploy -- keeps the 32 KiB default, so a prompt over it, a review's with its diff inlined among them, is lost, and its turn ends at `turn_deadline` with nothing naming the cause. 226's exit accepts that, and nothing schedules a remedy. Step 226 shipped correctly without either: its review found the control plane's read limit and its PR named it a follow-up rather than widen the Step, and its row, `wirelimits.go` and §6.1 scope the bound to prompts and to agents built from its release. The remedy: each side states the largest frame it reads, neither writes past what the other states, and a peer that states nothing is held to the library's default. One file, `internal/platform/wirelimits.go`, holds every bound: `MaxPromptFrameBytes`, unchanged; `MaxEventFrameBytes`, the largest agent event the control plane reads, 1 MiB, with its derivation in its comment -- from what the agent can emit (a text part or a tool's input is bounded by the model's output cap, a tool's output by the runtime's own truncation, each up to six times larger once JSON-escaped) and from what the control plane holds at once (one read per sandbox connection, up to `hubConnBufferSize` broadcast payloads per browser connection, and a history page, bounded below): 1 MiB covers a text part at a 64K-token cap three times over once ordinarily escaped, cuts the six-fold worst case visibly rather than losing it, and holds the agent's buffer to 1000 MiB at worst and a slow browser connection to 64 MiB, where sizing it as `MaxPromptFrameBytes` was, to cover that worst case, would have taken 4 MiB, a 4 GiB buffer and 256 MiB; the pinned runtime's own tool-output truncation is recorded nowhere in this repository, which the comment says, so nothing bounds a tool's output but the cut below; and `DefaultFrameReadLimitBytes` (32768), the library's default, pinned by a test that reads one byte past it on a fresh connection. **Control plane.** `NewSandboxHandler` sets a response header, `X-Max-Frame-Bytes`, to `MaxEventFrameBytes` before `websocket.Accept` and calls `SetReadLimit(MaxEventFrameBytes)` after it, and `readLoop` logs a read that fails with `ErrMessageTooBig` at WARN, with the limit. An agent built before this Step needs no change for any event up to that bound; one that sends more still closes every reconnect, now logged. A history page gets a byte budget, as the subscribe reply has one (`maxInitialReplayBytes`): `handleFetchHistory` (`wshub/client.go`) and its REST twin (`internal/adapters/inbound/httpapi/events.go`, whose `eventsMaxLimit` is also 500) stop a page at `FetchHistoryMaxReplyBytes`, 2 MiB, a new constant in `wirelimits.go` since both read it, and no lower than `MaxEventFrameBytes`, always keeping one event and setting `nextCursor` after the last one sent, so a raised read limit can never make one page 500 × `MaxEventFrameBytes`, read or sent: the budget bounds the read, not only the reply -- Postgres measures each event as the page walks the log and the control plane reads only the events that fit (`EventStore.ListPageForSession`), so a page holds what it sends and paging reads each event once -- and the subscribe replay reads its events the same way, under `maxInitialReplayBytes`. A prompt's bound is its gen's: `ready.capabilities` gains `maxFrameBytes`, the agent's read limit, recorded against the gen as `prompt_receipt_gen` is (the latest ready decides), in `sandboxes.agent_max_frame_bytes` and `agent_max_frame_bytes_gen` (migration 000157), which `RecordSandboxReady` writes and a dispatch reads from the sandbox row it builds the prompt from (`promptFrameBound`, `internal/app/sessionactor/framebound.go`); the stated value is clamped to `MaxPromptFrameBytes`, and a ready a replica built before 000157 records leaves both columns as they are, so a gen whose every ready such a replica recorded falls to the `promptReceipt` rule below. A gen whose latest ready stated none is held to `MaxPromptFrameBytes` when it advertised `promptReceipt` -- every agent that advertises it was built with that read limit, since 226 shipped both -- and to `DefaultFrameReadLimitBytes` otherwise. `executeDispatch`'s existing check measures against that bound: a prompt over it is never written, and its turn fails as one over `MaxPromptFrameBytes` already does (`OnTurnRefused`, a session warning naming both sizes and that the sandbox's agent reads no more, the synthetic `execution_complete` marked `"delivered": false`, `prompt_not_delivered` on a review attempt's check), at dispatch instead of at `turn_deadline`; `executeReceiptResend` refuses a re-send over it as `frame_too_large`. A 226 agent whose journal did not open advertises neither, so a prompt over 32 KiB that it could have read is refused, and named. **Agent.** The header bounds what the agent writes, never what it reads: `runConnection` keeps reading up to `MaxPromptFrameBytes`, reads the header from the dial's response, and bounds what it writes on that connection by it, or by `DefaultFrameReadLimitBytes` when it is absent -- a control plane built before this Step, during a rolling deploy or after a rollback, or a proxy that dropped the header. An entry stays whole in the buffer, and each write of it, live (`bestEffortSend`) or replayed (`flushBuffer`), is cut to that connection's bound when it exceeds it, in its own place in the order, so a later connection with a larger bound writes it whole; a best-effort event over `MaxEventFrameBytes` is cut to it once, before it is buffered, recording the string it cut so every later cut of the entry keeps its total, and one no cut fits is refused with the warning below, so no buffered entry is larger and the buffer's 1000 best-effort entries hold at most 1000 MiB, where nothing bounded an entry before. Only `token`, `tool_call` and `tool_result` are cut, and the cut shortens one string -- a `token`'s text, a `tool_result`'s output, the longest string in a `tool_call`'s input -- at a UTF-8 boundary, ends it with a line for people, `[text cut at <kept> of <total> bytes on its way from the sandbox]`, and records it for machines in an added property, `cut: {kept, total}`, the bytes of that string kept and its whole length; every control plane stores the raw payload, so `cut` survives a binary that does not know it. Any other best-effort frame over the bound, or one still over it once cut, is not written, and a `warning` naming its type, `messageId`, size and the bound goes out once per buffered entry -- not per `messageId`, which a `tool_call` and its `tool_result` share -- and the replay goes on to the entries behind it. Writing nothing in a `token` frame's place was rejected, and that is the only reason the rule cuts rather than skips: a text part whose closing frame is not written keeps only the empty frame it opened with, and `FinalText` then reads an earlier part as the turn's final text -- the narration before a plan, as the plan. (A `tool_call` or `tool_result` not written would change nothing stored: on `main` each carries its message's id, which that message's `step_start` has already stored, and storage keeps the first row per `(session_id, message_id)`, so neither ever adds a row -- a defect of its own, older than this row, which this row does not fix and Step 229 schedules.) The agent cuts even an entry it already wrote whole on a connection whose bound admitted it, because it cannot know that write was stored: skipping it would leave a binary built before this Step reading the earlier narration whenever it was not, wrong text with nothing to show it, where a cut at least shows its marker. When the whole frame was stored, that binary's `tokenFrameAddsNoRow` admits the cut form as the part's newest row (its marker makes it no prefix of the whole), so during a rollback that binary stores and shows the cut text, marker visible in the text, and lets such a plan be approved. That is accepted for the rollback window, since the older binary cannot be changed after the fact, and the whole text stays in the log, where rolling forward reads it again: this Step's readers let a cut frame yield to a stored frame of the same part only when that frame's text starts with the cut's kept prefix and is exactly `cut.total` bytes long -- the whole text the cut was taken from -- and between two cuts of one text (the same `total`), to the one keeping more; with no such frame, the cut frame is the part's text and the cut is reported. An older frame of the part that shares the kept prefix but is shorter than `total` -- an earlier cumulative frame, from a runtime that sends more than one non-empty frame per part -- never wins, since it is not the final text. The rule holds in `tokenFrameAddsNoRow`, which adds no row for a cut only when it yields to the part's latest or first stored frame -- that whole text, or a cut of it keeping more, two cuts included, by the same rule (`framecut.YieldsTo`) the readers apply -- in `FinalText`, whatever the frames' order, and in the web timeline (`timelineModel.ts`, comparing UTF-8 lengths). One residue does not come back: a plan approved during the window keeps the snapshot it was approved with. The approval writes `ExtractContent`'s text and `ExtractStructured` of it into `plan_documents` in its own transaction (`snapshotApprovedPlanContent`, `decideplan.go`), and `GET /plans` returns a snapshot as it is (`resolvePlanRenderedContent`, `plans.go`), so that plan's record keeps the cut text, its structured steps likely absent since the cut usually takes the `plan-steps` block at the end, and `GET /plans` and `narvi_list_plans` return the marker in the text while reporting no cut, the snapshot carrying no `cut`. This is accepted as a limit of the rollback window, not repaired: the snapshot is the record of what the person approved, which was that cut text, and rewriting it would misstate the approval; and the implementation turn does not read it, since it is sent the fixed `implementPlanPrompt` and works from the plan the agent itself proposed, whole, in its own conversation. `FinalText` and the plan parser learn a cut only from `cut`, never from the text: a marker-shaped line the agent wrote itself is text. `plan.FinalText` returns a cut part's text with its marker and reports the cut; `ExtractStructured` returns nil for cut content without looking for its fence; and a cut plan cannot be approved. `DecidePlanOnTx` (`decideplan.go`) refuses Approve with a new sentinel, `ErrPlanCut`, beside `ErrPlanOpenTurnInFlight`, Reject still open, and every surface relays it with its reason, each in a case of its own as each relays `ErrPlanOpenTurnInFlight` today: a Slack thread reply (`slack/handler.go`) answers with the reason instead of the generic retry text; a Slack button (`slack/interactive.go`) answers with an ephemeral message (`PostEphemeral`, as its not-linked branch does) and leaves the approval message as it is, because `updateMessage` sends a `chat.update` with no blocks (`chatUpdateRequest`, `slackapi/blockkit.go`), which would strip the plan and its Reject and Request-changes buttons from a plan still awaiting approval; the REST approve route (`planapprove.go`) answers `409` with it, and the web plan view shows that reason instead of the fixed "already decided" text it gives every `409` (`PlanModeView.tsx`); Linear (`linear/webhook.go`, which today logs any other decide error and posts nothing) posts it as an agent activity; and MCP's `narvi_approve_plan`, which runs the REST twin, returns it as its tool error (`mapOutcome`). `GET /plans`, the approval notices and the session result state the cut from `FinalText`'s report, never from the text, which a surface may shorten for display (the web plan view at `MAX_CONTENT_CHARS`). No surface offers Approve for a cut plan: the Slack message (`PostPlanApprovalMessage`) carries Request changes and Reject and says the plan was cut; Linear's notice (`planApprovalLinearText`, `internal/app/sessionactor/outboxenqueue.go`), which today always ends by offering the approve keywords, drops them for a cut plan, says it was cut, and points to `revise:` and the reject keywords; the web plan view shows the reason where Approve would be; the replies Slack (`ackPlanAwaitingText`) and Linear (`planAwaitingApprovalReplyText`) give a message that neither decides nor revises an awaiting plan read its cut (`sessionactor.ReadPlanCut`) and, for a cut one, give the reason and point to `revise:` and reject instead of the approve keywords and the Approve button; and the decision inbox shows it in place of "Approve & build" (`DecisionInboxView.tsx`), its plan rows (`buildPlanItems`, `internal/app/decisioninbox/aggregate.go`) carrying `FinalText`'s cut report, read from each awaiting plan's own token window (`EventStore.ListTokenFramesInWindow`, through `sessionactor.ReadPlanFinal`, the read the approval itself makes), so the inbox never offers Approve for a plan the approval would refuse. A cut is settled by the time its notice is posted -- the notice goes out as the turn completes, and no `token` frame adds a row once its turn has ended -- so an Approve button there could only be refused; Request changes, asking for a shorter plan, is the way on. The relays above still matter for a verdict no notice offered: a plain text reply, REST, MCP, or a button on a notice a binary built before this Step posted. No critical frame exceeds `DefaultFrameReadLimitBytes`, so every control plane reads one: `push_error`'s text is capped at 4096 bytes, its head kept, with its own `...[truncated]` marker, as `diagnosticFieldMaxBytes` caps a diagnostic field, and `SendCritical` refuses a critical frame over that default, buffering nothing and sending the same `warning`. The browser's socket needs no limit of its own: the web client is the browser's own `WebSocket` (`web/src/ws/transport.ts`), which sets no per-message limit, and what it sends (`subscribe`, `fetch_history`) is small; what grows with the read limit is the history page, bounded above. Contracts gain `ready.capabilities.maxFrameBytes` (1.20.0), and `cut` on `token`, `tool_call` and `tool_result` (properties added, not required: MINOR under `contracts/COMPATIBILITY.md`), and three REST read-only fields the surfaces state the cut from -- `Plan.cut`, `DecisionInboxItem.planCutKept` and `planCutTotal`, and `SessionOutcome.lastRun.summary.cut`, each required and nullable (MINOR producer-to-client); `cut` and the REST fields shipped as contracts 1.21.0; §6.1 states the header, the cut, its property and its marker. This row's filing corrected §6.1, which said a replay gives the turn back "every frame its sandbox still holds" and stated the frame limit for prompts alone. | 229 | a real `tool_call`, `tool_result` or `step_finish` is never stored | The runtime adapter gives every part-derived event but `token` its assistant message's id as `messageId` (`translateStepStart`, `translateStepFinish`, `translateToolCall`, `translateToolResult`, `internal/adapters/outbound/opencode/translate.go`, whose own comment names `token` as the one exception), and a message's `step-start` part comes before its other parts, so its `step_start` is the first event under that id. `events` keeps one row per `(session_id, message_id)` (migration 000019's unique index; `CreateEvent`'s upsert, `queries/events.sql`, returns the row already there with `inserted` false), and `handleSandboxEvent` stores every type but `token` under its wire `messageId` (`appendRawEvent`, `internal/app/sessionactor/sandboxevent.go`). So every `tool_call`, `tool_result` and `step_finish` of a real turn upserts onto its message's `step_start` row and is never stored, and since `appendRawEvent` queues a broadcast only for a fresh insert (`actor.go`), none reaches a live page either. Reproduced for this filing: the events the real translate path emits for one assistant message -- a `read` call and a `task` call that spawns a sub-task, each with its result, between a `step_start` and a `step_finish` -- sent through a real `wsbridge.Bridge`, the real wshub handler and the real actor on Postgres, left five rows, the `step_start`, two `token` frames, `sub_task_start` and `sub_task_finish`, and none of the two `tool_call`s, the two `tool_result`s or the `step_finish`; the step's cost still reached `turn_step_costs` and `turns.cost_usd`, which key on `stepId`. What it breaks: the Linear progress notice on a turn's first `tool_call`, §8.10's progressive AgentActivity update, returns at `!insertedFresh` and has never fired (`maybeEnqueueLinearProgress`, `progressnotify.go`); the web timeline shows no tool call and no per-step cost, and a step closes only at its turn's `execution_complete` (`timelineModel.ts`); the page's whole cost comes from `step_finish` rows and nothing else (`buildCostRollup`, `web/src/session/costRollup.ts`, the source of both the header's total and the right rail's `CostPanel`, `SessionRail.tsx`), so on every real session the rail's panel reads "—" for the turn, "—" for the session and "0 in · 0 out", and the header, which draws its cost-and-calls span only when a cost or a tool call is known, shows neither (`SessionHeader.tsx`); sub-task lanes never render, since a lane hangs only under its spawning tool call (`attachSubTask`, `timelineModel.ts`; `Timeline.tsx`), and a sub-task whose parent never arrives is dropped when its turn ends, never reaching `orphanedSubTasks`; and the history every reader pages -- `fetch_history`, `GET /api/sessions/{id}/events`, `narvi_get_session_transcript` -- holds no tool activity. What it does not touch: `sub_task_start` and `sub_task_finish` mint fresh ids (`newEventID`), so §26.4's corroboration, which reads only those (`ListSubTaskStartsForTurn`, `reviewverdict.go`), has its trace; `FinalText` and the session result read `token` frames only; §25.15's server-side total, `turns.cost_usd`, keys on `stepId` and is right, but no session-workspace view reads it, the page summing its own from `step_finish` rows; and no digest or analytics query reads these types. The overflow `warning` (`translateCompactionOverflow`) carries its message's id too; whether an earlier event of that message is stored depends on where the runtime puts its compaction part, which this filing did not establish, and the Step checks it against the pinned binary. The defect dates from 2026-07-20, when an audit fix made `(session_id, message_id)` unique (migration 000019), two days after Step 17's adapter gave these events their message's id. Step 93 found it for `step_finish` and shipped correctly around it: its cost write keys on `stepId`, and migration 000099, `turns.sql` and §25.15 state that `step_start` claims the row and every `step_finish` upserts onto it. Step 228's filing named the tool events, and its row says it does not fix them. The tests missed it the way Step 93 recorded: the progress-notice tests and the web fixtures mint a fresh id per event and never send the `step_start` that comes first. Two remedies were weighed. A per-event id the adapter derives from the part, as `token` uses its part id, fixes only agents built after it -- a sandbox from an older snapshot or repo image keeps losing its tool events (§6.1) -- and changes what `messageId` and `sub_task_start.parentMessageId` mean on the wire, for every reader and for a second runtime adapter. A storage key the control plane derives fixes every agent at once and leaves the wire as it is, as `token` frames are already stored under keys of the control plane's making (`tokenframe.go`); that is the remedy. `handleSandboxEvent` stores a `tool_call` under `messageId + "#tool_call:" + callId`, a `tool_result` under `messageId + "#tool_result:" + callId` and a `step_finish` under `messageId + "#step_finish:" + stepId`, one pure function in `internal/domain` deriving the key from the type, the wire `messageId` and the correlator the envelope peeks (`wshub/dispatch.go`); an event without its correlator keeps the bare `messageId`, as today. Every other type keeps its key: changing the key of a type already stored would make a sandbox's replay after the deploy store each such event a second time, the hazard §6.1 records for a `token` part's first frame, and these three have no production rows to collide with -- only tests that minted their own ids ever stored one. What each concern gets: a resend carries the same `callId` or `stepId`, so it still dedupes, on the derived key; the ack protocol is untouched, none of the three being critical; readers take the wire `messageId` from the payload, never from `events.message_id` (as `ContentEvent` already documents for tokens), so nothing that reads a row changes; and under Step 228, a `tool_call` or `tool_result` written cut is stored first and keeps its `cut`, and a whole copy written later dedupes onto it, first wins, like every non-token event. These rows are added only while their turn is live, by `token`'s own rule (§6.1): a turn is Processing and the event's message is that turn's -- its `step_start` row, under the bare `messageId`, lies above the turn's `dispatched_event_id`, or, with no such row, a turn is Processing at all -- so an event replayed after its turn's `execution_complete` never lands at the log's tail, where the timeline would open a new running turn. The readers gain what never arrived, and two pairings the stored events need: the timeline pairs a `step_finish` with its message's `step_start` by their shared wire `messageId`, since their `stepId`s are two different parts' ids; and it hangs a sub-task under the `task` call that spawned it by a correlator that does not depend on order. `parentMessageId` names a message, which may hold several tool calls, and the adapter emits `sub_task_start` before its `tool_call` only when the call's first `running` update already carries the task's `state.metadata`; when the metadata comes on a later update, the `tool_call` goes first, and two parallel `task` calls in one message can emit both calls before either sub-task (`dispatchTool`, `maybeStartTaskSubtask`, `sse.go`, as this row's review reproduced through the real `dispatchEvent`). Nothing on the wire names the call today -- `sub_task_start` carries `subTaskId`, `label`, `parentMessageId` and `subAgentType`, and the `tool_call` carries no `metadata` -- so the adapter stamps the task call's `callId` on `sub_task_start` as an added property, `parentCallId` (`translateSubTaskStartFromTask` holds the `toolPart`; a property added, not required: MINOR under `contracts/COMPATIBILITY.md`), and the timeline pairs on it whichever of the two arrives first: a `sub_task_start` whose call is not yet known waits under that `callId`, and the call takes it when it arrives. An older agent's `sub_task_start`, or one from the legacy `subtask` part, carries no `parentCallId`; the timeline then hangs it under the latest `task` call of its message, or else the next `task` call of it (`toolName` `task`), never under another tool's call -- today's `attachSubTask` takes the latest call of the message whatever its tool, so it hangs a lane under a `read` call that came before the `task` call, or under a `bash` call that came after it, as this row's review reproduced -- and a sub-task still unattached when its turn ends is shown as a lane of the turn itself, never dropped, where today `resetTurnState` drops it unseen. Two parallel `task` calls from an older agent are the one case this cannot tell apart: with no `parentCallId`, either lane may hang under either call. This is the one change on the wire, and it is additive. History already stored is not backfilled: these payloads were never kept, so every turn before the fix stays without its tool activity; a sandbox alive at the deploy replays its buffered events (up to 1000, §6.1) on its next reconnect, and the live-turn rule stores only the Processing turn's, at the tail of its window, as §6.1 records for `token` frames at their own deploy. No migration: the key is a value, and `events_session_id_message_id_idx` is unchanged. With Step 228: its scenario-23 `tool_result` cases assert that a `tool_result` adds no row, which holds only until this Step ships; whichever of the two ships second updates the other's assertion, and row 228's sentence naming this defect. This filing corrects §6.1 and §25.15, which said these events are stored, and notes in §12.2 item 1 what its timeline and its right rail's cost breakdown lack until this Step ships. Exit, as named tests on the production shape -- a `step_start` first, and every event of its message under that message's id, never a fresh id per event: `TestResilience_ToolEventsOfOneMessage_EachStoredOnce` (the events the real translate path emits for one assistant message, through a real Bridge, the real wshub handler and the real actor: each `tool_call`, `tool_result` and the `step_finish` stored once, under its derived key, and broadcast once, a reconnect's replay adding no row, and a `fetch_history` on the real client socket returning the message's `step_start`, `tool_call`, `tool_result` and `step_finish`); `TestEvents_ToolEventsOfOneMessage_ReturnedByEventsAndHistory` (the same stored message read back through `GET /api/sessions/{id}/events` and `fetch_history`, all four rows in each) -- these two, on the server, are what prove that the stored rows reach the page; `TestEventStorageKey` (the three types derive their keys, every other type keeps its `messageId`, an event without its correlator keeps the bare one); `TestToolEvent_ReplayedAfterItsTurn_AddsNoRow`; `TestLinearProgress_FirstToolCallOfATurn_ProductionShape_NotifiesOnce` (a Linear-origin session, `step_start` then two `tool_call`s under one id: one progress notice); `TestStepCost_StepFinishStored_CountedOnce`; `TestDispatchTool_TaskSubtask_StampsParentCallId_BothOrders` (through the real `dispatchEvent`: the task's metadata on the first `running` update, then on a later one, and two parallel `task` calls in one message, each `sub_task_start` carrying its own call's `callId`); and in the web, rendering checks on the production shape, which pin what the page draws from the rows it is given and cannot see a loss on the server -- `buildCostRollup` never reads `messageId`, and the header counts every `tool_call` it is handed, so those three would pass on today's code: `timelineModel`'s tests (each tool call shows with its result, a step closes at its `step_finish` with its cost, a sub-task hangs under its own `task` call whether its `sub_task_start` comes before or after that call, two parallel `task` calls each keep their own lane, a `sub_task_start` without `parentCallId` hangs under a `task` call of its message -- for `read` c1, the sub-task, then `task` c2, under c2, and for `task` c2, `bash` c3, then the sub-task, under c2 -- and never under a `read` or `bash` call, and one left unattached at the turn's end shows as a turn-level lane), `costRollup`'s test on the rows one message whose `step_start`, `tool_call`, `tool_result` and `step_finish` share one `msg_` id leaves once stored (the turn and session totals and the tokens come from that `step_finish`), and `SessionHeader`'s and `CostPanel`'s on the same rows (the header shows the cost and the call count, the panel the turn's and session's cost) | §6.1, §12.2, §25.15 |

## Phase 16 — External-client prerequisites and the MCP surface (8 Steps, additive)

What this repository owes an external client before one can be built honestly. It is filed
here because of where it was NOT filed: all three were specified in the *dependent's* plan —
a separate repository for a client that does not exist — and nowhere in this one. A
dependency recorded only by the party who needs it is a dependency that gets forgotten.

**The gate is lifted. DÉCIDÉ (2026-09-17) : exposer ce système à des clients MCP existants.** La
question « un client natif sera-t-il construit » reste ouverte et SÉPARÉE — les deux n'en avaient
l'air que d'une, et le device flow du Step 158 vise le client natif sans couvrir des clients tiers.
Step 157 is worth doing regardless — a compatibility policy over `/contracts` is good
hygiene for a repository whose wire shapes an embedded SPA and a growing test surface already
consume. Steps 158 and 159 buy nothing until something outside this repository speaks to it,
and should not be started on speculation.

The rule that keeps this phase honest, and the reason it is not Phase 11: these are not gaps
a shipped Step left behind. Nothing here is missing from what this repository set out to
build. They are the price of a product decision, and they appear the moment it is made.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 157 | `/contracts` as a stable external API | A version marker, a written compatibility policy, changelog discipline, and CI that fails a breaking change rather than reporting it. Today those contracts are an internal convenience shared between this repository and its own SPA, regenerated in lockstep — which is exactly why a breaking change costs nothing today and would cost a shipped client tomorrow. Independent of any client decision, and the only one of the three worth doing before one is taken | §6.3 |
| 158 | native-client authentication | A device flow and device tokens with per-client scopes. The web UI's cookie session is not a native client's authentication, and a long-lived token pasted into an application is not an answer. Note for whoever builds it: this repository already has a device flow, for linking a ChatGPT account to fetch provider credentials — a different purpose, and not to be widened into this one without deciding whether one mechanism should serve both | §13.1 |
| 159 | cursor-resumable event stream | Catch-up from a last-seen offset after a disconnect, without re-pulling a session's history. Partly present already, which is why this needs an audit before code: `fetch_history` is cursor-paginated, and Step 114 gave the reconnect replay a truncation flag so a consumer can at least tell a cut replay from a complete one. What is missing is resuming the subscribe replay itself from an offset. Close the gap, not the whole surface | §6.1, §6.2 |
| 180 | the MCP surface: entry point, transport, and the tools it exposes | One adapter over the application services that already exist, never a second path into them: the same repository, user, provider-configuration and authorization checks every other ingress passes, or this becomes an unguarded door wearing a protocol. Define the entry point, the transport, the protocol versions accepted, and what an error looks like to a client that speaks a version this does not. The parity analysis that motivated this counted **14 tools** in the surface it studied, grouped by use: discovery (repositories, models), session reads (list, detail), result and review (result, verdict), work and tracking (delegate, send a prompt), events and waiting (poll, wait for idle), plan (read, approve, reject), and stop. Those names identify the surface studied; they are not a proposed naming convention here. Exit: a client performs discovery and one session read through the same authorization path an equivalent HTTP call takes, and a version this does not speak is refused with a message naming the versions it does | §43, §6.3 |
