| 181 | MCP authentication, consent and revocation (4 PRs — (a) authorization-code core, (b) refresh and token revocation, (c) client metadata documents and dynamic registration, and (d) admin view, rate limits and the pending-request cap, all shipped) | **Split into four sequential PRs, each green behind the MCP flag with its own exit proof (§43.13).** **(a) the authorization-code core**: discovery documents, pre-registered clients, authorization with consent, code exchange with mandatory PKCE, bearer-only `/mcp` verified on every call with no cache, scope-gated tool discovery, and user revocation in Settings — its own exit proof is this row's exit criterion, end to end through the official SDK client. **(b) refresh and token revocation**: refresh tokens rotated on every use, whose scopes are fixed at issuance like every credential's -- a refresh narrows only relative to the presented refresh token, never to the grant's -- with reuse detection that deletes the grant and no grace window, and an RFC 7009 revocation endpoint through which a client revokes its whole authorization with either token type (always `200`, never another client's) -- its own exit proof is the official SDK client refreshing on the production router with no second consent, and stopping on the next call after revoking. **(c) registration without a prior relationship**: client ID metadata documents (on by default), fetched through an outbound adapter whose SSRF guard checks every address at dial time and follows a redirect only within the URL's own origin, validated byte for byte against the URL they identify, cached for at most an hour (the document's own cache headers only shorten that; the first re-fetch that fails since the last successful fetch keeps the cached document one hour more from that failure, but never past two hours after that last successful fetch -- a first failure past that bound gets no grace at all -- then refuses the client until a fetch succeeds), and shown on the consent page by the URL's host -- plain ASCII as written, an internationalized host in its `xn--` form -- rather than the name the client chose; and RFC 7591 dynamic registration (off by default), forced to a public client and braked by a per-network rate limit -- a re-fetch or re-registration never changes what an issued code, token or refresh chain can do, and unused self-registered clients no operator disabled are swept -- its own exit proof is the official SDK client completing the flow on the production router through a metadata document (fetched only through the guard's test seam, the production guard refusing the same loopback server) and through dynamic registration with its flag on. **(d) the admin view, the brakes and the cap**: an administrator lists a member's authorizations -- exactly what the member sees, never a token -- and revokes one on the member's behalf, reaching only that member's grants (any other id is `404`), audited as the administrator's with the member named; the authorization and token endpoints braked per client network like dynamic registration, a refused request spending nothing -- the token endpoint's `429` is `temporarily_unavailable`, never `invalid_grant`, so a client keeps its refresh token -- and one network never spending another's bucket, a network being the connecting address -- behind a proxy that hides client addresses everyone shares one, until trusting a forwarded header is decided (§43.14); at most 100 pending authorization requests per client, counted and stored under the client's lock so no race exceeds it; an administrator's disable and enable of any one client, audited, for a metadata-document client the durable block on its URL a deletion is not -- that URL only, since its publisher can come back under another, which only switching metadata documents off keeps out; and an on-call runbook for cutting a client off -- its own exit proof is an administrator's revocation stopping the member's SDK session on its next call, and each brake and the cap proven on the production router with the shipped values. The bulk of the cost, and the part Step 158 does NOT provide: its device flow is for a native client and does not extend to third-party clients. An OAuth surface needs server discovery, client registration or configuration, authorization with consent, credential exchange and refresh, and revocation. Scopes gate read and write, repository access, and the rights check on every session and every tool -- **and gate DISCOVERY as well as invocation**: a client that may not use a tool must not be told the tool exists, because the tool list is itself a description of the deployment. Expiry, protected credential storage, invalidation, and management of granted authorizations in user settings. Kept distinct from BOTH the model-provider OAuth and the deployment's own secrets -- three credential families that look alike and fail differently. Exit: a revoked authorization stops working on the next call, and a scope-less client's tool list omits what it cannot call | §13, §43 |
| 182 | a quiet status surface and a result worth reading (3 PRs — (a) status and transcript shipped; (b) the bounded wait shipped; (c) the result shipped) | **Split into three PRs, each with its own exit proof (§43.20).** **(a) status and transcript**: `GET /api/sessions/{sessionID}/status` answers what a session's work is doing now -- queued, running, delivering (a completed turn's push and pull request still under way, bounded), scheduled (work the server holds that may create a turn with no new input: an automatic re-review's debounce on a repository that opted in with budget left, and a release manifest check; every declared timer kind classified by one table, a kind it does not know counted as work; a review session never pushes, so no push of its own can arm a re-review behind a settled status), awaiting approval, idle or finished -- derived from the turn queue, that delivery, that armed work and the human gates in one database snapshot, never from `sessions.status` (re-derived only when a turn finishes, so a queued or running turn can sit under any of its values, a finished turn's outcome included), with a suggested delay before the next read from a small per-activity table clamped to `platform.Timeouts` bounds; `narvi_get_session_status` bridges to it and `narvi_get_session_transcript` bridges to the existing paginated events route, both `mcp:read`, and the status never carries the transcript -- its own exit proof is a follow-up queued under a row that says completed reading queued, then running, and finished only once terminal, and the official SDK client on the production router getting the twins' bytes with a scope-less grant seeing neither tool. **(b) the bounded wait**: the status tool's blocking form, returning on a settled state and never on a queue that has not started -- this row's own exit; `GET /api/sessions/{sessionID}/status?waitSeconds=N` and `narvi_wait_for_session` over it (`mcp:read`, the same twin), one application service (`sessionactivity.Waiter`) that reads at once and then once a second -- a Postgres poll, no connection held across a sleep, any replica -- for at most 25 s, answering the latest status with how the wait ended (settled, timeout, interrupted, capacity); two waits per grant (else per user), four per user across every grant and the browser, and 32 per replica, a wait past any of them answered at once as a plain status, never an error; every wait interrupted when the server's shutdown begins; its exit proof is the production router through the official SDK client: a wait answers `finished` within a poll of a turn really completing and `awaiting_approval` with the plan, stays blocked through queued, delivering and scheduled, runs the whole 25 s on a queue that never starts, and settles on a second router reading the same database. **(c) the result**: `GET /api/sessions/{sessionID}/result` and `narvi_get_session_result` over it (`mcp:read`, the same gate as the session read) answer what a session produced, from one read-only snapshot of what Postgres already holds -- the activity (the status's own), the last run with a summary that is its final text read by the one reader of a turn's final text (`plan.FinalText`, which plan content now reads through too), capped at 4,000 characters and never model-written, the pull requests it opened or reviews with `reviewScope` saying `none` explicitly so an empty list never reads as a clean review, and each one's review state read from the record (`absent`, `in_progress`, `not_assessed` with the older verdict superseded, `assessed`); an assessed verdict's freshness is read live on every call through the merge path's own comparison and live read, extracted from it unchanged (`autoapproval.CheckFreshness`, `reviewfreshness.ReadLive`; the eligibility, revalidation and auto-merge suites pass by name before and after), `current` only when that comparison passes on live facts and any failed read `unconfirmed`; its exit proof is `absent`, `in_progress`, `not_assessed`, `stale`, `unconfirmed` and `not_applicable` distinct on the wire on real Postgres, `current` never without live confirmation, a review session's verdict reading `stale` once a push moves its pull request's head, while the status reads `finished`, and the official SDK client on the production router getting the twin's bytes. Review round 1 added: a pull request record that names no pull request the session opened -- a creation suppressed in shadow mode, recognised by `shadowscm.IsSyntheticPRRef`, or a row that cannot be read -- listed in `excludedPullRequests` with why instead of failing the whole result; `not_applicable` decided from the record only for a merged pull request, a closed one read live since a reopen never clears the claim's closed stamp; the merge path's own probe run on the live head, so the result and the merge path give a verdict the record decides the same reason; one budget for all of a call's live reads (`SessionResultLiveReadBudget`, below the MCP wait bound) and `suggestedDelaySeconds`, with no narrower pull request read on the port to use instead of `GetOpenPR`. **Follow-up (not in this row; shipped as Step 220):** the decision inbox's cached read model (`decisioninbox`'s `computeRealEligibility`) assembled the live freshness facts itself, through its TTL cache, degrading and carrying on where `reviewfreshness.ReadLive` stops and names the failed step; only the comparison (`autoapproval.CheckFreshness`) was shared with it. Step 220 routes it through `ReadLive`, keeping its cache, so a change to the live read reaches the inbox too (§21.1b). A client that polls hard is a client that gets rate-limited into looking broken. A compact session state carries a suggested delay before the next read. State, summarised result and detailed transcript are three separate things; the paginated cursor transcript is served only on explicit request, never bundled into a status read. The bounded wait distinguishes **queued**, **running**, **awaiting approval** and **actually finished** -- a non-empty queue must never render as idle, which is the same failure as a truncated scan rendering clean. Expose the pull requests produced and the last run's result, then its verdict with its freshness and its possible ABSENCE, from the data this system already holds: §21.1's persisted context and §21.1b's rule that no consumer re-derives one. No second store of sessions or verdicts. Exit: a wait returns on a real terminal state and never on a queue that has not started | §21.1, §21.1b, §43 |
| 217 | stopping a session, and every session it started | Stop did not exist anywhere: no route, no actor command, no caller of `authz.ActionStopSession`, and the control plane never sent the sandbox the `stop` command its agent already handles. Row 183's MCP stop tool needs a real application capability under it, so this row builds that capability, REST only, from §3.3's existing transitions -- no new turn state, trigger or session status. Filed ahead of 183, which depends on it, by Phase 11's own rule for a gap that blocks a scheduled Step. **The request is data; the actor makes every transition.** `POST /api/sessions/{sessionID}/stop`, no body, answers `202 StopSessionResponse` (`sessionId`, `requestedAt`, `reachedSessionIds`, `openTurns`); `400` and `404` as on `GET`. In one transaction under the session's actor-epoch lock it sets `turns.stop_requested_at` on every turn open at that instant and `sessions.stop_requested_at` (migration `000151`, both nullable and reversible; once it is applied a binary without it does not boot), upserts a new `stop` session timer to now, and audits `session.stop`; the timer is what makes the request survive the loss of a replica. The timer's handler cancels each flagged pending turn (`TriggerCancel`, with the synthetic `execution_complete` §3.3 requires); sends a flagged turn in flight the sandbox `stop{gen}` after its commit and re-arms for the end of that turn's `StopGrace` (30s, `internal/platform/timeouts.go`, which `Validate` keeps positive and below `TurnDeadline`); cancels it with a synthetic event if it is still in flight then, as `turn_deadline` ends a turn, and retires the sandbox gen it ran on (its live state → `suspect` → `stopped` through the machine's table, its provider object stopped after the commit and reaped by the reconciler otherwise) once that gen is no longer delivering a completed turn's push and pull request, a wait bounded by `MCPStatusDeliveryWindow` that holds the retirement alone -- the turn is cancelled at its grace's end all the same, and nothing is dispatched to that gen or snapshotted meanwhile -- so the next turn runs on a new gen and the stopped work's late `execution_complete`, which names a gen and never a turn, cannot be booked against it; and, while the session's request stands, deletes the session's work-creating timers armed at or before the request (`ClassifyTimer`, which classifies `stop` as a turn-in-flight timer; `session_timers.created_at` against the request's latest instant, which a repeat moves forward, both on the database's clock). `planDispatch` cancels a flagged pending turn instead of dispatching it, and a flagged turn in flight is never re-sent to a respawned sandbox. While the session's request stands, a workflow attempt that ends, however it ends, ends its run `cancelled` without consulting `NextStep`; a step awaiting a person's decision keeps waiting, approving or revising it resumes the session and the run goes on, and rejecting it fails the run and leaves the request standing. **Every session it started.** After its commit the route walks `parent_session_id`, breadth first, and applies the same request to each descendant in its own transaction, audited with `detail.via_parent_session_id` and authorized by the check on the session named. `CreateSessionOnTx` reads a child's parent `FOR SHARE` before anything else and refuses a child of a stopped parent with a permanent marker, so the sentinel auto-fix outbox never retries it: a spawn racing a stop either commits first and is found by the walk, or waits and is refused. **Not an archive**: a person's next act that sets the session going again (a turn they create, their approval of the session's plan, or their approval or revision of a workflow step) clears the session's request, and turns created after it run normally; turns keep their own flags. A completed turn keeps its push and pull request, an `awaiting_approval` plan stays, and the sandbox idles out unless its gen was retired. Owner-decision defaults taken, open to revision: O1, a member stops their own or joined sessions (§13.3 amended), except a pull request's review session, which takes admin or maintainer as before O1 (decided in this row's review), admin and maintainer any, a viewer none; O2, stop filed as its own row, with 183 depending on it. Exit: a REST stop cancels the queue and the in-flight turn through the sandbox's `stop` command, or through the grace when the agent stays silent, after which the next turn runs on a new sandbox gen and the stopped work's late end changes nothing and pushes nothing; that gen is retired, and the next turn dispatched, never while it still delivers a completed turn's push and pull request within its window; a prompt sent after it runs, and a workflow step a person approves or revises after it runs its workflow on; it reaches every descendant, and a child spawn racing it is refused or stopped, in either order; a workflow run it interrupts ends `cancelled` with no next step, however its attempt ends while the request stands; the session's work-creating timers armed before it are disarmed, and one a push arms after it stays; and the request survives the loss of the replica that wrote it | §3.3, §2, §13.3 |
| 183 | plan, revision and stop over MCP (4 PRs — the mcp source, delegation, plan decisions and stop, all shipped) | Approval keeps every permission check it has on the existing paths -- a plan approved through a protocol is not a plan approved with fewer conditions. Define precisely what a plan revision QUEUED while an already-approved implementation is running does: the new request must not retroactively withdraw the authorization the running work holds, which is the shape that turns a safety feature into a mid-flight abort. Describe `Stop`'s effect on the execution, on the queue, and on any children, reusing §3.3's existing transitions rather than inventing a second stop. Exit: a revision queued mid-implementation leaves the running work authorized, and a Stop that reaches a parent leaves no orphan child running. **DECIDED (2026-09-28): a session created over MCP records `spawn_source = mcp`**, set server-side, never read from a request body: the REST create path keeps refusing any caller-supplied source other than `web`, the forged-provenance fix it carries. No MCP tool creates a session yet: the bridge's tools are twins of GET routes and `callTwin` (`internal/adapters/inbound/mcp/bridge.go`) sends no body, so the create tool this row adds also needs the bridge to carry one. The source comes from the MCP grant the /mcp authentication already attaches to the request context (`internal/platform/mcpgrantcontext.go`, set by `auth.RequireMCPBearer`), never from the principal's credential type, so a future native-client bearer (Step 158) is not recorded as `mcp`. The value is first opened among the contracts' open enums by a prior PR, then written by the migration, and notification routing learns it in the same change so an MCP session's turns log no unrecognised source. **That prior step has shipped (contracts 1.9.2):** `Session.spawnSource` is in `manifest.json`'s `openEnums`, so adding `mcp` grades MINOR; the web UI shows a source it does not know as a neutral "other", and the REST mapper and notification routing already pass one through or skip it. `CreateSessionRequest.spawnSource` stays closed. **The value and its migration have shipped (contracts 1.10.0, migration `000149_session_spawn_source_mcp`), with no writer:** `session_spawn_source` gains `mcp`, `Session.spawnSource` lists it (MINOR), both notification routers treat it like `web` (nothing enqueued, no unrecognised-source WARN), and the web UI labels it "MCP" with its own glyph. The migration is irreversible once used: Postgres cannot drop an enum value, so its down migration recreates the type and refuses while any session records `mcp`. Every boot migrates up, and golang-migrate refuses a schema version it has no file for, so once 000149 is applied an older pod already running keeps working, but an older pod that restarts does not boot, in a rollback or mid rolling-deploy: rolling the binary back is safe only to a binary that carries 000149. Rolling back further first needs the down migration, which works only before the first MCP session. It runs with the control plane scaled to zero, and the older binary is deployed after it: against live pods, each cached statement that binds or returns the source fails once per connection, and a 149-aware pod that restarts applies 000149 again. Deploy order: that change runs everywhere before anything writes the value, or an older replica logs the WARN and skips. **Delegation over MCP has shipped (contracts 1.11.0, migration `000150_session_create_idempotency_key`):** `narvi_create_session`, under `mcp:write`, bridges to `POST /api/sessions` with the request DTO as the twin's body (never the raw arguments, and still no header); `mcp:write` is advertised, and the consent page says what it risks; the route records `mcp` exactly when an MCP grant is on the context, and that value also labels the intent decision, the audit row and the gates' refusals; `CreateSessionRequest.idempotencyKey`, optional on REST and required by the tool, makes a retried create answer the session it started (a different request under the key is `409`, and so is a key whose session was started the other way, by cookie or over MCP; requests are compared by a canonical form, however their JSON is spelled; the key is 8-4-4-4-12 hexadecimal digits or `400`; and a unique index settles two concurrent ones; once 000150 is applied a binary without it does not boot, and its down migration runs with the control plane scaled to zero, since every query that returns a whole session names its columns); `/mcp` is braked per grant after the bearer gate and the create per grant again (owner decision D5 adopted, `docs/DECISIONS.md`); and every audit row a change over MCP writes carries the grant and the client. Owner-decision defaults taken there, open to revision: one `mcp:write` scope; the key required on MCP, optional on REST, scoped per user and kept with the session; the brakes' values (a burst of 30 calls then one a second on `/mcp`, a burst of 5 calls then one a minute on the create, a same-key retry counting as a call, both per grant and per replica); and the write checkbox pre-checked like read. The MCP section of the web guide moved to `docs/guides/mcp.md`. **Plan decisions over MCP have shipped (contracts 1.12.0):** `narvi_list_plans` (`mcp:read`) and `narvi_approve_plan`, `narvi_reject_plan`, `narvi_request_plan_revision` and `narvi_send_prompt` (`mcp:write`) bridge to the existing plan and turn routes with nothing in them changed, so an approval over MCP keeps every check REST makes: the role and own/joined rule, the open-turn gate, the first verdict winning across channels, the plan's ownership, the approved-content snapshot, the audit row (stamped with the grant) and the notices. The revision rule is normative text in §43.21 and §8.1: a revision, or any prompt, is refused `409` over REST and MCP alike while any turn is open, and nothing is queued; no ingress queues a revision behind an approved implementation today (the two `AlwaysQueue` callers, a code-host mention and the re-run-review button, never queue a plan-mode turn there); were one queued, it would wait for the implementation's terminal state, the implementation's push and pull request proceeding independently of it, and would then write the next version awaiting approval while the approved one stays approved. This is the first half of the row's exit: a revision queued mid-implementation leaves the running work authorized, and one requested mid-implementation is refused over MCP and REST alike. Owner-decision default taken there, open to revision: O5, a prompt while a turn is running is refused `409` as on REST, never queued. **Stop over MCP has shipped (contracts 1.14.0), and closes the row:** `narvi_stop_session` (`mcp:write`) bridges to Step 217's `POST /api/sessions/{sessionID}/stop` with nothing in it changed, so a stop over MCP keeps every check and every effect REST has: the role, own/joined and review-session rule (a member's token is refused a pull request's review session as the member's cookie is), the request written as data under each session's lock, the walk to every session it started, and a `session.stop` audit row per session reached, each stamped with the grant. Its hints are the shipped route's, not the design's: destructive, not idempotent (a repeat also stops whatever was started since, as the route documents) and open-world (a cancelled turn's notice goes to the Slack thread or Linear session its session came from) (§43.22). The cutoff runbook now pairs revoking a client's access with stopping the sessions it put to work. Exit met, on the production router through the official SDK client (`PlanRevisionThenStop_NoOrphan_SDKClient`): a plan is approved and its implementation runs on a sandbox connected to the router's own sandbox WebSocket; a revision is refused and leaves it running and approved; a child started by the sentinel auto-fix notifier has its first turn queued; a stop over MCP on the parent reaches both, the agent confirms the stop, and once every reached session has settled no turn of the parent or of any session it started is pending, dispatched or processing in Postgres. **Depends on Step 217** (owner decision O2): stop itself -- the route, the request written as data, the actor's cancel -- is that row's, REST only, and this row's stop tool bridges to its route | §3.3, §8.1, §43 |

**Phase 16 milestone**: Step 157's compatibility policy fails a breaking change in CI, and an
existing MCP client, through the official SDK on the production router, is authorized with consent,
discovers, starts and follows a session, reads its result, decides a plan and stops a session with
every session it started, each through the checks the equivalent HTTP call makes (Steps 180-183
and 217, each proven by its own row's exit). If a native client is decided, a client outside this
repository also authenticates as a native client,
follows a session to completion, survives a network drop and catches up from its own offset
without re-pulling — demonstrated by a script client in this repository's own tests, so the
guarantee does not depend on a repository this one cannot see. Under no native-client decision,
158/159 stay unstarted rather than half-built.

## Phase 12 — Extension boundaries (4 Steps, additive)

The seams that let an optional module compose a second binary on top of this repository
without this repository ever knowing that module exists. Technical plan §34 is the
normative specification; `docs/design/boundaries-design.md` holds the Go shapes and the
test lists.

On placement: this is appended rather than folded in, for the reason Phases 8, 9 and 10
each give for themselves — those phases' Steps were long since scoped and shipped,
folding new Steps into a closed phase would rewrite history, and inserting them
mid-sequence would shift every Step after them and every cross-reference that names one
(the Phase 4 standing rule; Step 128's own 183 citations are the standing evidence). It
follows Phase 11 numerically without waiting on it. That ordering was recorded while
Phase 11 was still a holding list with no exit criterion; it has since been scheduled in
full and has a milestone of its own, which changes nothing here — numbering records the
order in which work was appended, never a dependency, and no Step of this phase consumes
a Phase 11 row.

Execution order: **Step 132 → 133 → {134, 135}**. Step 132 is a prerequisite of
everything else here (nothing can be injected into a `main` that cannot be imported).
Step 134 is a **prerequisite of Step 107** and must ship before it, so that mode A's
selector is built against the seam rather than retrofitted onto it — that is the one
hard ordering constraint this phase places on work outside it. Step 135 is independent
of 134 and waits only on the capability vocabulary being settled, because its wire enum
is the first thing here that becomes a published contract.

Two pieces of this work are deliberately **not** Steps. The specification itself (§34)
is a documentation change, not a shippable unit. The enterprise composition — the
separate repository's own module, composition binary and boundary CI — lives outside
this repository entirely; a plan that tracks this repository's Steps has no business
claiming credit for a commit it does not contain. What *is* recorded here is the
property that repository proves: with no module registered, the composed binary is the
same product as this one.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 132 | importable composition root | `cmd/control-plane`'s ~2280-line `serve()` moved verbatim into a non-`internal` `controlplane` package, split into `Main` (subcommand dispatch + non-wiring boot pre-flights), `Build` (all wiring, against an already-open pool), `Run` (listener + the one errgroup) and `Routes` (a `chi.Walk` enumeration); `cmd/control-plane/main.go` becomes a one-line `main`. Proof obligation: a route-table golden generated by `ops.ScanRegisteredRoutes` from the **pre-move** tree, asserted against the live router post-move — two enumeration methods, two code states. Its own blind spot is closed in the same Step: the golden pins paths, so a second test asserts every `/api` route carries more middlewares than the router's global chain, with the baseline read off the live mux so a future global middleware raises the bar instead of lowering it. Retargets every scanner that hardcoded the old path — a scanner left pointing at the 16-line stub passes vacuously, which is worse than absent | §34 |
| 133 | capability registry, module façade, import ban | `internal/domain/license` (pure, Ed25519, offline, no `time.Now()`), `internal/app/capability.Registry` answering `Enabled = installed AND licensed AND valid-now`, the non-`internal` `extension` façade (Go's `internal` rule means a separate module can never import `internal/...`; every crossing type is re-exported by alias, which makes the crossing set an explicit, small list), `httpapi.RequireCapability`, and the module insertion points in the composition root (own migrations table, routes under a reserved prefix behind auth, workers in the existing errgroup, web assets). The two properties that matter more than the feature: a key can entitle but never create behavior (with no module composed nothing is installed, proven by a build that never consults the registry at all), and **a licence state can never be an input to a §30 suppression decision** — enforced by a seventh `narvichecks` analyzer banning the *import* of the registry, the licence domain and the façade outside a named allow-list, because an import is a syntactic fact a wrapper cannot dodge | §34, §30 |
| 134 ∥ | knowledge-retrieval seam | **Blocks Step 107.** `internal/domain/knowledge` (`Candidate`, `Query`, `OrderByScores`, `TakeTop`, `RecencyRanker`) and `ports.KnowledgeRanker`, whose `Score` returns **one score per candidate and no candidates at all**: a ranker receives what the gate already admitted and can reorder it, never add, drop, replace or re-select. §31.6's gate-then-rank stops being a convention someone must remember and becomes the shape of a signature. The public implementation keeps the gate's recency order; degradation on a failing or inconsistent ranker falls back to that order, never to empty, and is recorded on the turn so the mode A/B comparison never counts a degraded turn as the B arm | §34, §31.6, §31.5 |
| 135 ∥ | capabilities read model & UI slot | `GET /api/capabilities` as a derived read model — installed/licensed/expired per capability, never the key and never the subject — plus a runtime slot registry in the SPA and an honest affordance naming what a capability is and why it is unavailable here, in the "not available yet" idiom the analytics and settings views already use. No new Settings tab: the eight-tab mockup keeps screenshot parity and the affordance rides the repo-settings card column. Waits on the capability vocabulary being settled: this Step is where those names become a published wire enum, and a published enum value is expensive to rename | §34, §12.2 |

**Phase 12 milestone**: a binary composed in a separate repository builds against this
repository's `main` and, with no module registered, serves a route table identical to the
public binary's own golden — the property that keeps this repository honest, because it
means nothing was subtracted from here to make room for a module. In a build with no
module composed, a key in any state (absent, malformed, expired, valid) enables nothing,
proven by a build that never consults the registry at all rather than by a test of what it
answers. A registry import planted in a suppression package fails `make lint` with a
diagnostic naming §30. And Step 107 can ship its deterministic selector against the
ranking port with no module present.

Steps 134 and 135 are marked parallel by **dependency**, not by review: neither needs the
other's output, but each still ships as its own PR with its own exit criterion green
before the next begins, and each is audited on its own diff. Grouping Steps into a
delivery batch is a scheduling convenience for whoever is driving; it never becomes an
execution mode. A batch has no exit criterion — Steps do, and this phase does.

## Phase 13 — Silent failures (7 Steps, additive)

Six ways this system can fail while looking like it succeeded, and the write-time refusals that
stop a seventh. Technical plan §35 (sandbox lifetime rotation and turn continuity), §36 (the
broken-contract backstop), §24.8 (the base branch as part of the trigger decision) and §37
(configuration refused at write time) are the normative specifications.

What binds them is not a subsystem but a property: **in every one of these, the failure is
indistinguishable from the ordinary success it displaces.** A turn killed because its sandbox ran
out of life reads as a turn that failed on its merits. An agent with no memory of its own prior
work reads as an agent that found nothing in progress. A workflow step that never posted a status
advances on the `ok` edge. An automation that could not reach the system it exists to read reports
`completed`. A review dispatched against a base that will move reads like any other review. A
setting that can never match reads as data that genuinely did not match. None of these produces a
signal anyone can act on, which is why none of them was found from inside this repository.

On placement: by content Steps 136-139 are Phase 2 resilience work and 140-142 are Phase 5 review
and automations work. Appended rather than folded in, for the reason Phases 8 through 12 each give
for themselves — those phases' Steps were long since scoped, shipped and audited, folding new Steps
into a closed phase would rewrite history, and inserting them mid-sequence would shift every Step
after them and every cross-reference that names one (the Phase 4 standing rule; Step 128's own 183
citations are the standing evidence).

It is not filed in Phase 11 either, and that is a rule rather than a preference: Phase 11's filing
rule is that **a gap goes there only if the Step that found it shipped correctly without it, having
said on screen or in its own docs what is missing**. Nothing here was declared by the Step that
shipped it — these are defects and omissions in shipped behavior, found from outside. Filing them
under a self-declaration rule would be the one thing that makes that table stop meaning anything.

Execution order: **136 → 137 → 139 → {140, 141, 142}**, with 138 independent. Step 137 needs 136's persisted
deadline (a gate cannot read what nobody stamps). Step 139 needed its recap's text stored whole, because a recap assembled
from lossy storage is worth less than the warning next to it; that shipped when every distinct text frame became its
own stored row (§6.1, **Stored `token` frames**), and row 138 was re-scoped (2026-09-27) to what remains, needed only
by another runtime adapter. Step 141 is ordered after 140:
a backstop that ran before an interrupted turn stopped being a failed one would read every rotation
as a broken contract.

**One hard ordering constraint this phase places on work outside it**: if any later mechanism is
built to advance on a review verdict, it must be exempt from Step 142's deferral before it ships.
A deferred review posts no verdict, so such a mechanism would wait forever behind a gate whose
whole purpose is to avoid pointless work. §24.8 states the rule; this records that it is a
constraint on future Steps, not a note about this one.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 136 | sandbox lifetime as persisted state | `sandbox.lifetime_deadline_at` written in the same UPDATE that already stamps a spawn's or restore's `created_at`: conservative estimate first, exact value when `sandbox-agent` reports it on `ready`/`heartbeat`, so nothing depends on an agent-side change having shipped into a weeks-old snapshot. Per-session-type lifetime centralised in one place. Exit: a live sandbox's row carries a deadline within the provider's own, and a restored one carries a fresh one **Amended:** the snapshot must carry the runtime version it was minted under, kept across every restore. A fresh start date does not make a snapshot's binaries younger, and without that provenance an incompatible snapshot is indistinguishable from a current one. Refusal has to be visible; continuity and recap are Step 139's half. | §35.2, §3.2 |
| 137 | pre-dispatch runway gate and rotation | `RotationRunwayFloor` in `platform/timeouts.go` and nowhere else; a pure decision function in `internal/domain/sandbox` added to §9.1's exhaustive corpus; rotation as `snapshot → stopped → shutdown → restore` on the path §3.2 already specifies, gen incremented like any restore, superseded identity locked out at once. A declined rotation falls through to an ordinary dispatch so the queue can never stall; a sandbox that died idle at its deadline is marked `stopped`. §9.3 scenario 18, first half **Amended:** add a monotonicity test -- raising the floor must actually bring the rotation forward, which a floor that is read but never compared would pass without. Do not copy another system's duration constants. **Amended:** a rotation whose snapshot finds the sandbox already gone or shutting down restores from the previous valid snapshot and records the failure; the failed attempt never becomes a restore point, and this lot's acceptance asserts it. That restore is a successful one, so Step 139's fresh-lineage recap does not fire for it; the rotation records a persisted warning of its own naming the snapshot it fell back to, so the next turn and a reader know work since that snapshot was lost. Commits the session already pushed that the restored workspace lacks stay on its branch: the next push is an ordinary one, never forced, so it fails as non-fast-forward with the reason recorded rather than overwriting them. | §35.3, §5.4, §9.3 |
| 138 ∥ | token parts | **Re-scoped 2026-09-27: the premise was wrong.** The store never kept a multi-part answer's last fragment: the adapter already sends each text part under its own part id as `messageId`, and first-wins dedupe kept each part's **first** frame (empty or a prefix) -- fixed by storing every distinct frame as its own row (§6.1, "Stored `token` frames"), so the pinned runtime needs nothing more here. What remains, only for a runtime adapter whose `messageId` is not already per part: `/contracts` sandbox-WS amendment, optional part id on `token`, and §6.1's append-only storage keyed by `(messageId, partId)` -- one stored row per **distinct frame** of each `(messageId, partId)`, never a row updated in place, and no `created_at` pinning: a part's first frame is stored when the part opens, so its first row already sorts before the tool calls it introduced. **Additive, not breaking**: absent means today's exact behavior. Notification-surface posts pinned byte-identical to today's; the subscribe replay's existing byte budget must hold with up to two rows per text part | §35.6, §6.1 |
| 139 | fresh-lineage continuity | Mark a sandbox row that starts a fresh lineage, never a successful restore or resume; the first turn dispatched after the mark carries a recap built from stored events by the extractor the notification surfaces already use, framed as a third-party report to re-verify, cleared only once the prompt reaches the sandbox; a persisted continuity `warning`, never a broadcast, because §12.2's rail renders persisted events and a reload must still say the context was lost. Instrument the restore-without-a-usable-session case rather than widening to it. §9.3 scenario 19 | §35.5, §9.3, §6.1 |
| 140 | a deadline stop is an interruption | The turn stays `Processing` (Stop, watchdog deferrals and composer keep working; `turn_deadline` still bounds the whole turn, rotations included); neutral persisted `warning`; no terminal event, no completion, no outbox notification, no fallback verdict; the same turn re-dispatched on reconnect with an interrupted-turn context. Recognised by a typed reason, with the legacy-text path a permanent fallback and never the primary signal. Pins the elapsed-seconds trap: a deadline message embedding its own duration must not match a transient-provider classifier. §9.3 scenario 18, second half | §35.4, §3.2, §3.3 |
| 141 | the broken-contract backstop | One typed outcome for a turn that ends without its mandated artifact, applied to all three surfaces at once — §25.6's step-outcome tool, §8.2's verdict tool, §3.5's automation runs — through one path accounting, logging and notification all read. Best-effort (a backstop that cannot answer leaves the turn's own verdict standing) and never reconciled. Control-plane-side over already-persisted events; no image rebuild. **Amended:** for the verdict contract, one reminder precedes the backstop. A whole review turn (§21.1b's kind) that ends successfully with no verdict recorded and nothing queued for publication gets one follow-up turn in the same session asking for the verdict only (never a second review, never a question to the author). A narrowed turn carries no verdict mandate: one that ends without a verdict records that it ended without an answer, fails nothing, and replies to its requester that no answer was produced, with the re-run guidance. The follow-up is not a review attempt: it opens no check of its own and emits nothing when it ends; its verdict is attributed to the reminded turn's attempt and context by the verdict endpoint, and so is its kind (§21.1b: a verdict's kind is the kind of the turn it is attributed to), so it is never narrowed for being posted by the follow-up; the reminded turn's not-assessed emission waits until the follow-up settles, then goes out only if no verdict came; and counter-review corroboration (Step 71) reads the reminded turn's own sub-task events. It carries no context of its own, so a dispatch-time context check (Step 198, where it exists) does not apply to it: a head that moved meanwhile only makes its verdict stale by the ordinary freshness rule. Once per attempt, never for a failed or superseded turn; a follow-up that also ends without a verdict is backstopped as above (§36.2) | §36, §25.6, §8.2, §3.5 |
| 142 ∥ | base-branch gate, retarget lane, write-time refusals | Trunk allowlist gating §24's automatic paths only, every explicit trigger ungated; the one-time deferral notice, withdrawn when the gate passes; `pull_request` re-target routed, since a parent merging moves no head and so emits no `synchronize`. Plus §37's three refusals: tool-server name length against the provider ceiling, unrooted path expressions, bindings naming a repository that is gone. The verdict-waiter exemption is part of the gate, not a follow-up **Amended:** retarget routing is only half the lifecycle. A `stacked` membership path does not exist in the ingress at all: persist the work before acknowledging the webhook, re-read real membership after a removal or dissolution, invalidate immediately, and defer ONLY the launch of a new review until the quiet period ends -- deferring the invalidation with it would leave a stale approval usable throughout the delay. The debounce is per PR, not per stack: ten PRs still launch ten reviews, and claiming otherwise would be a property this does not have. **Amended:** a parent closed without merging ends the wait it created -- the close is routed like the other membership events, and the deferral notice is replaced by one naming the closed parent and what unblocks the PR (a re-target, or an explicit trigger, which stays ungated). | §24.8, §37 |

**Phase 13 milestone**: each class produces a signal that can be told apart from the success it used
to imitate. A turn dispatched onto a sandbox under its runway floor rotates and completes instead
of failing; a deadline reached mid-turn renders as a neutral notice with the turn still running,
and no notification, verdict fallback or completion is emitted for the interrupted attempt; a
fresh-lineage respawn ships a recap the agent is told to re-verify and a continuity warning that
survives a reload; a workflow step, review verdict or automation run that ends without its mandated
artifact is `failed` with a typed reason naming the contract, while an unreadable backstop leaves
the run's own verdict standing; a PR based on an unmerged PR is deferred with its base named, and
reviewed when the provider re-targets it. §9.3 scenarios 18 and 19 green — gating this phase, on
the Phase 4 precedent, never Phase 2's own closed criterion.

## Phase 14 — Decomposition and chaining (5 Steps, additive)

Two capabilities that let work this system already knows how to do be composed into more than one
unit: one automation's own conclusion starting another (§38), and a tracker ticket a team already
decomposed landing as one pull request per sub-issue rather than one monolithic diff (§39).

Unlike Phase 13, nothing here is a defect. These are capabilities this design does not have and
could reasonably choose not to build; they are specified because the decision to build them was
taken, not because their absence breaks anything. The two are grouped because they share their
substrate — a server-owned state machine advancing on a typed report an agent produced, never an
agent driving the next step itself — and because they share their prerequisite.

On placement: appended after Phase 13, for the reason Phases 8 through 13 each give for themselves.

**Phase 13 is a prerequisite of this phase, not merely earlier than it.** Both capabilities advance
on a report that a turn is obliged to produce, so both inherit §36's question of what happens when
that report never comes — a chain whose source silently reports nothing stalls with no signal, and
a train link that reports nothing blocks its successors forever. Building either before Step 141
would mean shipping that hole on purpose. The train additionally needs Step 142's verdict-waiter
exemption to exist before its first link is ever deferred (§24.8, §39.5).

Execution order: **143 → 144 → 145 → {146, 147}**. The chain (143-144) before the train, because
the train's own advance is the same shape with a stricter gate and the two must not grow two
implementations of it.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 143 | outcome reporting and the structural cycle guard | The mandatory closing report on the §25.6 tool shape — advisory summary, structured payload, never re-parsed prose — plus the source-or-target invariant enforced in the write itself, so a cycle is unrepresentable rather than bounded and two concurrent edits cannot assemble one. Exit: a chain of two fires on a real report; an attempt to point a source at one of its own sources is refused, including under concurrent writes | §38.1, §38.2 |
| 144 | chained enqueue and target conditions | The deterministic server-side enqueue, and the target's own conditions evaluated against the reported payload with skipped and failed distinguished in the run record. §37's write-time refusal covers the condition that can never match; §36's backstop covers the source that never reports — both cited, neither re-solved here | §38.3, §37, §36 |
| 145 | train plan submission and the link state machine | Sub-issue graph read at ingress with the tracker's declared dependency authoritative and the mother's own judgment a fallback only; the mother session that plans once and never writes code; the submission tool on the verdict-tool precedent, validated for self-consistency as well-formedness rather than as a boundary; one row per link with a short-lived claim state, under §2's single-writer discipline. Exit: a parent ticket with sub-issues produces the link rows and spawns the independent ones. **Amended:** the train's members are the **leaf** tickets, each keeping the ancestor chain it came from; a sub-issue that has sub-issues is a container that contributes structure and never a code session, since spawning one produces a duplicate of its children's diffs or an empty PR. A blocking relation declared on a container is inherited by the leaves beneath it and resolved against them -- a dependency on something nothing executes never clears, and the link waiting on it waits forever. Before any session spawns, the submission is **reconciled** against the tracker's live relations: the self-consistency check is well-formedness against the submission itself and cannot see a graph the tracker no longer has. The dangerous direction is a relation the tracker declares and the submission omits, which runs two dependent pieces of work concurrently with nothing reporting an inconsistency. The leaf -> link -> session association is persisted once and is what routes a follow-up message and surfaces progress; recomputing it per caller is how two callers disagree about which session a ticket is on. **Amended:** the claim is a lease -- a session id reserved before creation, a generation, and an expiry. The session create is fenced by the claim's generation, so a holder that wakes after its claim was recovered cannot create. An expired claim is recovered by a periodic sweep as well as by any finder: the reserved session adopted if it exists, the spawn retried a bounded number of times if not, then the link's terminal outcome with the reason. A claim only its holder could release stalls the link the first time a process dies between claiming and spawning, and an unswept one would also block Step 147's replan forever. "Plans once" (§39.1) now reads "plans once per request" (Step 147) | §39.1, §39.2, §39.5b |
| 146 | verdict-gated advance | The successor spawns on §21.2's recomputed `Shippable` and §21's eligibility engine, never on a self-reported risk level, and never on a verdict §21.1 calls stale. The advance is claimed atomically before any asynchronous work, so two overlapping verdicts for one PR produce one spawn and one no-op. A later contradicting verdict stops a running link's work, not merely its row. **Amended:** a link resolves its predecessor and effective base **when it starts**, not at submission -- the graph moves underneath a waiting link (a ticket reparented, a predecessor's PR retargeted) and a base captured at submission stacks the work on a branch chosen against a world that no longer exists. An unresolvable predecessor fails visibly; falling through to the default branch produces a PR that merges cleanly and contains the wrong diff. And a child can finish **without a PR** -- work found unnecessary, or an outright failure -- so there is no review, no verdict, and this gate never fires: every successor then waits forever on a signal that will not come, and from the ticket a train stalled this way looks like a train still working. That link needs a terminal outcome of its own, distinct from blocked, visible on the parent ticket, notified once and idempotently, with its successors released or terminated deliberately rather than left waiting. **Amended:** a predecessor whose pull request a PERSON merged clears the gate whatever its last verdict -- the merge's actor, read from the provider, must be a person, not an App or this system's own auto-merge -- and the successor's base then resolves at start, as above | §39.3, §39.5b |
| 147 ∥ | self-correction before escalation, and the three failure modes | A blocked link re-enters §25's audit-fix edges under `loopguard` and escalates only at the bound — one notice, never repeated — surfaced on the parent ticket, which carries the context a human needs. Plus the three §39.5 modes designed against rather than discovered: plan mode arriving in front of the train, the chain head's base, and the deferral gate that would otherwise starve it. **Amended:** an explicit human Stop is broader than the verdict-driven stop of §39.3 -- it has to reach the links that are only **waiting** and own no session to interrupt, as well as the children running, since stopping only what runs leaves a train that resumes itself on the next verdict. A stopped or closed link also retires its message routing: a follow-up arriving afterwards has no session to reach, and delivering it to the last one known is worse than reporting there is none. **Amended:** Resume is Stop's inverse -- an explicit, audited human action releasing the links still waiting, never one whose ticket closed. Replanning is adopted (owner decision, 2026-09-28): a human's explicit request re-invokes the mother, whose second submission for the same parent replaces the links not yet started; a started link's ticket, session and branch stay immutable, and a replan is refused while any spawn is claimed (Step 145's sweep keeps an abandoned claim from blocking it). An agent advancing the train itself is rejected: the train advances on server-observed facts only | §39.4, §39.5, §39.5b, §24.8 |

**Phase 14 milestone**: a parent ticket carrying sub-issues with a declared dependency between two
of them produces one pull request per sub-issue, the independent ones opened at once and the
dependent one opened only after its predecessor's *recomputed* verdict clears the gate — with the
train visibly paused, and its parent ticket told why, when a predecessor's verdict does not clear
it even after the auto-fix loop reached its bound. An automation reporting a structured outcome
starts its target with that payload in hand; a target whose conditions do not match is recorded
skipped and distinguishable from one that failed; and an attempt to make one automation both source
and target of a chain is refused at the write, including when two edits race.

## Phase 15 — Autonomy guardrails (4 Steps, additive)

The four controls that make the autonomy this system already grants bounded and legible. Technical
plan §40 is the normative specification: a spend cap that refuses the next turn (§40.1), one persisted
freeze every automatic action consults (§40.2), session-level bounds in the timeout ladder (§40.3),
and one per-repository level that constrains the automation-enabling toggles rather than replacing
them (§40.4).

What binds them is the question each answers, and that no shipped Step answers: not "is this loop
bounded" — every loop is — but "how much may this session spend", "stop everything automatic, now",
"how long may a session run", and "what is this repository allowed to do". Read against the levels of
autonomy this product is positioned in, the first is a Level 3 prerequisite the plan did not meet, and
the second is the sentence Level 4 rests on: a human can still step in.

On placement: by content Steps 148-150 are Phase 2 and Phase 5 control work and 151 is a Phase 7
Settings row. Appended rather than folded in, for the reason Phases 8 through 14 each give for
themselves. Not filed in Phase 11 either, under that phase's own rule: nothing here was declared
missing by the Step that shipped without it — these were found by reading the shipped controls
against an external definition of what a control is.

Execution order: **{148, 149, 150} → 151**. The first three are independent of one another and of
Phase 14; 151 is last because its milestone reads the other three. 150 adds a session timer kind
(`session_deadline`); Phase 11's Step 222, which bounds a kind a binary does not know (technical
plan §2), has shipped, so a rollback past 150 needs no down migration for that kind unless its wall
clock can come near a day (row 150 says how), and this phase waits on no Phase 11 row.

**One constraint this phase places on Phase 14, in either direction**: §38.3's chain enqueue and
§39.3's train advance consult the freeze. If Phase 14 ships first, Step 149 wires them; if Step 149
ships first, Steps 144 and 146 consult it before they ship. §40.6 states the rule; this records that
it binds whichever Step comes second.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 148 | the spend cap | `repo_settings.session_spend_cap_usd` and `automations.session_spend_cap_usd` (NULL = no cap; zero or less refused at write time per §37); checked at the turn-creation chokepoint against `SUM(cost_usd)` over the session's turns — derived, never a counter column; a refused turn leaves the session waiting on a human, never failed: persisted `warning`, one outbox notice, an audited raise re-admits. Applies to every turn regardless of who asked (§40.1's inversion of §24.6, stated in the code comment). Exit: a session at its cap refuses the next turn with the typed reason, and takes it after the cap is raised; overshoot bounded by one turn's own spend, documented not hidden **Amended:** an absolute cap is not a no-progress budget. Measure active time, cost and attempts since the last OBSERVABLE progress -- a PR created or merged as confirmed by the provider, or an explicit human resumption -- and enforce it server-side even when the agent never calls its own tool. An agent's self-declared success must not reset the server's counter. Keep this separate from the absolute ceilings rather than folding one into the other. **Amended:** the attempts in that window are the turns the server dispatched, never a count the agent reports through a tool: such a counter is one the uncooperative agent never consumes and the diligent one exhausts. Because expiry lets the turn in flight end rather than cancelling it, no report-before-cancel window is needed here. | §40.1, §40.3, §25.15, §37 |
| 149 | the freeze | One persisted platform-wide boolean, admin-only, audited as `autonomy.frozen`/`autonomy.unfrozen`; consulted at the automatic-action sites §40.2 lists and at no human command; each site records `skipped` with reason `frozen`, distinguishable from `failed`, consuming nothing — a **call-site** check, deliberately not a query exclusion, with the reason in the code comment. Decision-inbox banner. Exit: with candidates pending, no merge, no auto-fix spawn, no re-review enqueue and no automation invocation occurs while frozen; every candidate is still a candidate after unfreeze; no running turn is severed | §40.2, §32.8, §16 |
| 150 ∥ | session bounds | `MaxTurnsPerSession` and `SessionWallClock` in `platform/timeouts.go` and nowhere else, human and automation values, `SessionWallClock > turn_deadline` in the invariant test; turn count at the chokepoint as `COUNT(*)`; wall-clock as the sixth named timer `session_deadline`; a bound reached closes the session to new turns, lets the in-flight one end, and derives the terminal status with a new named reason through the transition table — never `failed`. Audited per-session extension. `session_deadline` is a new session timer kind; Phase 11's Step 222 has shipped, so a rollback past this Step leaves an older binary bounding that kind by the time since its row was last armed (§2) -- backed off past the grace, deleted a day after its last arm -- rather than redelivering it every claim window for good, and no down migration needs to delete its rows. One exception, which §2 states: `session_deadline` is armed when the session starts and due at `SessionWallClock`, so its lead is that bound. With a lead of about a day or more (`UnknownTimerDeleteAfter` less the pump's delivery delay), a binary that does not know the kind deletes it at its first delivery, and with a lead `L` it survives only about `UnknownTimerDeleteAfter` minus `L` after a rollback. So if `SessionWallClock` can come near a day, the Step re-arms `session_deadline` with a lead well under `UnknownTimerDeleteAfter` -- ideally at most `UnknownTimerGrace` -- or ships the down migration. Exit: an automation-created session stops at its bound with the reason persisted and the last turn intact | §40.3, §5.4, §2, §3.1 |
| 151 | the autonomy level | `repo_settings.autonomy_level` ∈ {`assisted`, `conditional`, `high`}, default `conditional`, migration backfilling `high` where auto-merge or sentinel auto-fix is already on (no behavior changes at migration); a ceiling on the toggles, not a replacement: forbidden toggles refused at write time naming the level, lowering turns off every toggle above it in one transaction with one audit row each, raising arms nothing; the permitted sets as a table in `internal/domain` with an exhaustiveness test over every automation-enabling `repo_settings` column (§13.3's matrix discipline). Settings row (admin-only, §13.3), `review_verdicts` analytics sliced by level. Exit: one field answers what a repository may do; a forbidden toggle is refused; a lowered level leaves one audit record per consequence | §40.4, §21.1, §21.2, §13.3 |

**Phase 15 milestone**: a session at its cap or bound stops taking turns without being marked failed,
with the reason persisted and the last turn intact, and resumes on an audited raise; the freeze stops
every automatic action at once without severing a running turn or losing a single candidate, and a
human command still works while it holds; a repository's level answers what it may do in one field,
refuses a toggle above it at the write, and leaves one audit record per consequence when lowered.
§9.3 scenario 20 green — gating this phase, on the Phase 4 precedent. Phase KPI: verdict precision
(§21.1) reported per level from the first repository moved.

## Phase 17 — Claims parity (4 Steps, additive)

What `docs/FOUNDATIONS.md` says in the present tense, made true, and then kept true. Technical plan
§41 is the normative specification. Read against the code on 2026-09-12, three of the document's
sentences were true of the plan and false of the binary: the control plane is "packaged as standard
containers" (no Dockerfile, chart or manifest exists for it), the platform "natively supports Modal
and RWX" (`rwx.Provider` implements the port; `controlplane/serve.go` constructs `modal.New`
unconditionally), and sign-in offers "pluggable OIDC SSO" (`inbound/auth` is GitHub OAuth only, and
says so). The sign-in view was honest — its SSO button is rendered "disabled today". The document
was not, and could not have been caught, because it lived outside the repository.

Not Phase 11, under that phase's own rule: Step 57 never declared RWX unwired, and no Step ever owned
control-plane packaging. Only the OIDC gap qualifies, and one document's corrections belong in one
phase with one exit.

Execution order: **{162, 163, 164} → 165**. The first three are independent; 165 is last because it
cites them. Step 163 is a prerequisite of Phase 18's Step 166 — the Kubernetes provider is a third
value of the switch 163 builds, never a second selection mechanism. Independent of Phase 16 in both
directions.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 162 ∥ | control-plane packaging | Multi-stage `Dockerfile` at the root running the exact `make dist` recipe (`web-build` + `go build -tags web_assets`), distroless final stage, non-root; CI publishes to the GitHub container registry on every tag with tag and SHA as labels — the two values the boot fingerprint already logs; `deploy/control-plane/` with `Deployment`, `Service` and a `Secret` template naming every required `platform.Config` variable, and nothing else — no Helm, because `platform.Config`'s boot validation is the one configuration surface (§37's principle). Exit: the image, against the compose Postgres, serves the Phase 12 golden route table; `docker run` unconfigured fails with `platform.Config`'s own message, not a stack trace | §41.1 |
| 163 ∥ | sandbox provider selection | `NARVI_SANDBOX_PROVIDER` ∈ {`modal`, `rwx`}, required, validated at boot together with the chosen provider's own variables (missing names in the refusal); `serve.go`'s one construction site becomes a switch, no other file learns the name; value in the boot fingerprint and every spawn log; `kubernetes` reserved for §42 and not constructed here. The RWX real-binary test leaves `t.Skip` behind a CI job carrying the pinned binary and `RWX_ACCESS_TOKEN`, settling `Resume` empirically — Step 57's first exit criterion, never run. One external dependency, named: an RWX account; without it the switch ships, the test stays skipped, and the document says "selectable", not "supported". Exit: one end-to-end session per configured provider through the same binary; `rwx` without a token refuses to boot naming the variable **DÉCIDÉ (2026-09-17) : oui, le projet prend un compte RWX.** Le test contre le binaire réel perd son `t.Skip` et part dans un job CI portant le binaire épinglé et `RWX_ACCESS_TOKEN`, ce qui règle EMPIRIQUEMENT le comportement de `Resume` — premier critère de sortie du Step 57, jamais exécuté une seule fois depuis qu'il existe. Le document peut dire « supporté » et non « sélectionnable ». Écartées : livrer sans compte (honnête, et c'est exactement la discipline de la phase 17, mais laisse `Resume` non mesuré et la phase 18 fermée) ; reporter avec un déclencheur (même état, avec un suivi propre). Ce que la décision ouvre : les phases 17 ET 18, la seconde étant gatée sur celle-ci | §41.2 |
| 164 ∥ | OIDC sign-in | `GET /auth/oidc/login` + `/callback` beside the GitHub pair; discovery via `.well-known/openid-configuration`, code flow with PKCE, `nonce`/`state` on the existing pre-auth cookie; `NARVI_OIDC_ISSUER`/`CLIENT_ID`/`CLIENT_SECRET` all-or-none at boot, unset = today's disabled button; `email_verified` required true or refused; same allowlist, same default role, same `users` row; `identities` gains provider `oidc` with issuer-qualified `external_id` by migration; the push and the PR use the session creator's GitHub token (§8.11), so a session whose creator has no linked GitHub identity cannot push until the creator links GitHub through the ordinary flow: the push is not attempted and the session records a warning saying so. **As shipped:** the bot-credential fallback this row first named was built and removed in review, because it gave a GitHub-less member the bot's write access on a branch of their choosing; whether a bounded App credential may push for such a session is an open decision under Step 195 (`docs/DECISIONS.md`, §44.4); §13.1's "configuration, not code" amended in place. Exit: a user with no GitHub account signs in through a real IdP, lands on the inbox with the allowlist's role, appears once in Members, and a second sign-in creates no second user; a token without `email_verified` is refused with the audited reason | §41.3, §13.1 |
| 165 | the document and its drift test | `docs/FOUNDATIONS.md` joins the repository; every bullet ends in exactly one of a citation `(path/file.go:Symbol)` or `[planned §N]`; `internal/ops` gains `TestFoundationsClaims` on the `sectionref.go`/`guidedrift.go` model — neither marker fails, both fail, a dangling citation fails, an unknown section fails. Ships the two wording corrections (transitions through the actor, creation under the same row lock; the fence rejects inside the transaction under a row lock) and the mode B rewrite ("specified behind a ranking port, deliberately not built until mode A's measurement says so" — §31.9's decision, never overturned to make a sentence true). Exit: the test fails on a synthetic dangling bullet and passes on the shipped document | §41.4 |

**Phase 17 milestone**: the published image serves the golden route table; one binary runs a session
on either configured provider, selected by one variable; a user with no GitHub account signs in
through a real IdP; and `go test ./internal/ops/` fails on a dangling citation in
`docs/FOUNDATIONS.md` and passes on the shipped one — every present-tense sentence in it resolves to
a file that exists.

## Phase 18 — Kubernetes-native sandbox provider (5 Steps, additive, gated on Step 163)

The third `SandboxProvider`, and the last component of this system that still leaves the operator's
cluster. Technical plan §42 is the normative specification. The adapter is the small part: the port
has held for two implementations, `Capabilities()` already lets a third declare what it lacks, and
`sandbox-agent` dials out, so no pod ever needs an inbound route. The substance is the three
decisions a pod needs before it may be called a sandbox — what runs it (§42.3: the RuntimeClass is
the boundary; Kata by default, gVisor where the pool cannot run a hypervisor, `runc` never,
fail-closed on an absent class), what it may reach (§42.4: FQDN-capable egress policy, or
`EgressPolicy: false` and §27.6's fail-closed refusal), and how long it lives (§42.2: Narvi sets
`activeDeadlineSeconds` itself — the first provider whose hard cap is Narvi's own value).

One thing this phase says about itself that a green badge would otherwise imply falsely (§42.6): CI
runs `kind`, `kind` cannot run Kata, so CI proves the lifecycle and not the boundary. Step 167's exit
is an acceptance run on the target cluster, recorded with the RuntimeClass handler, node type and
date. The first fact that run establishes is whether the node pool can run a hypervisor at all —
bare metal or nested virtualization — which is a fact about the cluster, and the one that decides
between Kata and gVisor.

Execution order: **166 → 167 → 168 → {169, 170}**. 166 is gated on Step 163. 167 and 168 cannot be
skipped by declaring capabilities false: without 167 the provider is a shared kernel; without 168,
§27.6 leaves every `allowlist` Environment unspawnable here. 169 and 170 gate nothing.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 166 | the adapter, on `kind` | `internal/adapters/outbound/kubernetes` implementing the nine methods: one pod per `(session, gen)`, `sbx-{session-short}-{gen}`, labels `narvi.dev/session`/`narvi.dev/gen`, dedicated namespace and a ServiceAccount scoped to pods and secrets there; `SESSION_CONFIG` as one owner-referenced `Secret` surfaced as the single env var; `activeDeadlineSeconds` from the new `KubernetesPodActiveDeadline` in `platform/timeouts.go`, asserted in §5.4's chain as the provider hard cap and stamping §35's `lifetime_deadline_at` exactly; mandatory requests/limits; the full security context (`automountServiceAccountToken: false`, non-root, no privilege escalation, all capabilities dropped, `RuntimeDefault` seccomp, no host namespaces, read-only root except sized `emptyDir`s) in a namespace labelled PSS `restricted`; `Capabilities{Snapshots: false, Resume: false, ImageBuilds: false, ExplicitStop: true}`, Docker unsupported; errors classified by `StatusReason`, unknown → transient; `List` by label, orphan GC unchanged, a test that no `Secret` outlives its pod; `NARVI_SANDBOX_PROVIDER=kubernetes` as the switch's third value. Exit: §9.3 scenario 21's lifecycle half green on `kind` in CI | §42.1, §42.2, §42.5 |
| 167 | the boundary: RuntimeClass, fail-closed | `NARVI_K8S_RUNTIME_CLASS` required, no default; the adapter reads the `RuntimeClass` at boot and refuses to start if absent — never a fall-through to `runc`; handler → `Capabilities.IsolationClass` ∈ {`vm`, `user-kernel`, `shared-kernel`}; `shared-kernel` refused unless `NARVI_K8S_ALLOW_SHARED_KERNEL=1`, an escape hatch for `kind` and laptops that stamps the boot fingerprint and is listed in `PRODUCTION_CHECKLIST.md` as must-be-unset; §27.5's dispatch check reads the typed value. **First exit criterion, empirical, on the RWX precedent**: an acceptance run on the target cluster — the node pool's hypervisor capability established, a Kata pod boots the sandbox image and passes the boot contract, or the run records why the pool cannot and gVisor is the class. Recorded with handler, node type and date | §42.3, §42.6 |
| 168 | egress: FQDN policy | `NARVI_K8S_EGRESS_POLICY` ∈ {`cilium-fqdn`, `none`}; with `cilium-fqdn`, one `CiliumNetworkPolicy` per pod — default-deny egress, DNS to the cluster resolver, the server-appended floor (control-plane Service, git hosts by FQDN), the Environment's allowlist by FQDN, all ingress denied — and `EgressPolicy: true`; with `none`, `false`, and §27.6's fail-closed rule refuses an `allowlist` Environment on this provider. The adapter does not claim to verify the CNI enforces the policy — it cannot — so the value is an operator assertion in the production checklist beside the RuntimeClass; §27.6 amended to say "FQDN-capable" and why. Exit: from inside a spawned sandbox on the acceptance cluster, the control plane and the git host are reachable and a host outside the allowlist is not; scenario 21's egress half green | §42.4 |
| 169 ∥ | in-cluster image builds | `BuildImage` as a BuildKit `Job` in the provider's namespace pushing to an in-cluster registry, `DeleteImage` through the registry API, `ImageBuilds: true`; §19's fingerprint, freshness pump and fall-back-to-base unchanged — they never knew which provider built an image. Gates nothing; until it lands, this provider runs the configured base image with `setup.sh` on every fresh spawn, §4.1.1's RWX posture. Exit: a warm boot from a prebuilt image on this provider, measured against §19.2's staleness window | §42.5, §19 |
| 170 ∥ | previews | Per-session `Service` + `Ingress` rule on `{session}.{NARVI_K8S_PREVIEW_DOMAIN}`, created on `push_complete` through §4.1.2's outbox kind and deleted with the pod; per-repo opt-in, off by default (§24.5); UI copy stating an in-cluster preview is reachable only where the operator exposes the wildcard. Gates nothing. Exit: a `preview` artifact on a Kubernetes-provider session resolves to the pushed commit's app | §42.5, §4.1.2 |

**Phase 18 milestone**: §9.3 scenario 21 green on `kind` — a pod deleted mid-turn respawns under a
new gen with the old gen's `Secret` gone and the same turn resumed; an absent RuntimeClass refuses
boot naming the class; an `allowlist` Environment on `EgressPolicy: false` refuses dispatch with the
typed reason — and one acceptance run on the target cluster, recorded with the RuntimeClass handler,
node type and date, in which a sandbox reaches the control plane and its git host and nothing else.
The boundary is proven there and nowhere else, and the phase says so.

## Sequencing & parallelism

- **Parallel streams in phase 1**: control-plane (07-08, 09-12, 18-20) ∥ sandbox-agent (13-17) — converge at 21.
- **Phase 3**: 32/33/34 parallel after 31; 36-38 after 35.
- **Phase 4**: 40 → 41 → 42 → 43 are sequential (each builds on the prior Step's own schema/behavior change; 43 needs 42's `workspaceMoved`/manifest plumbing, but is not telemetry-gated on it — §19.6/§19.9 record why). 44 has no dependency on 40-43 or on any Phase 3 Step beyond Step 17 (OpenCode adapter) — it may run in parallel with any of them, or with Phase 5.
- **Phase 5**: 53 (provider credential injection) is a blocking prerequisite for 54-56 —
  per-step/per-turn model override is inert without it, and the design treats this gap as blocking
  for the whole chantier, not standalone scope. 54 (dark: domain/workflow + loopguard + schema, no
  dispatch wiring) depends only on 53. 55 (execution engine) depends on 54's schema and is
  exercised by 100% of production traffic from day one. 56 (HITL gate + circuit breaker) depends
  on 55 (the same step-transition hook) and reuses the Slack/Linear notifier extension points, not
  new channels. 61 is independent (no dependency on the other new Steps or on 45-52, 57-60). 62
  depends on 45 (verdict shape) and 47 (posting path) — the verdict schema is designed once,
  before persistence/analytics/digest/auto-approval build on it. 63 depends on 47 (needs the
  posting path new patterns get weighed against), 39 (`Authorize`), and, for its content-anchored
  finding positioning (§22.1.1), the `LLM` port (§4.3) already shipped for Step 36's classifier —
  no new port. 64 depends on 36
  (classifier) and 37-38 (plan mode, the dispatch point it amends). 65 depends on 46 (the
  claim/coalescing primitives it extends with a second, automatic ingress lane) and 62
  (`review_verdicts.head_sha`, its trigger-state source) — it adds a parallel path onto Step 46's
  own primitives, never a mechanism of its own. 66 (merge-readout digest) depends on 45/47
  (verdict shape + posting path) and 62 (persistence — the instrument that measures it); 67
  depends on 66 (the diff-derived summary is its adequacy reference); 68 is independent of 66-67
  (depth-based model/effort tiering is valuable alone) but precedes 69, whose deep path must exist
  to route to; 69 depends on 66 and 68, uses 53's credential injection for cross-family sub-agent
  pinning, and measures on 62's instrument. 70 (cost-budget wiring) depends on 69 (the mechanism
  §26.7/§26.9 specify) and, for the accumulator's own production call site, on 53's sandbox-side
  orchestration seam. 71 (post-hoc sub-task corroboration) depends on 69 (`CounterReview`'s own
  schema) and §7.1's already-shipped ack-guaranteed event persistence — no new port either.
- **Phase 6**: 72 (secrets + opencode config) extends Step 53's mechanisms and needs Step 39's
  `Authorize`; 73 (cloud identity + kubeconfig) builds on 72's delivery family (and its `static`
  kubeconfig rung stores through 72's table); 74 (docker/egress/toolchain) is the ports/substrate
  piece (Steps 12-16's seams, §19's image pipeline adjacency) and may run in parallel with 73. 75
  (config/data seeding) seeds the surfaces 72-74 build, so it follows them.
- **Phase 7** can start 79-80 during phase 6 (backend and contracts are frozen by then). 88
  (workflow definition & run API) is a blocking prerequisite for both 91 (the canvas that edits
  those definitions) and 93 (the run view that reads them) — neither has anything to call without
  it — and depends on 54-56, the schema, engine and HITL gate it exposes. 90 (integrations read
  model & preview config) is likewise a blocking prerequisite for 92, and additionally closes the
  `rwx_preview_*` gap 89 shipped an honest "not configurable here" notice for. 91 also leans on 86
  (Settings, whose shell it reuses). 94 (ui finalize) must stay last: its screenshot review is
  against everything before it.

  **A note on the two backend Steps.** They are Phase 7 Steps rather than deferred to Phase 11
  because they block scheduled work — that is the whole line between the two. Each was found by
  checking a UI row against the REST surface it assumes, which is worth doing for a row BEFORE
  starting it: three of the four remaining rows did not match the code.
- **Phase 8**: 97 and 98 both depend on 96 (the resolver and its egress capability token) and may
  run in parallel with each other. 99 (OS isolation) is independent of 96-98 and of 100 — a
  platform-wide hardening valid in live mode, parallelizable with any of them, but required
  before 101 for that Step's guarantee statement to hold (§30.4/§30.5 are a deliberate
  conjunction). 100 (GitHub App plumbing) is likewise independent of 96-99 but a **hard
  prerequisite for 101**: the shadow mint substitutes an App-minted read-only installation token,
  and 101's fail-closed scope introspection rides 100's plumbing. 101 depends on 96 (the flag), 100
  (the token), and 99 (the isolation half of its guarantee). 102 depends on 96-97 (the
  mandatory-gate constructors it extends to Slack/Linear). 103 depends on 97 (the ledger its
  entries land in) and 101 (the persisted per-turn push decision it honors). 104 reads the ledger
