  97-98 populate and extends Step 86's Settings surfaces, so it comes last. Phase 8 may execute
  any time after Phase 5's milestone — nothing in 96-103 waits on Phase 6 or 7 (§10-P8); only 104's
  Settings surface waits on Step 86.
- **Phase 9**: 105 is independent of everything else in the phase (and of Phases 6-8) — it may run
  first or in parallel. 106 is independent of 105 and 107 and gates only 109-110; it presumes the
  in-flight four-handler authorization fix has landed (a dependency, not a Step). 107 needs Phase
  5's milestone (the three seams, Step 62's persistence, Step 69's §26.5 instrument), Step 98's
  verdict egress-mode stamp (its SELECT's shadow-epoch exclusion rides it, §31.8), and the
  in-flight G1 write-path sanitization; 108 additionally needs Step 96's resolver (its corpus
  rows' own `captured_live` stamp);
  it does not need 105 or 106. 108 needs 107 (the pipeline whose SELECT it swaps, the instrument
  its A/B reads) and **its start is gated on reading 107's baseline window** — a calendar gate,
  not a code dependency, but one that may resolve to "never": if that readout kills engagement,
  108 does not execute at all, and its whole deliverable is the recorded kill decision (§31.9,
  §31.10). When 108 does ship, it also extends Step 102's Activate for graduation — the barrier
  logic is part of 108's own (engaged-branch) content, wired into Activate when both exist; under
  a kill decision there is no mode B graduation surface to extend. 109 needs 106 and 108. 110
  needs 106 and 108 — the export renders from 108's corpus tables and serves only promoted,
  live-epoch content, so the promotion state it filters on does not exist before 108. Both 109 and
  110 are therefore unreachable under a kill decision, same as 108 itself. 109 and 110 may run in
  parallel with each other.
- Go/no-go after Step 21 (~1 month).

## Verification

- Each Step: CI (lint, `go test -race`, contract tests) + its own criterion listed on its row.
- Phase milestones = blocking gates: e2e via API/UI (P1), 12 resilience scenarios (P2), classifier shadow report (P3), review-verdict diff reviewed for precision (P5), flag-reversible rollout (P6), 9 views built to mockups + screenshot review (P7), a zero-egress evaluation run on a live customer repository verified against the suppression ledger (P8 — and P8 gates any customer-repo attachment, whatever the numeric order suggests), mode A live on all three seams with the mode-stamped KPI reporting and Step 107's baseline readout read with its three-way decision recorded (§31.9) (P9). Phase 4 (Steps 40-44) is additive scope, not a blocking gate — its own milestone verifies warm-boot behavior and compaction-retry recovery but never holds up Phase 5. Phases 10, 11 and 12 are appended scope on the same footing: Phase 10 additive (Step 111 closes P6's own criterion, Step 112 gates nothing), Phase 11 scheduled in full with a milestone of its own, Phase 12 additive — each has a gate, none holds up a phase before it. Phase 13 likewise: additive, gated on its own two §9.3 scenarios, which extend that catalogue rather than reopening Phase 2's closed criterion. Phase 14 is additive too, and the only phase gated on another: Steps 141 and 142 must ship before either capability it adds. Phase 15 is additive, gated on §9.3 scenario 20, and binds Phase 14 in one respect: chain enqueue and train advance consult the freeze, wired by whichever of Step 149 or Steps 144 and 146 ships second.
- Phases 16 to 21: each phase's own milestone block is its gate (Phase 19, which has none, row by row).
- Project end: `make dist` produces the standalone `narvi` binary; all phase gates green.

## Phase 19 — Adopted product capabilities (5 Steps, additive)

Capabilities adopted by an explicit owner decision on 2026-09-17 (188: 2026-09-18), filed here because adopting is
what creates the work and a decision with no row is a decision that drifts out of tracking.

**The grouping is provenance, not subject.** These five share an owner decision and
nothing else; they are neither a coherent feature nor a dependency chain, and none gates another.
The phase exists so they are scheduled rather than floating, in the same way Phases 17 and 18 were
appended rather than folded into a phase they did not belong to. Each may land independently, in
any order, whenever its own dependencies allow.

The decisions themselves, with the alternatives rejected and why, live in `docs/DECISIONS.md`.

| # | Step | What it is | § |
|---|---|---|---|
| 184 | secrets reachable from a newly created automation | The path a person actually takes: having just created an automation, reach the secrets it will need without navigating away and losing the context. Reuses the EXISTING secret scopes -- a scope meaning "secret belonging to one automation" would be an extension of the model and a **separate decision**, named here so it is never smuggled in as an implementation detail of this one. Also draws the boundary this surface keeps blurring: personal settings and deployment settings become visually and permissively distinct, each with the permissions §13 already defines, because a person editing what they believe is their own preference and changing the deployment's is a mistake the interface invites rather than one they make. **The existence of a Settings area and a secrets store is not this feature**, and the temptation to read it as already covered is exactly what the decision record warns against. Exit: a newly created automation reaches its secrets in one path, and a change to a deployment setting is refused for a caller who may only edit their own. **Amended (2026-09-17, owner decision): this Step also closes the `automation` secret scope, which has been SCHEMA-ONLY since migration 000090.** That column shipped deliberately so the follow-up would need no second migration, and the follow-up is this: nothing writes an automation-scoped row (no CRUD endpoint exists) and `sandboxsecretsdelivery.go` never resolves a candidate at that scope, so the value is reserved vocabulary rather than a capability. Both ends land here -- CRUD at the automation scope, and candidate resolution in the delivery path -- because a scope written but never resolved is the same shape as a value written in one place and read in none. The decision the register named as separate has been taken WITH this one, and the row says so rather than letting the separation persist as a caveat nobody reconciles. Exit, second half: a secret created at the automation scope is delivered to that automation's session and to no other | §27.1, §27.3, §12.2, §13 |
| 185 | browser-side error capture and user feedback | Server traces and metrics do not cover browser-side failures -- the existing OTel/OTLP work is not partial coverage of this, it is coverage of something else, and reading it as coverage would be a capability claimed by adjacency. A configurable endpoint, source maps, and association with the correct release: **a stack trace that cannot be symbolicated against the release it came from is a diagnostic that reads as present and is not**, which is this repository's own recurring defect wearing a front-end costume. User feedback rides the same path so a report and the errors around it are one record rather than two nobody joins. Exit: an error thrown by a released build resolves to a symbolicated frame naming the release, and a report submitted from the same session is retrievable beside it | §32.7, §33 |
| 186 | session replay | Adopted as its own decision, deliberately separate from 185, because it is a choice about other people's data rather than about instrumentation. Three things to settle before any of it ships: what is masked and how the masking is verified (an unmasked replay is not a default to inherit); how it is activated, and by whom; and where the data goes for each deployment shape, since a self-hoster and a hosted deployment do not have the same answer. Depends on 185's release association to be worth anything -- a replay that cannot be tied to the build it recorded is a video of an unknown program. Exit: a replay of a session containing credentials and customer prose shows neither, verified by an assertion rather than by inspection | §32.7, §33 |
| 187 | automation env vars reach the process, not just the prompt | `automations.env_vars` has existed since migration 000055 and is written by the API and read at run time -- but `buildRunPrompt` delivers it as a **prompt preamble**, `NAME=value` lines prepended to the prompt text. The agent READS the values; the process does not carry them, so `$VAR` resolves to nothing in a shell the agent runs and `os.Getenv` returns empty. `settings.go`'s own comment records this as intended for the non-sensitive config it was built for and names the follow-up. **The mechanism it waits on already exists**: `internal/sandboxagent/opencodeproc/spawn.go` populates `cmd.Env` from two sources today -- provider credentials (Step 53) and sandbox secrets (§27.1) -- with an explicit append order between them. So this is a third source on a built path, not a new mechanism. Decide and record where automation env vars sit in that order, because the order is what resolves a collision, and a third contributor that lands wherever it happens to be appended makes the existing ordering rationale false. **Keep the preamble**: the two are not exclusive and §8.4's own note says so -- an agent that must KNOW a feature-flag name and a shell that must RESOLVE it are different needs. Exit: a value set on an automation is visible to `os.Getenv` inside that automation's session, still appears in the prompt preamble, and a name colliding with a provider credential or a sandbox secret resolves the way the recorded order says -- asserted, not assumed | §25.1, §27.1, §8.4 |
| 188 | human acceptance of a verdict the engine refuses | Split out of Step 174 (2026-09-18) and starting after it, since an acceptance identifies what it accepts by the check the publisher creates. An authorisation, never an override, and §21.1b states the contract this implements. It binds to ONE verdict, ONE attempt and ONE context: a new attempt, a moved base or a changed ancestor chain makes it inapplicable, exactly as they make the verdict stale -- an acceptance that survives the thing it accepted is an approval of code nobody looked at. It carries author, justification and auditable revocation, and the four answers §13 requires of every privileged action: who may do it, through which surface and API, under which role, and how it is audited. **It never rewrites the assessment.** The risk level stands and the conditions that are not about human judgment -- CI green at the current head, blast radius known, sensitive paths -- stay mandatory, because an acceptance that silently reclassifies a verdict as low-risk destroys §21.1's analytics and the contradiction-rate signal §21.2 is calibrated on. Exit: an accepted verdict merges while a moved base makes the same acceptance inapplicable without a human touching it, and the analytics still show the original risk level | §13, §21.1, §21.1b, §21.2 |

## Phase 20: GitHub App pool (8 Steps, additive)

A pool of GitHub Apps, adopted by owner decision on 2026-09-25 without waiting for a measured
saturation, and filed here because a decision with no row drifts out of tracking. Technical plan §44
is the normative specification.

Three of its Steps repair shipped behavior, which is why they are in this phase rather than in
Phase 11: the Steps after them write to GitHub through them. Step 190 makes the formal review a
verdict posts idempotent, pinned to the commit it reviewed, and no longer blocking once a newer whole
verdict exists while it is still pending; Step 191 honors GitHub's own rate-limit deadlines; both come
first. Step 193, which needs the registry (Step 192), gives the `narvi/review` check the credential it
has lacked: GitHub lets only an App create a check run, the only App this
deployment mints for is refused at boot unless it is read-only, and an installation token pasted in
the static bot token's place stops working after the hour GitHub gives it, so granting `checks:write`
to an installation does not fix it on its own (§44.1).

What the pool is not, in this plan: a promise of capacity. GitHub's terms forbid sharing API tokens
to exceed its rate limits, and whether several Apps for one workload fall under that is GitHub's call;
adding Apps for headroom is the operator's choice (owner decision, 2026-09-28), stated in Step 197's
runbook beside that sentence. No Step depends on it.

On placement: appended, for the reason Phases 8 through 19 each give for themselves.

Execution order: **190 → 191 → 192 → {193, 194} → {195, 196} → 197**. 190 comes first because 191
stops counting rate-limited attempts and a non-idempotent review would then be posted more often, not
less. 192 is dark until 193 and 194 consume it. 193 already brings a second App, so it keeps that
App's repository-event deliveries unprocessed until 196 designates a receiver; 197 reads everything
before it.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 190 | a verdict's formal review is posted twice, on the wrong commit, or blocking after it was replaced | `VerdictNotifier.Deliver` (`internal/adapters/outbound/githubapi/verdictnotifier.go`) submits the formal review, whose body is the verdict, and then syncs the risk labels in one outbox delivery, and three defects share that write. **Twice:** when the label sync fails, the retry repeats both, and `CreateReview` is not idempotent -- the doc comment says so and calls it an accepted limitation shared with the other notifiers -- so a label call that keeps failing (a missing permission, a secondary rate limit) leaves up to `MaxAttempts` (10) copies of one review. **On the wrong commit:** `CreateReview` sends only `body` and `event`, no `commit_id`, so GitHub attaches the review to whatever the pull request's head is at delivery, not the head the verdict reviewed. **Blocking after it was replaced:** the payload carries neither attempt nor context, so nothing stops a `REQUEST_CHANGES` from landing after a newer verdict replaced it. Fix: split the review from the label sync so a failure in the second cannot repeat the first; post with `commit_id` set to the reviewed head; make the write create-once, finding the review it already posted by a marker in its body naming the verdict's own row (so a second, different verdict from the same turn is never taken for the first), matched only on reviews authored by one of this deployment's own logins and reading every page of the listing, before re-posting; and carry the attempt, the context and the verdict's kind (§21.1b). The listing, the create and its fallback fit together within `OutboxDeliveryTimeout`. **Unlike the check (§21.1b), a formal review is never refused as superseded**: it publishes, as history, and only its blocking force follows verdicts. A pending `REQUEST_CHANGES` is posted as `COMMENT` only when a newer WHOLE verdict for the pull request has been recorded, and never because of a newer attempt that recorded no whole verdict (it failed or never started) or of a narrowed or delta verdict (§21.1b's kinds). A narrowed verdict's own review is always a `COMMENT`, never `REQUEST_CHANGES`: an answer to part of the question never blocks the whole. A verdict from a turn that recorded no head is narrowed (Step 212), and its `COMMENT` review names no commit and says so. A commit no longer part of the pull request makes GitHub refuse `commit_id`; that verdict is recorded as not published with the reason, never re-posted on the current head. The §24.6 budget-exhausted notice rides the same write with no attempt: create-once by its own marker, never blocking. The other non-idempotent writes (`BotNotifier`'s comments) take the same receipt, enumerated in the Step. Stated limit: Narvi posts no `APPROVE` and dismisses nothing, so a `REQUEST_CHANGES` already on GitHub stays until a person dismisses it (Step 174's row names the same limit); this rule covers a review still pending. Exit: a label sync failing on every attempt leaves exactly one formal review; the review names the commit the verdict reviewed even when the head moved before delivery; a newer attempt that failed leaves the older blocking review posting as blocking; a newer recorded whole verdict turns it into a comment; a narrowed verdict never posts `REQUEST_CHANGES`; a review whose response was dropped after GitHub accepted it is not re-posted | §21.1b, §5.1, §8.2 |
| 191 | GitHub's deadlines, honored end to end | `githubapi.APIError` carries a boolean `RateLimited` and no deadline. `isRateLimitedResponse` sets it on a 403 only, from `x-ratelimit-remaining`, the presence of `retry-after` and, as a fallback, the message text, then discards the values; a 429 is never marked; and `RemoveLabel` builds its error without computing the boolean at all. The outbox retries a rate limit on the same curve as any failure (`platform.Timeouts.OutboxBackoffBase` 30 seconds doubling to `OutboxBackoffMax` 5 minutes, evaluated by `domain/outbox.EvaluateBackoff`), so a primary limit reached early in its hour dead-letters (`MaxAttempts`, 10) about 33 minutes later, before its reset. Carry the deadline GitHub states, typed, on a 403 or a 429 from every GitHub call path: `x-ratelimit-reset` when remaining is `0`, `retry-after` when present, one minute otherwise; the message text may still classify, never compute a deadline. Record it in the failure class that does not consume an attempt: the claim's increment is taken back and the row rescheduled at the deadline, under its own, longer ceiling in `internal/platform/timeouts.go` measured from a first-deferred time the row records, that dead-letters naming the rate limit. A deadline learned from one response applies to the credential, not only to the row: it is recorded per rate-limit owner (an App installation; for a personal token, the GitHub account behind it, so two tokens of one account share one budget) and the claim skips every row due to use that owner until it passes, so a limit is not rediscovered once per row. Where GitHub states no deadline, the one-minute wait doubles on each consecutive rate-limited deferral of that owner, capped in `internal/platform/timeouts.go`, as GitHub's guidance for secondary limits asks, and resets on a success. Step 203 introduced that class (`domain/outbox.ClassDeferred`, decided by `EvaluateFailure`, recorded by `OutboxStore.Defer`); this Step reuses it, adding its deadline to `domain/outbox.Failure`, its own rules and its first-deferral column. Permission and authentication refusals keep their existing path. Every GitHub caller that retries on its own schedule gets the same deadline, enumerated in the Step (the auto-merge worker and the image freshness pump are two). Exit: a limit hit by one row holds every other row for the same credential until its deadline; a 403 with `x-ratelimit-remaining: 0` and a reset 45 minutes away leaves its row pending with its attempt count unchanged and delivers after the reset; a 429 with `retry-after` is not retried before it; a genuine permission 403 still dead-letters on today's schedule | §44.2, §5.1 |
| 192 | the App registry, its roles and states (dark) | Apps with their installations, the repositories each reaches and the permissions GitHub reports; one role (`read`, `publish`, `work`) and one state (`active`, `retiring`, `retired`) each; its bot login read from GitHub's own App metadata at registration; a SET of private keys per App, as GitHub lets an App hold several at once for rotation, and the webhook secret, encrypted at rest, never readable back; admin-only, audited writes. Eligibility derived from GitHub's own installation data (installation events from every registered App, and a periodic re-read), never declared. An App whose permissions exceed its role is refused at registration. Seeded from configuration ONCE: `NARVI_GITHUB_APP_ID`'s App becomes the `read` App and the configured key its first key. Afterwards the registry is authoritative, and configuration can only add: a configured key already in the App's set is ignored (so a key rotated through the operator surface never reverts on restart, and an environment still holding the old key boots), a configured key not yet in the set and not tombstoned is added to it, audited, which is the rotation path before Step 197 exists (a key removed from the set leaves a tombstone by fingerprint, and a configured key matching one is refused with a boot warning, never re-added), and only a configured App id that differs from the registry's `read` App refuses boot, naming both. Every boot re-reads the permissions of the App currently serving §30.4's read-only mint and refuses to start if it can write, or if they cannot be read, as today's boot check does; any other registered App granted more than its role since, or whose permissions cannot be read, is marked ineligible with the reason instead of refusing boot, and a retired App is not read at all. The `read` role names what §30.4's read-only mint serves: shadow sessions, build boots and review sessions (§30.4). The newest active key signs, the others are its fallback. Moving the `read` role to another App is an operator action (Step 197), never a configuration change. Nothing consumes the registry yet. Exit: a deployment configured exactly as today boots with a one-row registry and unchanged behavior; a restart after a key rotation keeps the rotated key, with the old key still in the environment; a new key in the environment is added and audited; a configuration naming another App id refuses boot and says why; an App whose installation lacks the repository, or the role's permission, is never reported eligible; the read-mint App granted a write permission refuses the next boot, another over-permitted App is marked ineligible | §44.4, §13.3 |
| 193 | the publish role: one App writes, with minted, refreshed tokens | The first consumer, and the one that makes a shipped Step work: formal reviews, labels, comments and check runs (Step 174) are written by the deployment's `publish` App, ONE at a time, with installation tokens instead of the static bot token. The `publish` role holds only what publication needs: the bot token's other writes (auto-merge's merge, sentinel fix pull requests, description autofix, the commit statuses of Step 153's links) stay on it, named in §44.4, until a decision moves them. Tokens are minted per (App, installation, repositories, permissions requested), narrowed with GitHub's own `repositories` and `permissions` parameters, cached until shortly before `expires_at`, their granted permissions checked before use; a request for less never receives a cached token for more. The ingress recognizes the publish App's bot login (registered from GitHub's App metadata by Step 192, not learned from a first write) as its own before that App writes anything, so a comment it posts is never read as a mention from an unlinked account. Until Step 196 designates a receiver, the publish App's own repository-event deliveries are verified and not processed: the existing webhook stays the only processed source, so no event is handled twice. A check stays with the App that created it (GitHub lets only that App update it): each check row records its creating App, replacing today's one deployment-wide learned writer id, and while that App is active a head keeps it for every later `narvi/review` check on that head, so a restart on the same head never opens a second check under another App; a new head goes to the current publish App. **Retirement ends in one step.** A retiring publisher re-completes, as not assessed with the reason (the publisher changed; a re-review refreshes it), every check it created that is still open or is the current check of an open pull request's head, completed ones included, since a `success` left on a head would stay green on code no active publisher could mark stale; the next review of such a head then opens its check under the current publisher, one active identity at a time, and the old App retires once no binding references it (Step 196). With no `publish` App registered, today's bot token stays in use and boot says so. Exit: a `narvi/review` check is created and then updated more than an hour after boot; after a publish-App change and a restart, an old run is updated by its creator and no second `narvi/review` appears on the same head while that creator is active; retiring the old publisher leaves every one of its current checks on an open pull request re-completed as not assessed, and the next review of such a head publishes under the new App; a comment posted by the publish App triggers nothing; a read request never receives a cached write token | §44.5, §44.1, §21.1b |
| 194 ∥ | budgets, observed per credential | Limit, remaining, used and reset per credential (the bot token, the image-build token `NARVI_GITHUB_IMAGE_BUILD_TOKEN` the freshness pump spends, and each App installation) and per GitHub resource, from the headers of responses to requests actually made, with the time observed and the provenance (response or probe). Two personal tokens of one GitHub account share that account's primary budget and are observed as one owner (Step 191). "Never observed" is a state of its own, never a full budget and never an empty one, and a reading whose reset time has passed is stale and counts as never observed, since GitHub has refilled it: shedding never triggers on either, so a credential used only by background work is observed again rather than shed for good. Selection (Step 195) prefers a known budget with room, then an unknown one, and never refuses on unknown alone. A probe is recorded beside the response-sourced reading, never merged into it. First consumer: below a floor, background GitHub reads on these credentials (the image freshness pump, the auto-merge worker's polling, the release-review worker) defer with a recorded reason while human-initiated work and publications proceed. The decision inbox is not among them: it reads with the viewing user's own GitHub credential, on request. Exit: before any call a credential's budget reads unknown and nothing is shed; after one, remaining equals the response header; under the floor the freshness pump records a shed with its reason while a human-triggered review still dispatches; once the reading's reset has passed, the pump runs again | §44.3 |
| 195 | a turn's work App, bound durably | When a turn needs a `work` identity (a push or a pull request for a session no human GitHub credential covers), one eligible App is bound to it through a claim taken atomically (§5.1's idiom) and persisted on the turn: two dispatchers never bind two Apps, and a restart finds the binding. Only an unconfirmed claim (a dispatch that crashed before confirming) expires; a confirmed binding lasts as long as the turn and every write it still has pending, so an outbox retry hours later keeps the same author. Publication stays with the `publish` App (Step 193), so a turn's writes never change author between retries and a check never changes owner. Selection reads Step 194's budgets and never binds a retiring App. Which sessions may use the `work` role at all -- in particular whether a creator with no linked GitHub identity may push through a bounded credential -- is an open decision (docs/DECISIONS.md); until it is taken, the binding is built and exercised by tests only. Whatever the answer, the sandbox never holds a `work` installation token: GitHub cannot limit one to a branch, so it would let the agent write any branch, tag or release of the repository; the decision says how the push is bounded instead (for example, made by the control plane for the session's own branch only). Exit: two concurrent dispatches bind one App; a retry after a restart uses the same App; a write retried after the claim's original expiry still uses it | §44.6, §5.1 |
| 196 ∥ | webhooks from several Apps, and retirement | A receiver for repository events: GitHub sends one delivery per installed App, each under its own delivery id, which the per-delivery claim would process once per App. For each repository, the designated receiver App processes its events where it is installed and subscribed to them with the permissions they need (GitHub reports each App's subscribed events), and where it is not, one other App meeting the same conditions, chosen deterministically (the lowest registry id among them), does, so no repository is left unprocessed; every other App's repository-event deliveries are verified with their own secret, kept for a bounded window, and not processed. The existing webhook (its single secret) is the receiver until an App receiver is designated, never processed beside it. **A change of receiver loses and doubles nothing**, planned or not (a fallback changing because installation data moved, or the receiver uninstalled from a repository, after which GitHub delivers nothing more to it): the new receiver first replays the kept deliveries of the window that no receiver processed, then processes, and during the window both deduplicate by a key derived from the payload that separates genuinely different events (event, action, the object's id, the label name for a label event, the before and after shas for a push or synchronize, the base for an edit, `merged` for a close, and the object's update time; never the delivery id or the installation). Keys are recorded for every processed repository event, not only during a change, so a copy processed before the window began is recognized. Stated residual: two identical actions on one object within the update time's resolution (a label removed and re-applied in the same second) can collide, and only inside a change's window. Events about an App itself (`installation`, `installation_repositories`) are processed from every registered App, since only that App receives them and Step 192's eligibility reads them. Every registered App's bot login is recognized as this system's own, in ADDITION to the configured handle, which stays recognized while the static bot token is in use. Retirement drains: a retiring App takes no new binding, bound work finishes, a retiring publisher re-completes its current checks as Step 193 states, and the App is retired once nothing references it; a receiver cannot be retired before another is designated and has replayed its window. Exit: a repository event delivered by three installed Apps is processed once; a repository the receiver is not installed on is still processed, once; an event arriving during a receiver change, or while the receiver's uninstallation is being processed, is processed exactly once; a label removed and re-applied outside a change is processed twice; an installation event from a non-receiver App updates its eligibility; a comment by any registered App, or by the bot token's account, triggers nothing; retiring an App with bound work finishes that work and binds nothing new | §44.7 |
| 197 | the operator surface and runbook | Admin-only Settings view with one row per App: role, state, installations and eligible repositories, budget with its provenance and age ("never observed" shown as such), live bindings, open checks it owns, recent refusals. Register, rotate a key, retire and designate the receiver are audited actions. A runbook in `docs/runbooks/` covers registering a second App, rotating a private key, changing the publish App (including a branch protection or ruleset that names the old App as the expected source of `narvi/review`, which Step 202's reader can detect), retiring an App and moving the receiver, and states that adding an App for rate-limit headroom is the operator's choice, quoting Section H of GitHub's Terms of Service. Exit: an operator retires an App from the view and watches its bindings and open checks drain to zero; a never-observed budget renders as unknown, never as full | §44.8, §13.3 |

**Phase 20 milestone**: a verdict leaves one formal review on the commit it reviewed, however often
its delivery is retried; a `narvi/review` check is still being updated a day after boot, under the
`publish` App; a primary rate limit delays a notification until its reset and loses none; with two
Apps installed on one repository, an event is processed once and neither App's comment triggers
anything; and an App retired from the operator view drains to zero bindings and open checks with no
write failing in between.

## Phase 21: Adopted on 2026-09-28 (10 Steps, additive)

Adopted by the owner on 2026-09-28, each item checked against this codebase first, together with the
owner's decisions of the same day on the questions it raised. As in Phase 19, the phase is grouped by
provenance; within it, four groups
by subject, whose only dependencies are the ones the execution order below names; the defects found
in shipped Steps along the way are Phase 11 rows (198-203), and no Step here waits on one.

**Review quality, measured (206-208, §21.5).** A ruling per finding, and the measures recorded where
they happen with their populations. Most of the mechanisms this needs already exist here;
these do not. Where a Phase 11 row adds a fact (a finding's source, an unreadable input), the
readouts show it once it is recorded and "not measured" before.

**A second agent runtime and provider accounts (209-211, §45).**
Step 209 starts with a reading of the provider's terms on subscription credentials, before any code;
Step 210's first exit criterion is a real turn on the real SDK in CI.

**Review scope and publication (212-214).** Decisions on the questions this work raised or reopened:
a narrowed review request never stands for the whole pull request (212, D-13); a verdict's findings
go inline on the diff (213, D-09); a stack layer whose ancestors alone moved gets a delta review in shadow
beside its full review, experimentally (214, D-12).

**Unresolved conversations (215).** An eligibility gate decided by the owner.

Execution order: **{206, 207} → 208**; **209 and 210 in either order → 211**; **190 → 213**;
**142, 207 and 212 → 214** (a delta review is paired through 207's records, starts from the last whole
verdict, and stays out of the readers and effects 212 sets; 208's readout shows the pairing); **212** and **215** any time. Whichever of 207 and 212 lands first adds the
verdict-kind column the other uses.

| Step | Title | Content | Ref. |
|---|---|---|---|
| 206 | a human's ruling on one finding | A maintainer rules on an individual finding with a vocabulary of its own -- `false_positive`, `accepted_risk`, `policy_disagreement`, `fixed_later`, `duplicate`, `unverified` -- recorded beside, never instead of, the finding's status (open, rebutted, the fix states; `internal/domain/reviewpost/finding.go`), which says what happened to it and not whether it was right. Each value's role in a precision rate is a table in `internal/domain`, exhaustively tested: only `false_positive` counts against the reviewer; `accepted_risk`, `policy_disagreement` and `fixed_later` (the finding was right, and was fixed after it was raised) count for it; `duplicate` and `unverified` leave both numerator and denominator. A precision rate is computed over ruled findings only; an unruled finding is reported as unruled, never counted as right. Distinct from rebuttals (Step 48), from learned false-positive patterns (Step 63) and from acceptance of a whole verdict (Step 188): a ruling on one finding never accepts a verdict, and only `false_positive` may feed the learned patterns. Its own route and command, maintainer and above through `Authorize`, audited; it never changes the finding's status, a later status change never erases it, and it follows the same finding into a later verdict by finding identity. Exit: an `accepted_risk` ruling leaves the reviewer's precision unchanged where a `false_positive` ruling lowers it; an unruled finding appears in no rate; a ruling survives a re-review that raises the same finding | §21.5, §22 |
| 207 | what the review measures need, recorded where it happens | Facts §21.5's readouts need that nothing records today. The number of findings each fact-check run SAW (the primary run, and Step 201's second run when it exists, each recorded apart): `review_verdicts` keeps whether the pass ran (`fact_check`) and how many it killed, not how many it checked, so a kill rate has no denominator; a run that did not happen records no count at all. These counts are the reviewer's own report; they are recorded as self-reported, and checked against the sub-task events Step 71 corroborates where it applies. Each finding's presence in each verdict, by finding identity, as history: two verdicts compared through a mutable findings table compare the current state with itself. And each verdict's kind (whole, narrowed, delta: §21.1b's single rule, recorded once by the verdict endpoint from the turn the verdict is attributed to), in a column added by whichever of this Step and Step 212 lands first, so comparisons between verdicts use whole verdicts. Exit: a verdict records how many findings each fact-check run saw, and none for a run that did not happen; two adjacent whole verdicts of one pull request compare finding by finding from what each recorded at the time | §21.5, §26.5, §26.6 |
| 208 | review quality, readable | Readouts over those records, on the Analytics review-risk section (Step 86) and its API: the light and deep split with its reasons, rated against routed verdicts and not all verdicts, rendering no rate rather than 0 % when nothing in the range was routed, and keeping reasons that mean the rules worked apart from an input that could not be read (Step 199's cause, once recorded); fact-check kills against the findings each run saw, a run that happened and killed nothing shown as 0 % and never as missing; findings repeated, fresh and dropped between ADJACENT whole verdicts of one pull request, findings without an identity skipped rather than counted fresh; defect against coverage findings; precision under Step 206's rulings, and per finding source once Step 201 records it; and, once Step 214 runs, each delta verdict paired with the full review of the same context, per instruction version. Every readout states its population and keeps "not measured" apart from zero. Exit: each readout renders its denominator; a range with no routed verdict renders no rate; a run that killed nothing renders 0 %; a fact not yet recorded renders "not measured" | §21.5, §26.5, §12.2 |
| 209 | provider accounts under this deployment's roles | Model-provider accounts as product objects: a user connects a subscription (Anthropic, OpenAI) through the provider's own sign-in or a pasted setup token, or an API key, stored encrypted like §29's ChatGPT link. **An account a user connects, subscription or API key, is used only where it was explicitly selected, never by automatic resolution.** Today a credential at the user scope heads `scopePriority` (`internal/domain/providercredential/scope.go`) and is resolved from `sessions.created_by`, so a linked personal credential already beats every deployment key for all of its owner's sessions (row 205 takes review sessions out of that). New accounts are stored where the resolution never reads them (not the `user` scope `ListForResolution` returns): connecting one changes nothing until its owner selects it for a session they create and run themselves; a review session, an automation's session, a session another person created and any child session (a train's link, a fix child) never use it, since a selection never passes to a child, and their turns keep the deployment's credentials. Within a session its owner selected it for, participants' prompts run on it, the multiplayer attribution §29.10 already accepts for a creator's seat. §29's existing ChatGPT link keeps its current resolution for its owner's own sessions. §29.4's "no creating endpoint" for user-held keys is amended by this Step. Management follows §13.3's matrix (a user their own accounts, an admin the deployment's), and a default is an explicit, audited choice. Both easy shapes are refused: any signed-in user administering accounts, and the first connected account becoming the default for unpinned sessions. **First exit criterion, a reading and not code, before any code**: the provider's terms on subscription credentials driving automated and multi-user sessions, recorded with its date in docs/DECISIONS.md; where they forbid it, this Step ships API-key accounts only, under the same selection rule. Exit: a member connects an account and nothing about their existing or future sessions changes until they select it; a review session they opened and another member re-triggers never uses it; an automation and a child session cannot use it | §45, §29, §13.3 |
| 210 | a second agent runtime, at parity (3 PRs) | An `AgentRuntime` adapter (§4.2) for a provider's agent SDK beside OpenCode, behind the same port and the same anti-corruption contract (§7), in three PRs: (a) events translated into the same `AgentEvent` vocabulary, a plain turn, cost per step (§25.15), compaction, resume by conversation id, and plan mode with at least OpenCode's guard (its `plan` agent denies the edit tool; a guard on edits, not a sandbox, since bash is not denied: `internal/adapters/outbound/opencode/types.go`), which a train's mother session (§39.1) relies on; (b) sub-task fan-out (§7.1) and the review sub-agents (§26.4, §26.6); (c) the tool and permission restrictions this design depends on (the verdict tool and its raw-comment block, §8.2; the sentinel fix's edit restriction, §17.2). What the adapter supports is declared as an ALLOW list in its capabilities, like the sandbox provider's (`internal/app/ports/capabilities.go`), naming plan mode among the rest, and a turn needing anything not declared is refused at dispatch, never silently degraded, so a runtime can never ship without the review and stack tools. **First exit criterion, empirical, on Step 57's precedent (made a CI job by Step 163)**: a real turn on the pinned SDK in CI, which no mock stands in for. Independent of Step 209 (API-key credentials suffice). Exit: the contract tests §7 runs against OpenCode pass against this adapter; a plan-mode turn on it cannot use its edit tool; a review turn on it produces a verdict through the same tool | §45, §4.2, §7 |
| 211 | choosing the runtime | `runtime` on a session and on an automation, defaulting to today's, validated at creation against what the chosen runtime declares (a session needing a capability the runtime does not declare is refused naming it), recorded on the turn like `model_id`, visible in the timeline and in the analytics; a choice never changes a running session. After Step 210, and after Step 209 for a subscription account. Exit: an automation pinned to the second runtime runs there; a session needing an undeclared capability is refused with the capability named; a turn's runtime is readable after the fact | §45 |
| 212 | a narrowed review request never stands for the whole pull request | A mention can narrow what it asks to review ("review only the transfer method"). Decided (2026-09-28, D-13): the verdict such a request produces **never** becomes the one this system reads for the pull request. **One rule decides a verdict's kind**, recorded once by the verdict endpoint and read by every reader, never re-derived: a verdict takes the kind of the turn it is attributed to (a reminder's verdict is the reminded turn's, Step 141). A turn records its review mode when it is inserted (`whole`, `narrowed` or `delta`), kept consistent with `turns.is_review_attempt` (migration 000133; true exactly for `whole`), with a constraint that a delta turn is never a review attempt. Verdicts recorded before the column exists read as whole, which is today's behavior: an attempt turn older than 000133 reads `is_review_attempt` false for want of a backfill and must not be reclassified narrowed. The column and the turn mode are added by this Step or Step 207, whichever lands first. What today does not follow the line: the verdict endpoint emits an assessed check for whatever turn posts a verdict (`internal/adapters/inbound/httpapi/reviewverdict.go`); the check publisher's supersession and the latest-verdict queries (`GetLatestReviewVerdict`, `GetLatestNonShadow`, and `ListLatestAutoApprovedInRepo`, which auto-merge discovery keeps in agreement with them) order by attempt or turn time without it; MCP's result takes its attempt state from review attempts but its verdict from the unfiltered latest query, and acceptance applicability compares against the same unfiltered id. **The endpoint's effects follow the kind, from one table tested exhaustively.** A whole verdict does everything it does today. A narrowed verdict records its row and its findings (upserted into `review_findings` as today, so the open-findings count eligibility reads keeps weighing them) and posts a formal review that is always a `COMMENT` (enforced on today's notifier, and carried in Step 190's payload once it exists); it emits no check, syncs no risk label, runs neither the description autofix nor the sentinel autofix, never enters a latest-verdict read (which repairs MCP's result and acceptance applicability with it), never lifts a block (Step 190), and never counts for the automatic lane's "already reviewed" comparison (§24.3) or a train's gate once Phase 14 builds it. A delta verdict's effects are Step 214's. **Which mentions are whole is decided deterministically, never by a model.** A follow-up mention is a review attempt only when its text after the handle, ignoring case, surrounding whitespace, trailing punctuation and quoted reply lines, is `review`, the re-run command a verdict itself recommends (`internal/domain/reviewpost/rerunguidance.go`); anything else is narrowed, and its answer says it was answered as a question and renders the re-run guidance, so "@handle review please" is never a silent non-event. The mention that OPENS a review session (and resolves a head, as today) is always a whole review, whatever its text: the review instruction is the standard whole review, and the requester's text is carried as an untrusted question to answer beside it (§5.2, as the pull request body is), which cannot narrow the scope; the coverage floor (§26.1) still classes a review that covered less than the change. Exit: an opening "@handle review only X" produces a whole review that also answers the question; with a whole review published, a follow-up "@handle review only X" is answered and the check, eligibility, labels, acceptance and MCP's result still follow the whole review, while its findings stay open for eligibility; a follow-up "@handle Review." is a review attempt; a narrowed verdict never makes a pull request eligible, never posts `REQUEST_CHANGES` and never opens a fix pull request; a reminder's verdict is whole when the reminded turn was; a verdict recorded before the column exists still reads as whole | §21.1b, §5.2 |
| 213 | a verdict's findings, inline on the diff | Decided (2026-09-28, D-09). The formal review a verdict posts (Step 190) carries its anchorable findings as inline comments in the SAME create-review call, anchored on the commit the verdict reviewed, at the server-resolved lines (`StartLine`/`EndLine`, §22.1.1, never the model's), each validated against that commit's diff hunks before the call; a finding that cannot be anchored stays readable in the verdict body. One write and one receipt: an inline review and a verdict comment posted as two writes would accept the partial state between them; here the verdict is the review's body, so there is no second write to lose. A narrowed verdict's review (always a `COMMENT`) carries its inline comments the same way. Reading every page of the thread listing, the reads and the create fit within `OutboxDeliveryTimeout`. GitHub's refusal of a comment is told apart from its refusal of the commit (Step 190's commit-gone case): a refusal naming the commit is that case, and any other refusal, recognised or not, posts the review without the comments and records why, so one bad anchor never drops a blocking review. A finding already raised in an open thread this system started, on the same anchor, unresolved and with no human reply, is cited rather than posted again, decided from GitHub's live thread state (GraphQL `reviewThreads`, matched on this deployment's own logins) and never from a mirror of past webhooks; any doubt posts. The review sandbox never posts a comment itself. Exit: a verdict with three anchorable findings leaves one formal review carrying three inline comments on the reviewed commit; an unanchorable finding appears in the body only; a re-review raising a finding still open in its own unanswered thread cites it instead of posting it twice | §21.1b, §22, §8.2 |
| 214 | a delta re-review of a stack layer (experimental, shadow) | Decided (2026-09-28, D-12, experimental). Once Step 142 routes stack membership events, an ancestor's move invalidates a layer's verdict and §24.8 launches a replacement review, the review of record, which publishes as today. When the layer's own patch is unchanged, a delta review runs IN SHADOW beside it, in a session and sandbox of its own, never in the review session: a turn of the review session would resume that session's conversation (the conversation id every dispatch carries), so each review would read the other's reasoning and the pair would measure nothing, and it would queue behind or ahead of the review of record, which §24.9's hold would make every other review wait on. The shadow session is not a review session: it holds nothing, is held by nothing, and its outcome never changes the review session's status; its turn's mode is `delta` and its verdict's kind is delta (§21.1b). A delta verdict's effects, from Step 212's table, are only its row and its per-verdict finding history (Step 207): no finding upserted into `review_findings` (which the next whole review is told not to re-report), no formal review, no inline comment, no check, no label, no autofix of either kind, and it is left out of the digest (§21.3) and of every readout but the delta pairing. The delta turn has the same verdict tool and passes; its prompt carries the last published WHOLE verdict (the one before the review of record, never the concurrent one) and the ancestors' diffs, marked untrusted, and asks first whether that change can reach the layer (calls, contracts, signatures, units, types, configuration, shared state), reviewing in full when it can or when unsure, and never copying the previous verdict without that assessment. Eligibility read from the last whole verdict's attempt, never the last launch: same head, base, policy and chain of open ancestors with at least one moved; an identical own patch by fingerprint (per file: path, status and ordered changed lines); readable ancestor diffs within a byte bound, touching none of the layer's files. Its cost is its own: it spends no slot of §24.6's budget, and a per-repository cap on delta reviews per period bounds the experiment. The mode and its instruction version ride the verdict's provenance and are never exempted from the policy version. Per repository, off by default. **The measurement is the pairing itself**: each delta verdict is compared, finding by finding (Step 207's records), with the review of record of the same context, and Step 208's readout reports what the delta missed and added per instruction version. Letting a delta verdict stand for the pull request in place of the full review is a decision of its own, taken on that readout and recorded in docs/DECISIONS.md; this Step never does it. A carry-forward with no turn at all is rejected: it would miss a contract changed in a file the layer does not touch. Exit: an eligible ancestor move on a repository that opted in runs the review of record, which publishes as today, and a delta review in a shadow session whose verdict is recorded as delta and has no other effect; neither waits on the other; the readout pairs the two per instruction version; an ineligible move runs only the review of record with the reason logged; the per-period cap stops delta reviews and nothing else; with the setting off nothing changes | §24.8, §21.1b, §21.5 |
| 215 | an unresolved review conversation blocks auto-merge | Decided (2026-09-28). Eligibility (§21.2) also requires that no review conversation on the pull request is unresolved, read live from GitHub's thread state (GraphQL `reviewThreads`, the only source of `isResolved`, every page read; a failed or incomplete read makes the pull request ineligible, never eligible), through a per-repository setting on by default, so repositories that do not turn on GitHub's own conversation-resolution rule are covered too. A thread this system started for one of its own findings, in which no person has replied, does not count while that finding is weighed elsewhere, that is, while it is in the whole verdict eligibility reads or open in the findings count eligibility reads (a narrowed verdict's findings are, Step 212); counting it would keep every pull request with an inline finding (Step 213) ineligible until a person resolves the system's own comments. Any other thread this system started counts. A thread any person started or answered counts. Its own PR, apart from Step 202's required checks. Exit: a person's unresolved comment makes the pull request ineligible and names it; resolving it restores eligibility; an unanswered thread of this system's own does not block; a thread read that fails keeps the pull request ineligible; with the setting off nothing changes | §21.2, §8.2 |

**Phase 21 milestone**: a maintainer's ruling moves the precision rate the way its role says, and
every review-quality readout renders its population with "not measured" apart from zero; a session
runs a review turn on the second runtime and produces a verdict through the same tool, with the
provider-terms reading recorded before any subscription account was accepted; a narrowed request is
answered while the whole review keeps deciding eligibility; a verdict's findings appear inline in its
one review; a delta re-review, where a repository opted in, runs in shadow beside the full review and
is paired with it in the readout; and an unresolved conversation a person started blocks auto-merge.
